 
 [2605.08485] Sinkhorn Treatment Effects: A Causal Optimal Transport Measure 
 Sinkhorn Treatment Effects: A Causal Optimal Transport Measure 
 Medha Agarwal
 Affiliation:  Department of Statistics, University of Washington, Seattle, WA, USA
 Correspondence to: medhaaga@uw.edu 
 Alex Luedtke
 Affiliation:  Department of Health Care Policy, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, USA
 Abstract 
 We introduce the Sinkhorn treatment effect, an entropic optimal transport measure of divergence between counterfactual distributions. Unlike classical quantities such as the average treatment effect, this measure captures differences across entire distributions. We analyze this divergence as a statistical functional and show it can be written as a smooth transformation of counterfactual mean embeddings with an appropriate kernel. This characterization allows us to establish first-order pathwise differentiability in general, and second-order pathwise differentiability under the null hypothesis of equal counterfactual distributions. Leveraging this smoothness, we construct debiased estimators and use them to obtain asymptotically valid tests for distributional treatment effects with a fixed entropic regularization parameter. Because the power of the test depends on this unknown parameter, we further propose an aggregated test that combines evidence across a grid of regularization choices. Experiments on simulated and image data demonstrate the practical advantages of our estimator and testing procedure. 
 Keywords:  Machine Learning, ICML
 1 Introduction 
 Estimating causal treatment effects is a central goal of causal inference (Rubin, 1974 , 2005 ; Rosenbaum and Rubin, 1983 ; Chernozhukov et al. , 2017 ) . In the canonical binary treatment setting, the scientific goal is the discrepancy between the counterfactual outcome distributions under treatment and control, denoted by Y 1 ∼ P 1 Y_{1}\sim P_{1} (treatment) and Y 0 ∼ P 0 Y_{0}\sim P_{0} (control). However, P 1 P_{1} and P 0 P_{0} are never observed via independent samples. Instead, we observe independent copies of ( X , A , Y ) ∼ P (X,A,Y)\sim P , where treatment A A and observed outcome Y Y may depend on confounders X X . Under standard causal identifiability conditions (see Sec.   2 ), P 1 P_{1} and P 0 P_{0} can be learned from P P . Most empirical work summarizes treatment effects through the average treatment effect (ATE), 𝔼 P 1 ​ [ Y 1 ] − 𝔼 P 0 ​ [ Y 0 ] \mathbb{E}_{P_{1}}[Y_{1}]-\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}[Y_{0}] (Imbens, 2004 ) .
While convenient for computation and inference, mean-based summaries can mask important effects that occur away from the center of the distribution. Empirical studies have shown that interventions may induce large distributional shifts despite a negligible ATE (Bitler et al. , 2006 ) . 
 These limitations motivate inference on the entire counterfactual outcome distributions, commonly referred to as distributional treatment effects (DTEs) (Abadie, 2002 ) . Classical approaches target specific functionals of P 1 P_{1} and P 0 P_{0} , including quantiles (Firpo, 2007 ) , cumulative distribution functions (Chernozhukov et al. , 2013 ) —which imply results for the 1-Wasserstein distance in univariate outcome settings (Lin et al. , 2023 ; Balakrishnan et al. , 2025 ) —and probability density functions (Robins and Rotnitzky, 2001 ; Kennedy et al. , 2023 ) . More recently, kernel-based methods estimate counterfactual distributions via kernel mean embeddings (KME) into characteristic reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHS) (Muandet et al. , 2017 ) and quantify DTE using maximum mean discrepancy (MMD) (Fawkes et al. , 2024 ; Martinez Taboada et al. , 2023 ; Luedtke and Chung, 2024 ) . Although MMD metrizes weak convergence, it induces a flat geometry on the space of probability measures and may fail to capture meaningful geometric discrepancies between counterfactual distributions, especially when their supports weakly overlap or separate. Related approaches based on f f -divergence further require mutual absolute continuity, limiting applicability (Kennedy et al. , 2023 ) .
Unlike f f -divergences, MMD is finite for disjoint distributions; however, MMD saturates quickly as this disjointness increases, a phenomenon visualized in Fig.   1 . 
 Figure 1 : MMD vs. Sinkhorn divergence across increasing separation θ \theta between counterfactual outcome distribution under control P 0 = 𝒩 ​ ( 𝟎 2 , I 2 ) P_{0}=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}_{2},I_{2}) and under treatment P 1 , θ = 1 2 ​ 𝒩 ​ ( − θ ​ 𝟏 2 , I 2 ) + 1 2 ​ 𝒩 ​ ( θ ​ 𝟏 2 , I 2 ) P_{1,\theta}=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}(-\theta\mathbf{1}_{2},I_{2})+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}(\theta\mathbf{1}_{2},I_{2}) . The average treatment effect is zero for all θ > 0 \theta>0 . Here D θ D_{\theta} denotes either MMD or Sinkhorn divergence between P 0 P_{0} and P 1 , θ P_{1,\theta} . As θ \theta increases, the distributions diverge and MMD saturates, failing to distinguish between ‘far’ and ‘very far’ distributions , whereas the Sinkhorn divergence continues to grow.
 To overcome these limitations, we propose measuring DTE via the Sinkhorn divergence (Feydy et al. , 2019 ; Ramdas et al. , 2017 ) , a centered entropy-regularized optimal transport (EOT) cost, that respects the intrinsic geometry of the outcome space. Under identifiability, we define the Sinkhorn treatment effect (STE) as 
 STE ​ ( P ) = OT ε ​ ( P 1 , P 0 ) − 1 2 ​ OT ε ​ ( P 1 , P 1 ) − 1 2 ​ OT ε ​ ( P 0 , P 0 ) . \text{STE}(P)=\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(P_{1},\,P_{0}\right)-\tfrac{1}{2}\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(P_{1},\,P_{1}\right)-\tfrac{1}{2}\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(P_{0},\,P_{0}\right). 
 (1) 
 For measures μ \mu and ν \nu , the EOT cost with quadratic cost function c ​ ( x , y ) = 1 2 ​ ‖ x − y ‖ 2 c(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}\|x-y\|^{2} and ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 regularization is 
 OT ε ​ ( μ , ν ) = inf π ∈ Π ​ ( μ , ν ) ∫ c ​ ( x , y ) ​ 𝑑 π ​ ( x , y ) + ε ​ KL ​ ( π ∣ μ ⊗ ν ) , \text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\int c(x,y)\,d\pi(x,y)+\varepsilon\mathrm{KL}(\pi\mid\mu\otimes\nu), 
 (2) 
 where Π ​ ( μ , ν ) \Pi(\mu,\nu) denotes the set of couplings of ( μ , ν ) (\mu,\nu) , and KL ( ⋅ ∣ ⋅ ) \mathrm{KL}(\cdot\mid\cdot) is the Kullback–Leibler divergence. Here c c reflects the cost of moving mass between two outcome values, thereby encoding domain-specific notions of discrepancy between outcomes, while ε \varepsilon controls the degree of entropic smoothing. As ε → 0 \varepsilon\to 0 , STE approaches the corresponding unregularized OT cost, and as ε → ∞ \varepsilon\to\infty , it approaches the MMD distance corresponding to the Gibbs kernel e − c / ε e^{-c/\varepsilon} . More broadly, Sinkhorn divergence interpolates between the OT cost and MMD (Feydy et al. , 2019 ) . 
 We show that STE admits a pathwise differentiable representation, enabling construction of a doubly robust, asymptotically normal one-step estimator (Pfanzagl, 1982 ) . While this first-order estimator is degenerate under the null of equal counterfactual outcome distributions, mirroring known degeneracy phenomena for debiased MMD-based tests (Muandet et al. , 2017 ) , we further develop a second-order influence function-based correction, yielding a valid and powerful test with an n n -rate limiting distribution.
With our asymptotic result, we construct a test with provable type I error control, for each fixed regularization parameter ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . Since ε \varepsilon is unknown in practice and an unfavorable choice may result in low power, we further propose STEAgg, a multiple testing procedure that combines evidence across a grid of ε \varepsilon values. This approach is inspired by aggregated kernel tests such as Schrab et al. ( 2023 ) , which combine MMD statistics across bandwidths while maintaining non-asymptotic type I error guaranties. We prove that STEAgg-based tests achieve nominal asymptotic type I error control. 
 Our Contributions. 
 1. 
 We introduce Sinkhorn treatment effects. Unlike prior causal OT approaches, ours allows for multivariate outcomes. 
 2. 
 We characterize the smoothness of the STE functional, showing it is first-order differentiable and second-order differentiable under the null of no DTE. 
 3. 
 We use this differentiability to construct efficient first- and second-order bias-corrected estimators of STE. 
 4. 
 We propose STE- and STEAgg-based hypothesis tests for the causal null, at a fixed ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 and aggregated over a finite grid of ε \varepsilon values, respectively. 
 5. 
 We demonstrate the practical advantages of our approach on simulated and high-dimensional image data. 
 2 Background

 Nonparametric Statistical Model. 
 We consider a model 𝒫 \mathcal{P} of distributions of Z = ( X , A , Y ) Z=(X,A,Y) , where X ∈ 𝒳 ⊂ ℝ p X\in\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{p} are covariates, A ∈ { 0 , 1 } A\in\{0,1\} is a binary treatment indicator, and Y ∈ 𝒴 ⊂ ℝ d Y\in\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} is the observed outcome. Let 𝒵 := 𝒳 × { 0 , 1 } × 𝒴 \mathcal{Z}:=\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}\times\mathcal{Y} be endowed with the product topology. The model 𝒫 ⊂ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒵 ) \mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z}) is unrestricted, save for three conditions: 
 1. 
 𝒳 \mathcal{X} is Polish and 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} is a bounded closed ball. 
 2. 
 The model is dominated: there exists σ \sigma -finite λ \lambda such that p := d ​ P d ​ λ p:=\frac{dP}{d\lambda} is well defined for all P ∈ 𝒫 P\in\mathcal{P} . 
 3. 
 Strong positivity holds: e P ​ ( a | x ) := P ​ ( A = a | X = x ) e_{P}(a\,|\,x):=P(A=a\,|\,X=x) satisfies inf P ∈ 𝒫 ess ​ inf ( a , x ) ⁡ e P ​ ( a | x ) > 0 \inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\operatorname*{ess\,inf}_{(a,x)}e_{P}(a|x)>0 . 
 The first condition ensures 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} is Polish, making regular conditional probability distributions well-defined. The second simplifies the notion of pathwise differentiability discussed in the sequel. The third is common in causal inference settings, and will be used in the causal identifiability result to follow. 
 Causal Setup. 
 Let Y 1 Y_{1} and Y 0 Y_{0} denote the potential outcomes under treatment and control, respectively. Our objective is to assess the Sinkhorn divergence between the marginal distributions of Y 1 Y_{1} and Y 0 Y_{0} . Consistent with other DTE works, we call each such distribution a counterfactual distribution (Martinez Taboada et al. , 2023 ; Kennedy et al. , 2023 ) , with the understanding that it would be called an interventional distribution in the structural causal model framework (Bareinboim et al. , 2022 ) . 
 Under standard causal conditions (Hernán and Robins, 2024 ) , the distribution of Y a Y_{a} , a ∈ { 0 , 1 } a\in\{0,1\} , is identifiable through an observed data distribution P ∈ 𝒫 P\in\mathcal{P} . Concretely, this identifiability result involves P a ∈ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) P_{a}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) defined so that, for all measurable 𝒴 ′ ⊂ 𝒴 \mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{Y} : 
 P a ​ ( 𝒴 ′ ) \displaystyle P_{a}(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}) 
 = ∫ P ​ { Y ∈ 𝒴 ′ | A = a , X = x } ​ d ​ P X ​ ( x ) , \displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\limits\kern-0.99998pt}{\textstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits\kern-0.99998pt}{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits\kern-0.81943pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits\kern-0.7361pt}P\{Y\in\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\,|\,A=a,X=x\}\,dP_{X}(x), 
 (3) 
 where P X P_{X} is the marginal of X X under P P .
Formally, ( 3 ) holds if Z Z has an associated potential outcome Y a Y_{a} and the following hold: no unmeasured confounders ( Y a ⟂ ⟂ A | X Y_{a}\mathrel{\perp\!\!\!\perp}A\,|\,X ), consistency ( A = a A=a implies Y = Y a Y=Y_{a} ), and positivity ( e P ​ ( a | X ) > 0 e_{P}(a\,|\,X)>0 P X P_{X} -a.s.). 
 Distributional Kernel Mean Embeddings. 
 For a fixed choice of s > d / 2 s>d/2 , we work with the Sobolev space W s ​ ( 𝒴 ) W^{s}(\mathcal{Y}) , which is an RKHS due to the lower bound on s s (Adams and Fournier, 2003 , Thm. 4.12) .
Let k : 𝒴 × 𝒴 → ℝ k:\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R} , k y := k ​ ( y , ⋅ ) k_{y}:=k(y,\cdot) , be the kernel associated with W s ​ ( 𝒴 ) W^{s}(\mathcal{Y}) , and let ( ℋ , ⟨ ⋅ , ⋅ ⟩ ℋ ) \left(\mathcal{H},\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right) denote the corresponding RKHS with norm ∥ ⋅ ∥ ℋ \left\|\cdot\right\|_{\mathcal{H}} and unit ball ℋ 1 ⊂ ℋ \mathcal{H}_{1}\subset\mathcal{H} . By Sobolev embedding and compactness of 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} , the kernel k k is bounded, i.e., M k := sup y , y ′ ∈ 𝒴 | k ​ ( y , y ′ ) | < ∞ M_{k}:=\sup_{y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}|k(y,y^{\prime})|<\infty . Consequently, for every P ∈ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) , the map h ↦ P ​ h h\mapsto Ph defines a bounded linear functional on ℋ \mathcal{H} , and admits a kernel mean embedding m ​ ( P ) ∈ ℋ m(P)\in\mathcal{H} given by the Bochner integral m ​ ( P ) = ∫ k y ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( 𝐲 ) m(P)=\int k_{y}\,dP(\mathbf{y}) 
 (Smola et al. , 2007 ; Sriperumbudur et al. , 2010 ) . Identifying ℋ \mathcal{H} isometrically with a subspace of ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) via the map J ​ ( h ) : f ∈ ℋ 1 ↦ ⟨ h , f ⟩ ℋ J(h):f\in\mathcal{H}_{1}\mapsto\langle h,f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}} , the composition J ∘ m J\circ m embeds 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) into ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) . Since W s ​ ( 𝒴 ) W^{s}(\mathcal{Y}) contains all polynomial functions restricted to 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} , W s ​ ( 𝒴 ) W^{s}(\mathcal{Y}) is dense in 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) by the Stone-Weierstrass theorem, implying that k k is a c c -universal kernel (Steinwart, 2001 , Thm. 9) and characteristic (Micchelli et al. , 2006 ) . Hence, the KME map m m is injective and induces the maximum mean discrepancy MMD ​ ( μ , ν ) := ‖ m ​ ( μ ) − m ​ ( ν ) ‖ ℋ = ‖ μ − ν ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \text{MMD}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right):=\left\|m(\mu)-m(\nu)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\left\|\mu-\nu\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})} , which metrizes 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) . 
 Following Muandet et al. ( 2017 ) , we represent counterfactual distributions via their KMEs.

 Let Ψ ​ ( P ) := ( ψ 1 ​ ( P ) , ψ 0 ​ ( P ) ) \Psi(P):=\left(\psi^{1}(P),\psi^{0}(P)\right) .
 Muandet et al. ( 2021 ) define the kernel treatment effect (KTE) as the MMD between ψ 1 ​ ( P ) \psi^{1}(P) and ψ 0 ​ ( P ) \psi^{0}(P) . A natural estimator of the KTE is obtained by estimating ψ a ​ ( P ) \psi^{a}(P) via inverse propensity weighting (IPW) (Imbens, 2004 ) . While the resulting estimator is unbiased when the propensity score is known (Muandet et al. , 2017 , Thm. 5) , valid root- n n inference in double machine learning settings typically requires debiasing based on the efficient influence function (Luedtke and Chung, 2024 ) . 
 Sinkhorn Divergence. 
 The EOT problem defined in ( 2 ) between measures μ \mu and ν \nu admits a unique optimal coupling π ( μ , ν ) \pi^{(\mu,\nu)} . The dual EOT problem admits optimal potentials ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) , φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) ∈ L 1 ​ ( μ ) × L 1 ​ ( ν ) (\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)},\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)})\in L^{1}(\mu)\times L^{1}(\nu) called entropic potentials. The entropic potentials are unique up to an additive constant; see Sec.   B.2 for details. The primal and dual solutions satisfy 
 d ​ π ( μ , ν ) d ​ ( μ ⊗ ν ) = exp ⁡ ( 1 ε ​ ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) ⊕ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) − c ) ) , \frac{d\pi^{(\mu,\nu)}}{d(\mu\otimes\nu)}=\exp\left(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\big(\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}\oplus\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}-c\big)\right), 
 (5) 
 where ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) ⊕ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) ​ ( y 1 , y 2 ) = φ 1 ( μ , ν ) ​ ( y 1 ) + φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ​ ( y 2 ) (\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}\oplus\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)})(y_{1},y_{2})=\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}(y_{1})+\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}(y_{2}) , and c ​ ( y 1 , y 2 ) = ‖ y 1 − y 2 ‖ 2 / 2 c(y_{1},y_{2})=\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|^{2}/2 . Since OT ε ​ ( μ , μ ) ≠ 0 \text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\mu\right)\neq 0 , the EOT cost is not a divergence. The Sinkhorn divergence (Ramdas et al. , 2017 ; Feydy et al. , 2019 ) corrects this through the centering 
 S ε ​ ( μ , ν ) = OT ε ​ ( μ , ν ) − 1 2 ​ [ OT ε ​ ( μ , μ ) + OT ε ​ ( ν , ν ) ] . {S}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)=\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)-\tfrac{1}{2}[\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\mu\right)+\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\nu,\,\nu\right)]. 
 (6) 
 It admits dual representation S ε ​ ( μ , ν ) = μ ​ υ 1 ( μ , ν ) + ν ​ υ 0 ( μ , ν ) {S}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)=\mu\upsilon_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}+\nu\upsilon_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)} where ( υ 1 ( μ , ν ) , υ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) (\upsilon_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)},\upsilon_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}) are the centered entropic potentials 
 υ 1 ( μ , ν ) := φ 1 ( μ , ν ) − φ 1 ( μ , μ ) , υ 0 ( μ , ν ) = φ 0 ( μ , ν ) − φ 0 ( ν , ν ) . \upsilon_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}:=\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}-\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\mu)},\quad\upsilon_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}=\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}-\varphi_{0}^{(\nu,\nu)}. 
 Higher-order Efficient Influence Function. 
 Let 𝒫 \mathcal{P} be a nonparametric statistical model and Φ : 𝒫 → ℝ \Phi:\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{R} a parameter that is pathwise differentiable at P ∈ 𝒫 P\in\mathcal{P} (van der Vaart, 1991 ) . The efficient influence function (EIF) at P P , denoted by Φ ˙ P ∈ L 0 2 ​ ( P ) \dot{\Phi}_{P}\in L_{0}^{2}(P) , characterizes the semiparametric efficiency bound for estimating Φ ​ ( P ) \Phi(P) (Bickel et al. , 1993 ) .
For any regular parametric submodel ( P t , t ∈ [ 0 , δ ) ) ⊂ 𝒫 (P_{t},t\in[0,\delta))\subset\mathcal{P} with P 0 = P P_{0}=P and (Fisher) score function s s —which take the form s = d d ​ t ​ log ⁡ p t | t = 0 s=\tfrac{d}{dt}\log p_{t}|_{t=0} under regularity conditions—pathwise differentiability implies d d ​ t ​ Φ ​ ( P t ) | t = 0 = ⟨ Φ ˙ P , s ⟩ L 2 ​ ( P ) \tfrac{d}{dt}\Phi(P_{t})|_{t=0}=\langle\dot{\Phi}_{P},s\rangle_{L^{2}(P)} . This representation directly generates the usual notion of a gradient from calculus, with the EIF playing the role of a gradient and the score playing the role of a direction along which an input to a function is perturbed. The EIF can be used to construct a one-step estimator that corrects for first-order plug-in bias and is semiparametrically efficient under regularity conditions. However, for nonnegative functionals such as the STE, first-order linearization may be insufficient under boundary null hypotheses where the first-order term vanishes (Luedtke et al. , 2019 ; Williamson et al. , 2023 ) . 
 Following Robins et al. ( 2008 ) and van der Vaart ( 2014 ) , we therefore employ second-order influence functions, which arise from the quadratic term in a higher-order von Mises expansion. Formally, the second-order influence function is a symmetric, degenerate kernel Φ ¨ P ∈ L 2 ​ ( P ⊗ 2 ) \ddot{\Phi}_{P}\in L^{2}(P^{\otimes 2}) , satisfying: 
 d 2 d ​ t 2 ​ Φ ​ ( P t ) | t = 0 = 𝔼 P ⊗ 2 ​ [ Φ ¨ P ​ ( Z 1 , Z 2 ) ​ r ​ ( Z 1 , Z 2 ) ] , \frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Phi(P_{t})\Big|_{t=0}=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\otimes 2}}[\ddot{\Phi}_{P}(Z_{1},Z_{2})\,r(Z_{1},Z_{2})], 
 where r r is the second-order score function (Small and McLeish, 2011 ) . For paths of the form P t = ( 1 + t ​ s ) ​ P P_{t}=(1+ts)P , this reduces to s ⊗ s s\otimes s . In a nonparametric model, there is only one second-order influence function, and this is known as the second-order efficient influence function (Robins et al. , 2008 ) . The second-order EIF is analogous to the Hessian from multivariable calculus. Technical details on second-order tangent spaces, score functions, and influence functions are deferred to Sec.   B.1 . 
 An unfortunate fact uncovered in the literature is that most functionals of interest fail to be smooth enough to admit a second-order EIF (Robins et al. , 2008 ; van der Vaart, 2014 ) . In the DTE setting, this appears to be true for counterfactual f f -divergences, complicating the study of estimators’ limiting distributions under the null (Kennedy et al. , 2023 , Sec. 5.2) . A major finding of this work is that the Sinkhorn treatment effect is smooth enough to admit a second-order EIF under the null of equal counterfactual distributions. We leverage this property to construct a bias-corrected estimator of the STE with tractable asymptotic behavior. 
 3 Sinkhorn Treatment Effect 
 Let P ∈ 𝒫 P\in\mathcal{P} denote a data-generating distribution and fix entropic regularization ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . We define the Sinkhorn treatment effect from ( 1 ) as the functional 𝒮 : 𝒫 → ℝ \mathscr{S}:\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{R} given by 
 𝒮 ​ ( P ) = S ε ∘ J ∘ Ψ ​ ( P ) . \mathscr{S}(P)=S_{\varepsilon}\circ J\circ\Psi(P). 
 In this chaining argument, first, Ψ \Psi maps P P to the pair of counterfactual mean embeddings defined in ( 4 ). Second, J J is the canonical isometric embedding of ℋ \mathcal{H} in ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) , as discussed in Sec.   2 . For notational convenience, we use J J to denote the induced canonical embedding of the product space ℋ × ℋ \mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H} . Lastly, S ε S_{\varepsilon} computes the Sinkhorn divergence between the counterfactual distributions. 
 The goal of this section is to characterize the smoothness properties of 𝒮 \mathscr{S} and leverage them for efficient semiparametric estimation. Our analysis builds on recent advances in the differentiability theory of EOT (Goldfeld et al. , 2024 ; Kokot and Luedtke, 2025 ) . In particular, Goldfeld et al. ( 2024 ) analyze the Hadamard differentiability of the map ( μ , ν ) ↦ S ε ​ ( μ , ν ) (\mu,\nu)\mapsto{S}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right) in the ℓ ∞ ​ ( B s ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( B s ) \ell^{\infty}(B^{s})\times\ell^{\infty}(B^{s}) topology, where B s B^{s} is a unit ball in 𝒞 s ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{Y}) . Following this, we embed 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) into the normed linear space ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) via the KME, and view the Sinkhorn divergence as a smooth functional S ε : 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ⊂ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) → ℝ S_{\varepsilon}:\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\subset\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\to\mathbb{R} . Such identifications have been employed in literature on other spaces to derive limit theorems for EOT objects—see Appx.   C for details. 
 We show that this smoothness propagates through the causal composition defining 𝒮 \mathscr{S} , yielding first-order pathwise differentiability at all P ∈ 𝒫 P\in\mathcal{P} and second-order pathwise differentiability under the null that P 1 = P 0 P_{1}=P_{0} . We present the corresponding first- and second-order EIFs in what follows.

 This representation allows for a concise expression of the influence functions of 𝒮 \mathscr{S} , particularly in the second-order case. We begin by presenting the first-order result.

 The proof of Lem.   3.1 relies on Hadamard differentiability of S ε S_{\varepsilon} (Kokot and Luedtke, 2025 ) and pathwise differentiability of the Hilbert-valued parameter Ψ \Psi (Luedtke and Chung, 2024 ) , and is provided in Sec.   D.1 . 
 Before establishing the second-order pathwise differentiability of 𝒮 \mathscr{S} , we set some kernel operator notation.  Let 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R} denote the quotient space of all continuous functions identified up to shifts by constant functions, and denote by [ f ] [f] the corresponding equivalence class of f ∈ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) f\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) .  The operator K μ K_{\mu} is termed as the Hadamard operator of Sinkhorn divergence in Kokot and Luedtke ( 2025 ) and Gonzalez-Sanz et al. ( 2022 ) , and plays a central role in establishing second-order pathwise differentiability of STE. Its definition relies on lifting T μ T_{\mu} to quotient spaces: since T μ ​ 𝟙 𝒴 = 𝟙 𝒴 T_{\mu}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Y}} , T μ T_{\mu} has a nontrivial null space spanned by constant functions, rendering it non-invertible on 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) . As shown in Thm. 3.8 in Lavenant et al. ( 2024 ) , T μ T_{\mu} is a well-defined linear operator on 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R} , and ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 (I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1} exists and is a bounded linear operator on 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R} (see Section 3 of Lavenant et al. , 2024 ) . 
 Since 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R} is canonically dual to ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y}) , the bilinear form γ 0 ′ ​ K μ ​ γ 0 \gamma_{0}^{\prime}K_{\mu}\gamma_{0} is well-defined for all γ 0 , γ 0 ′ ∈ ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \gamma_{0},\gamma_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y}) . As shown in Kokot and Luedtke ( 2025 ) , K μ K_{\mu} is precisely the Hadamard operator of Sinkhorn divergence, governing the second-order expansion of the Sinkhorn divergence. We denote by k μ : 𝒴 × 𝒴 → ℝ k_{\mu}:\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R} the associated symmetric kernel, representing the bilinear form γ 1 ​ ( K μ ​ γ 2 ) \gamma_{1}(K_{\mu}\gamma_{2}) as ( γ 1 ⊗ γ 2 ) ​ k μ (\gamma_{1}\otimes\gamma_{2})k_{\mu} for γ 1 , γ 2 ∈ ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y}) . The explicit form of k μ k_{\mu} is derived in Lem.   C.1 . Letting ℋ 0 := { P ∈ 𝒫 : P 1 = P 0 } \mathscr{H}_{0}:=\{P\in\mathcal{P}:P_{1}=P_{0}\} with ( P 1 , P 0 ) (P_{1},P_{0}) as defined in ( 3 ), we now state our second-order pathwise differentiability result. 
 Theorem 3.2 (Second-order differentiability under the null) . 
 At any P ∈ ℋ 0 P\in\mathscr{H}_{0} , 𝒮 : 𝒫 → ℝ \mathscr{S}:\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{R} is second-order pathwise differentiable with the second-order efficient influence function 
 𝒮 ¨ P = ( I − ( I − ( I − P X ) ​ P A | X ) ​ P Y | A , X ) ⊗ 2 ​ g P , \ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P}=\left(I-\left(I-(I-P_{X})P_{A\,|\,X}\right)P_{Y\,|\,A,X}\right)^{\otimes 2}g_{P}, 
 (10) 
 where g P ​ ( z 1 , z 2 ) = ω P ​ ( a 1 , x 1 ) ​ ω P ​ ( a 2 , x 2 ) ​ k P 1 ​ ( y 1 , y 2 ) g_{P}(z_{1},z_{2})=\omega_{P}(a_{1},x_{1})\,\omega_{P}(a_{2},x_{2})\,k_{P_{1}}(y_{1},y_{2}) for z j = ( x j , a j , y j ) z_{j}=(x_{j},a_{j},y_{j}) and ω P ​ ( a , x ) = ( 2 ​ a − 1 ) / e P ​ ( a | x ) \omega_{P}(a,x)=(2a-1)/e_{P}(a\,|\,x) . 
 For shorthand, we will occasionally write g P = ω P ⊗ 2 ⊙ k P 1 g_{P}=\omega_{P}^{\otimes 2}\odot k_{P_{1}} ,
identifying ω P ⊗ 2 \omega_{P}^{\otimes 2} and k P 1 k_{P_{1}} with their canonical extensions
to 𝒵 2 \mathcal{Z}^{2} that ignore the y y and ( x , a ) (x,a) coordinates, respectively. 
 A second-order analysis of 𝒮 \mathscr{S} is possible under the null because the first derivative of the Sinkhorn divergence vanishes in these cases. This eliminates, through the second-order chain rule, the term that would ordinarily involve the second-order pathwise derivative of the KME map Ψ \Psi , whose presence would typically preclude the existence of a second-order EIF (van der Vaart, 2014 , pp. 681–682) . 1 1 1 The second-order analysis is also possible for the squared counterfactual maximum mean discrepancy. We derive its second-order EIF in Lem.   F.2 , which to the best of our knowledge, is a novel contribution of our work. Hence the second-order pathwise derivative of STE depends only on the first-order perturbation of Ψ \Psi , equivalently on its EIF, together with the second derivative of S ε S_{\varepsilon} . A similar phenomenon was observed in Luedtke et al. ( 2019 ) for a test based on a different discrepancy measure. 
 4 One-Step Estimation of STE 
 We now leverage the first- and second-order EIFs of STE to construct one-step bias-corrected estimators of 𝒮 ​ ( P ) \mathscr{S}(P) . Let P ∗ ∈ 𝒫 P^{*}\in\mathcal{P} be our data-generating distribution and Z 1 , … , Z n Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n} be independent draws from P ∗ P^{*} , with corresponding empirical distribution denoted by P n P_{n} . Let P ^ \widehat{P} be an initial estimator of P ∗ P^{*} constructed from an independent sample of size n n . For notational ease, we present our one-step estimators under the sample splitting approach, assuming P n P_{n} and P ^ \widehat{P} are based on independent data. A cross-fitting approach (Schick, 1986 ; Klaassen, 1987 ; Chernozhukov et al. , 2018 ) is recommended, and the resulting efficiency is discussed for both first- and second-order one-step estimators. Our test for the causal null is based on the second-order one-step estimator of 𝒮 ​ ( P ∗ ) \mathscr{S}(P^{*}) for a fixed ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . To reduce sensitivity to the choice of the unknown entropic regularization parameter ε \varepsilon , we also provide to a way to aggregate tests over a finite grid of ε \varepsilon values. 
 4.1 One-Step Estimator 
 The one-step estimator of 𝒮 ∗ := 𝒮 ​ ( P ∗ ) \mathscr{S}^{*}:=\mathscr{S}(P^{*}) is defined as 𝒮 ^ := 𝒮 ​ ( P ^ ) + P n ​ 𝒮 ˙ P ^ \widehat{\mathscr{S}}:=\mathscr{S}(\widehat{P})+P_{n}\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}} . We study conditions under which a cross-fitted version of 𝒮 ^ \widehat{\mathscr{S}} is asymptotically linear and semiparametrically efficient. As is standard in semiparametric theory, efficiency follows if 𝒮 ^ − 𝒮 ​ ( P ∗ ) = P n ​ 𝒮 ˙ P ∗ + o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) \widehat{\mathscr{S}}-\mathscr{S}(P^{*})=P_{n}\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}+o_{p}(n^{-1/2}) , in which case the asymptotic variance attains the efficiency bound (Bickel et al. , 1993 ; Chernozhukov et al. , 2018 ) .
This expansion is obtained via the decomposition 𝒮 ^ − 𝒮 ​ ( P ∗ ) − P n ​ 𝒮 ˙ P ∗ = ℛ n + 𝒟 n \widehat{\mathscr{S}}-\mathscr{S}(P^{*})-P_{n}\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}=\mathcal{R}_{n}+\mathcal{D}_{n} , where ℛ n := 𝒮 ​ ( P ^ ) + P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ˙ P ^ − 𝒮 ​ ( P ∗ ) \mathcal{R}_{n}:=\mathscr{S}(\widehat{P})+P^{*}\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}-\mathscr{S}(P^{*}) is the remainder term from first order von Mises expansion and 𝒟 n := ( P n − P ∗ ) ​ ( 𝒮 ˙ P ^ − 𝒮 ˙ P ∗ ) \mathcal{D}_{n}:=(P_{n}-P^{*})(\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}-\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}) is a stochastic drift term. Under appropriate convergence rates for the nuisance components of P ^ \widehat{P} , we show that both terms are o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) o_{p}(n^{-1/2}) in Sec.   E.1 .
Consequently, 𝒮 ^ \widehat{\mathscr{S}} is asymptotically linear, satisfying the weak convergence 
 n ​ ( 𝒮 ^ − 𝒮 ∗ ) → 𝑑 𝒩 ​ ( 0 , 𝔼 P ∗ ​ [ 𝒮 ˙ P ∗ 2 ​ ( Z ) ] ) . \sqrt{n}\big(\widehat{\mathscr{S}}-\mathscr{S}^{*}\big)\xrightarrow[]{d}\mathcal{N}\big(0,\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\big[\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}^{2}(Z)\big]\big). 
 (11) 
 This asymptotic normality enables the construction of asymptotically valid Wald-type confidence intervals for 𝒮 ∗ \mathscr{S}^{*} . 
 Under the null hypothesis ( P ∗ ∈ ℋ 0 P^{*}\in\mathscr{H}_{0} ), the first-order EIF is degenerate, i.e. 𝒮 ˙ P = 0 \dot{\mathscr{S}}_{P}=0 , since υ 1 ( P 1 , P 1 ) = υ 0 ( P 1 , P 1 ) = 0 \upsilon_{1}^{(P_{1},P_{1})}=\upsilon_{0}^{(P_{1},P_{1})}=0 . As a consequence, the first-order limit distribution in ( 11 ) collapses to a point mass, rendering the first-order inference invalid. To address this, we construct a second-order one-step estimator based on a Newton–Raphson bias correction, in the spirit of Robins et al. ( 2008 ) , which attains n n -rate convergence rate to 𝒮 ∗ \mathscr{S}^{*} under the null. Inference based on this second-order expansion yields asymptotically valid hypothesis tests for detecting DTEs, with the second-order one-step estimator serving as the test statistic. 
 The second-order one-step estimator for 𝒮 ∗ \mathscr{S}^{*} is defined as 
 𝒮 ¯ := 𝒮 ​ ( P ^ ) + P n ​ 𝒮 ˙ P ^ + 1 2 ​ 𝕌 n ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ , \overline{\mathscr{S}}:=\mathscr{S}(\widehat{P})+P_{n}\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{U}_{n}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}, 
 (12) 
 where 𝕌 n \mathbb{U}_{n} denotes the canonical U-statistic operator from the same samples as P n P_{n} . However, second-order pathwise differentiability of 𝒮 \mathscr{S} is only established under the null, and therefore the second-order EIF 𝒮 ¨ P ^ \ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}} need not be well-defined at a general P ^ ∉ ℋ 0 \widehat{P}\notin\mathscr{H}_{0} . To address this, we construct an extension of the second-order EIF to distributions P ∉ ℋ 0 P\notin\mathscr{H}_{0} . This extension is defined by the same analytic expression as in ( 10 ), where for notational simplicity we denote this extension by the same symbol, 𝒮 ¨ P \ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P} . Since k P 1 ≠ k P 0 k_{P_{1}}\neq k_{P_{0}} when P ∉ ℋ 0 P\notin\mathscr{H}_{0} , the definition in non-null cases depends on the choice of counterfactual distribution used to construct the self-transport kernel. We fix this choice to P ^ 1 \widehat{P}_{1} in our theoretical arguments for concreteness, though any convex combination of P ^ 1 \widehat{P}_{1} and P ^ 0 \widehat{P}_{0} would enjoy similar guarantees. Note that 𝒮 ¨ P \ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P} is already P P -centered, and therefore, does not require further centering common in second-order one-step estimation (Luedtke et al. , 2019 ) . 
 4.2 Inference Under Null 
 We now study the asymptotic properties of ( 12 ). Define the centering operator C P ∗ C_{P^{*}} such that for any measurable kernel ℓ : 𝒵 → 𝒵 → ℝ \ell:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R} , C ∗ ​ ℓ ​ ( z , z ′ ) C^{*}\ell(z,z^{\prime}) is equal to 
 ℓ ​ ( z , z ′ ) − P ∗ ​ ℓ ​ ( ⋅ , z ′ ) − P ∗ ​ ℓ ​ ( z , ⋅ ) + [ ( P ∗ ) ⊗ 2 ​ ℓ ] ​ 𝟙 , \ell(z,z^{\prime})-{P^{*}\ell(\cdot,z^{\prime})-P^{*}\ell(z,\cdot)}+[(P^{*})^{\otimes 2}\ell]\mathbbm{1},

The following breakdown is key to the inference of 𝒮 ¯ \overline{\mathscr{S}} :

 = 𝒮 ​ ( P ^ ) + ( P ∗ − P ^ ) ​ 𝒮 ˙ P ^ + 1 2 ​ ( P ∗ − P ^ ) 2 ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ − 𝒮 ​ ( P ∗ ) . \displaystyle=\mathscr{S}(\widehat{P})+(P^{*}-\widehat{P})\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}+\frac{1}{2}(P^{*}-\widehat{P})^{2}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}-\mathscr{S}(P^{*}). 
 In the next result, we show that all three terms above are o P ​ ( n − 1 ) o_{P}(n^{-1}) under suitable conditions. Consequently, the asymptotic distribution of n ​ ( 𝒮 ¯ − 𝒮 ​ ( P ∗ ) ) n(\overline{\mathscr{S}}-\mathscr{S}(P^{*})) is determined by the limiting distribution of n ​ 𝕌 n ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ / 2 n\mathbb{U}_{n}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}/2 . The drift term 𝒟 n \mathscr{D}_{n} is o P ​ ( n − 1 ) o_{P}(n^{-1}) provided that the integrand 𝒮 ˙ P ^ + ∫ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ ​ ( z , ⋅ ) ​ 𝑑 P ∗ ​ ( z ) \dot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}+\int\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}(z,\cdot)\,dP^{*}(z) is o P ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) o_{P}(n^{-1/2}) in L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ) L^{2}(P^{*}) norm ( Lem.   E.1 ). This result follows from a conditioning argument that combines Chebyshev’s inequality with the dominated convergence theorem. We prove the desired rate on our integrand in Lem.   E.5 .
The U-process term 𝒰 n \mathscr{U}_{n} is also o P ​ ( n − 1 ) o_{P}(n^{-1}) using similar arguments using Chebyshev’s inequality, only requiring L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ⊗ P ∗ ) L^{2}(P^{*}\otimes P^{*}) convergence of C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}} to 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ \ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}} —see Lem.   E.6 . Finally, the remainder term ℛ n \mathscr{R}_{n} is o p ​ ( n − 1 ) o_{p}(n^{-1}) under regularity conditions commonly satisfied by smooth statistical functionals (Luedtke et al. , 2019 ; Robins et al. , 2009 ) . We verify these conditions for ℛ n \mathscr{R}_{n} in Lem.   E.8 and discuss the feasibility of the required assumptions later in this section. Similar to the first-order one-step estimator, a cross-fitting approach for 𝒮 ¯ \overline{\mathscr{S}} is recommended (Kim and Ramdas, 2024 ) , as it can improve sample efficiency. 
 For brevity, we write P Y | a , X P_{Y\,|\,a,X} for P Y | A = a , X P_{Y\,|\,A=a,X} , e ^ \hat{e}\, for e P ^ e_{\widehat{P}} , and e ∗ e^{*} for e P ∗ e_{P^{*}} . Recall k μ k_{\mu} is the kernel associated with operator K μ K_{\mu} and defined in Lem.   C.1 . We now state the main theorem on asymptotics of the second-order one-step estimator 𝒮 ¯ \overline{\mathscr{S}} . 
 Theorem 4.1 (Null distribution) . 
 Let P ∗ ∈ ℋ 0 P^{*}\in\mathscr{H}_{0} . Suppose: 
 1. 
 the estimated nuisance parameters satisfy 
 P X ∗ ​ ( e ^ ​ ( a | X ) e ∗ ​ ( a | X ) − 1 ) ​ ( P ^ Y | a , X − P Y | a , X ∗ ) ​ k P ^ 1 ​ ( Y , y ) = o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) , P^{*}_{X}\left(\tfrac{\hat{e}(a\,|\,X)}{e^{*}(a\,|\,X)}-1\right)\big({\widehat{P}_{Y\,|\,a,X}-P^{*}_{Y\,|\,a,X}}\big)k_{\widehat{P}_{1}}(Y,y)=o_{p}(n^{-1/2}), 
 uniformly in y ∈ 𝒴 y\in\mathcal{Y} , a ∈ { 1 , 0 } a\in\{1,0\} ,

 n ​ 𝒮 ¯ = n 2 ​ 𝕌 n ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ + o p ​ ( 1 ) → 𝑑 ∑ j = 1 ∞ λ j 2 ​ ( N j 2 − 1 ) , n\overline{\mathscr{S}}=\frac{n}{2}\mathbb{U}_{n}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}+o_{p}(1)\xrightarrow[]{d}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{j}}{2}(N_{j}^{2}-1), 
 (13) 
 where { λ j } j = 1 ∞ \{\lambda_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty} are the eigenvalues of the integral operator f ↦ ∫ 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ ​ ( ⋅ , z ) ​ f ​ ( z ) ​ 𝑑 P ∗ ​ ( z ) f\mapsto\int\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}(\cdot,z)\,f(z)\,dP^{*}(z) , counted with multiplicity, and { N j } j = 1 ∞ \{N_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty} are iid standard normal random variables. 
 The proof of Thm.   4.1 is presented in Sec.   E.2 .
We now discuss the feasibility of the conditions. We let the initial estimator P ^ \widehat{P} of the data-generating distribution P ∗ P^{*} satisfies ‖ P ^ a − P a ∗ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) = 𝒪 p ​ ( n − r ) \|\widehat{P}_{a}-P^{*}_{a}\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(n^{-r}) for some 1 / 4 < r < 1 / 2 1/4<r<1/2 and a ∈ { 1 , 0 } a\in\{1,0\} .
The first condition is standard in the doubly robust causal inference literature (Luedtke and Chung, 2024 ) and is implied by o p ​ ( n − 1 / 4 ) o_{p}(n^{-1/4}) convergence of the nuisance estimators in their appropriate norms. The second condition is a consistency requirement ensuring stability of the second-order term; it follows from convergence of ω P ^ ⊗ 2 ⊙ k P ^ 1 \omega_{\widehat{P}}^{\otimes 2}\odot k_{\widehat{P}_{1}} to ω P ∗ ⊗ 2 ⊙ k P 1 ∗ \omega_{P^{*}}^{\otimes 2}\odot k_{P^{*}_{1}} in L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ⊗ P ∗ ) L^{2}(P^{*}\otimes P^{*}) , together with a uniform bound on the Radon–Nikodym derivatives d ​ P ^ / d ​ P ∗ d\widehat{P}/dP^{*} over P ^ ∈ 𝒫 \widehat{P}\in\mathcal{P} . The third condition controls the remainder of the second-order von Mises expansion and is o p ​ ( n − 1 ) o_{p}(n^{-1}) under n − 1 / 3 n^{-1/3} -rate conditions on the nuisance estimators ( Lem.   E.8 ). 
 4.3 Formulation of Test 
 To test the null hypothesis that P ∗ ∈ ℋ 0 P^{*}\in\mathscr{H}_{0} , we use the test statistic n ​ 𝒮 ¯ n\overline{\mathscr{S}} . Under the null, Thm.   4.1 establishes that n ​ 𝒮 ¯ n\overline{\mathscr{S}} converges weakly to a weighted sum of centered chi-squared distributions. A non-conservative test of nominal level α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) rejects the null hypotheses when n ​ 𝒮 ¯ n\overline{\mathscr{S}} exceeds the ( 1 − α ) (1-\alpha) th quantile of the limiting distribution, q 1 − α q_{1-\alpha} . The weak convergence in ( 13 ) implies the corresponding test asymptotically attains nominal type I error: 
 lim sup n → ∞ P ∗ ​ ( n ​ 𝒮 ¯ ≥ q 1 − α ) = α . \limsup_{n\to\infty}P^{*}(n\overline{\mathscr{S}}\geq q_{1-\alpha})=\alpha. 
 (14) 
 Moreover, if q ^ 1 − α \hat{q}_{1-\alpha} is a consistent estimator of q 1 − α q_{1-\alpha} , then the same conclusion holds with q 1 − α q_{1-\alpha} replaced by q ^ 1 − α \hat{q}_{1-\alpha} in ( 14 ).
To obtain such an estimator, we use the general approximation strategy of Gretton et al. ( 2009 ) , which was adapted to tests based on second-order influence functions in Luedtke et al. ( 2019 ) . This approximates the spectrum of the operator f ↦ ∫ 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ ​ ( ⋅ , z ) ​ f ​ ( z ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( z ) f\mapsto\int\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}(\cdot,z)f(z)dP(z) through that of an empirical Gram matrix. In our case, this is the Gram matrix of the kernel 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ \ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}} , which depends on P ∗ {P^{*}} through three nuisance parameters: the propensity e ∗ e^{*} , outcome regression model P Y | A , X ∗ P_{Y\,|\,A,X}^{*} , and entropic potentials ( υ 1 ( P 1 ∗ , P 0 ∗ ) , υ 0 ( P 1 ∗ , P 0 ∗ ) ) (\upsilon_{1}^{(P_{1}^{*},P_{0}^{*})},\upsilon_{0}^{(P_{1}^{*},P_{0}^{*})}) . Since these nuisances are unknown, we estimate them to obtain 𝒮 ¨ P ^ \ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}} , form its empirical Gram matrix, and compute its eigenvalues { λ ^ j } j = 1 n \{\hat{\lambda}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n} . The limiting distribution is then approximated by ∑ j = 1 n λ ^ j ​ ( N j 2 − 1 ) \sum_{j=1}^{n}\hat{\lambda}_{j}(N_{j}^{2}-1) , and its ( 1 − α ) (1-\alpha) -th quantile q ^ 1 − α \hat{q}_{1-\alpha} is obtained via Monte Carlo simulation. Under sufficient regularity—specifically, if the nuisance estimators converge at rates fast enough to guarantee operator-norm consistency of the plug-in kernel and continuity of the limiting spectral distribution—nominal type 1 error control follows by standard kernel spectral approximation and perturbation arguments (Shawe-Taylor et al. , 2005 ; Zwald and Blanchard, 2005 ; Rosasco et al. , 2010 ) . 
 Regarding power, the proposed test will reject fixed alternatives P ∗ ∉ ℋ 0 P^{*}\not\in\mathscr{H}_{0} with probability tending to 1 1 . This follows from the relation 𝒮 ¯ = 𝒮 ^ + 𝕌 n ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ / 2 \overline{\mathscr{S}}=\widehat{\mathscr{S}}+\mathbb{U}_{n}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}/2 between the second- and first-order bias-corrected estimators, the approximate 1-degeneracy of 𝕌 n ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ \mathbb{U}_{n}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}} resulting from its P ^ \widehat{P} -centering, and the asymptotic normality in ( 11 )—see Lem.   E.9 . 
 4.4 STEAgg: Max-Aggregated Test 
 In finite samples, power of the STE-based test can depend on the choice of entropic regularization parameter ε \varepsilon : small values cause dispersion in the asymptotic distribution of the U-statistic, while large values oversmooth the discrepancy and reduce power. To reduce sensitivity to this choice, we propose a multiple testing procedure, STEAgg, that aggregates evidence over a finite set of stable candidate regularization parameters Ξ = { ε 1 , … , ε m } ⊂ ( 0 , ∞ ) \Xi=\{\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{m}\}\subset(0,\infty) . We also establish asymptotic nominal type I error guarantee for STEAgg. 
 For each ε ∈ Ξ \varepsilon\in\Xi , let 𝒯 n , ε = n ​ 𝒮 ¯ \mathcal{T}_{n,\varepsilon}=n\overline{\mathscr{S}} denote the test statistic with explicit dependence on n n and ε \varepsilon . Under the null, Thm.   4.1 shows that 𝒯 n , ε → 𝑑 W ​ ( ε ) \mathcal{T}_{n,\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{d}W(\varepsilon) , where W ​ ( ε ) W(\varepsilon) is the Gaussian chaos limit in ( 13 ), induced by the spectral decomposition of the degenerate kernel 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ \ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}} . We define the aggregated test statistic 
 𝒯 n agg = max ε ∈ Ξ ⁡ 𝒯 n , ε q n , ε , \mathcal{T}_{n}^{\mathrm{agg}}=\max_{\varepsilon\in\Xi}\frac{\mathcal{T}_{n,\varepsilon}}{q_{n,\varepsilon}}, 
 (15) 
 where q n , ε q_{n,\varepsilon} consistently estimates the ( 1 − β ) (1-\beta) quantile of W ​ ( ε ) W(\varepsilon) for a pre-specified β ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \beta\in(0,1) . Normalization by q n , ε q_{n,\varepsilon} calibrates each statistic 𝒯 n , ε \mathcal{T}_{n,\varepsilon} to the scale of its own marginal null distribution. This ensures that aggregation compares evidence for different ε \varepsilon on a common scale, rather than allowing monotonic dependence on ε \varepsilon to dominate the maximum. 
 Theorem 4.2 (Asymptotic behavior of STEAgg) . 
 If the null holds ( P ∗ ∈ ℋ 0 P^{*}\in\mathscr{H}_{0} ), the conditions of Thm.   4.1 hold, and q n , ε → 𝑃 q ε > 0 q_{n,\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{P}q_{\varepsilon}>0 for each ε ∈ Ξ \varepsilon\in\Xi , then 
 𝒯 n agg → 𝑑 W agg := max ε ∈ Ξ ⁡ W ​ ( ε ) / q ε . \mathcal{T}_{n}^{\mathrm{agg}}\xrightarrow{d}W^{\mathrm{agg}}:=\max_{\varepsilon\in\Xi}W(\varepsilon)/q_{\varepsilon}. 
 Further, if P ∗ ∉ ℋ 0 P^{*}\notin\mathscr{H}_{0} is a fixed alternative, the nuisance estimators satisfy the conditions in Lem.   E.2 , and Lem.   E.3 , and, for each ε ∈ Ξ \varepsilon\in\Xi , 𝒮 ¨ ε , P ∗ ∈ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ⊗ P ∗ ) \ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\varepsilon,P^{*}}\in L^{2}(P^{*}\otimes P^{*}) , and ‖ C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ ε , P ^ − C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ ε , P ∗ ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ⊗ P ∗ ) = o p ​ ( 1 ) \|{C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\varepsilon,\widehat{P}}-C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\varepsilon,P^{*}}}\|_{L^{2}(P^{*}\otimes P^{*})}=o_{p}(1) , then the proposed test will reject the null with probability converging to 1 1 as n → ∞ n\to\infty . 
 The proof proceeds by first deriving the joint limit distribution of ( 𝒯 n , ε 1 , … , 𝒯 n , ε m ) (\mathcal{T}_{n,\varepsilon_{1}},\dots,\mathcal{T}_{n,\varepsilon_{m}}) . Then we apply Slutsky’s theorem together with the continuous mapping theorem to obtain the limiting distribution of the maximum—see Appx.   H . 
 5 Finite-Sample Estimation 
 We derive explicit, computable formulas for the first- and second-order one-step estimators, 𝒮 ^ \widehat{\mathscr{S}} and 𝒮 ¯ \overline{\mathscr{S}} . Nuisance parameters are estimated on one data split, with all remaining computations performed on the empirical distribution of the other data. In finite samples, expectation operators in ( 8 ) and ( 10 ) are replaced by matrix operations, kernel evaluations by Gram matrices, and function evaluations by vectors, yielding concise, intuitive, and implementable formulas in closed form. 
 Sample splitting and nuisance estimation. 
 Let 𝒟 n 1 = { z i } i = 1 n \mathcal{D}_{n}^{1}=\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n} and 𝒟 n 2 = { z i ′ } i = 1 n \mathcal{D}_{n}^{2}=\{z_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i=1}^{n} be independent
samples from P P , where z i = ( x i , a i , y i ) z_{i}=(x_{i},a_{i},y_{i}) . Using 𝒟 n 2 \mathcal{D}_{n}^{2} , we estimate the nuisances: the
propensity score e P e_{P} , outcome regression P Y | A , X P_{Y\,|\,A,X} , centered entropic
potentials ( υ 1 ( P 1 , P 0 ) , υ 0 ( P 1 , P 0 ) ) (\upsilon_{1}^{(P_{1},P_{0})},\upsilon_{0}^{(P_{1},P_{0})}) , and self-transport entropic potential φ 1 ( P 1 , P 1 ) \varphi_{1}^{(P_{1},P_{1})} . Modern nonparametric learning methods can be used for estimating e P e_{P} and P Y | A , X P_{Y\,|\,A,X} , while entropic potentials should be computed via
the Sinkhorn algorithm (Cuturi, 2013 ) . We denote the resulting estimators by e ^ , P ^ Y | A , X , υ ^ 1 , υ ^ 0 , φ ^ 1 \hat{e},\widehat{P}_{Y\,|\,A,X},\hat{\upsilon}_{1},\hat{\upsilon}_{0},\hat{\varphi}_{1} . Let 𝟏 n \mathbf{1}_{n} and 𝟏 n × n \mathbf{1}_{n\times n} denotes the all-ones vector and matrix, respectively. Also define the data projection operator 𝐃 A , X \mathbf{D}_{A,X} acting on tensors 𝐀 ∈ ℝ 2 × n , p \mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times n,p} by ( 𝐃 A ​ X ​ 𝐀 ) [ i , : ] = 𝐀 [ 2 − a i , i , : ] (\mathbf{D}_{AX}\mathbf{A})_{[i,:]}=\mathbf{A}_{[2-a_{i},i,:]} . 
 Precomputations. 
 Let 𝐆 \mathbf{G} be the n × n n\times n Gram matrix of 𝒟 n 1 \mathcal{D}_{n}^{1} . For i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] and a ∈ { 1 , 0 } a\in\{1,0\} , define the nuisance evaluations 
 𝐄 i = e ^ ​ ( x i ) , 𝐔 i a = υ ^ a ​ ( y i ) , 𝐅 i = φ ^ 1 ​ ( y i ) . \mathbf{E}_{i}=\hat{e}(x_{i}),\,\,\mathbf{U}^{a}_{i}=\hat{\upsilon}_{a}(y_{i}),\,\,\mathbf{F}_{i}=\hat{\varphi}_{1}(y_{i}). 
 Let 𝐏 ∈ ℝ n × 2 × n \mathbf{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 2\times n} collect evaluations of P ^ Y | A , X \widehat{P}_{Y\,|\,A,X} , so 𝐏 i , j , k = P ^ Y | A , X ​ ( y i |  2 − j , x k ) \mathbf{P}_{i,j,k}=\widehat{P}_{Y\,|\,A,X}(y_{i}\,|\,2-j,x_{k}) , and define the marginal
 𝐏 1 \mathbf{P}^{1} (resp. 𝐏 0 \mathbf{P}^{0} ) by averaging the treatment (resp. control) slice of 𝐏 \mathbf{P} along along the third axis; e.g., 𝐏 i 1 = 1 n ​ ∑ k = 1 n 𝐏 i , 1 , k \mathbf{P}^{1}_{i}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathbf{P}_{i,1,k} . 
 Debiasing. 
 Using Algs.   1 and  2 in Appx.   G , we compute the first- and second-order EIF evaluations on 𝒟 n 1 \mathcal{D}_{n}^{1} , yielding 𝐈 1 ∈ ℝ n \mathbf{I}^{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n} and 𝐈 2 ∈ ℝ n × n \mathbf{I}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n} . In both cases, the intermediate steps T i T_{i} correspond to the four additive components of the operator ( I − P Y | A , X + P Y , A | X − P ) (I-P_{Y\,|\,A,X}+P_{Y,A\,|\,X}-P) appearing in ( 8 ) and ( 10 ). In both algorithms, multiplication by matrix 𝐏 \mathbf{P} is done along the Y Y (first) axis of both matrices. The resulting one-step estimator is 𝒮 ^ n = S ε ​ ( 𝐏 1 , 𝐏 0 ) + ( 1 n ⊤ ​ 𝐈 1 ) / n \widehat{\mathscr{S}}_{n}=S_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{P}^{1},\mathbf{P}^{0})+\ (1_{n}^{\top}\mathbf{I}^{1})/n , and the test statistic ( 12 ) is
 𝒮 ¯ n = 𝒮 ^ n + ( 1 n ⊤ ​ 𝐈 2 ​ 1 n − Tr ​ ( 𝐈 2 ) ) / 2 ​ n ​ ( n − 1 ) . \overline{\mathscr{S}}_{n}=\widehat{\mathscr{S}}_{n}+\left(1_{n}^{\top}\mathbf{I}^{2}1_{n}-\text{Tr}(\mathbf{I}^{2})\right)/2n(n-1). 
 Computational complexity. 
 Treating the generic estimators that yield e ^ \hat{e} and P ^ Y | A , X \widehat{P}_{Y\,|\,A,X} as black boxes, the procedure’s remaining complexity is O ​ ( n 2 ​ ( n + t ) ) O(n^{2}(n+t)) , with t t the number of Sinkhorn iterations. This comprises O ​ ( n 2 ​ t ) O(n^{2}t) for the Sinkhorn algorithm (Dvurechensky et al. , 2018 ) , O ​ ( n 2 ) O(n^{2}) for the precomputations and Alg.   1 , and O ​ ( n 3 ) O(n^{3}) for Alg.   2 . Several strategies can substantially reduce these costs; see Sec.   I.3 . In particular, low-rank Nyström approximations to the Gibbs kernel matrix used in the Sinkhorn algorithm can reduce the per-iteration Sinkhorn cost from O ​ ( n 2 ) O(n^{2}) to O ​ ( n ​ r ) O(nr) , where r ≪ n r\ll n is the Nyström rank. Similar Nyström-based ideas can also be used to accelerate the computations in Alg.   1 and Alg.   2 . 
 6 Experiments 
 We evaluate our methods on simulated Gaussian outcomes and microscopy medical image outcomes (Veeling et al. , 2018 ) . In both settings, the causal mechanism is simulated to control the treatment effect, so ground truth regarding the null hypothesis is known. We compare our proposed test to an MMD-based distributional treatment effect (shortened as MTE) test constructed using a similar second-order influence function–based debiasing strategy—see Appx.   F . 
 The propensity score nuisance e P ∗ e_{P^{*}} is estimated using XGBoost (Chen, 2016 ) with hyperparameters selected via 5-fold cross-validation. The distribution P Y | A , X ∗ P^{*}_{Y\,|\,A,X} is modeled using a conditional normalizing flow with affine coupling layers, following the RealNVP framework (Dinh et al. , 2017 ) . Centered entropic potentials are computed via the Sinkhorn algorithm (Cuturi, 2013 ) . See Sec.   I.2 for computational details, memory usage, and wall-clock time for our experiments. 
 6.1 Simulation Study 
 Figure 2 : I : Type I error of the MTE and STE under null ( θ = 0.0 \theta=0.0 ); II : Power of MTE and STE under increasing separation between counterfactual distributions (increasing θ \theta ) under Exp (i); III : Power of MTE and STE under increasing separation between counterfactual distributions (increasing θ \theta ) under Exp (ii); IV : Mean squared error of plugin vs one-step STE for θ = 1.6 \theta=1.6 from Exp (i); V : Coverage of Wald-type 95 % 95\% confidence intervals for θ = 1.6 \theta=1.6 from Exp (i).
 We conduct a simulation study to assess the finite-sample performance of the proposed one-step estimator for STE and the associated hypothesis test under known ground truth. We consider a low-dimensional model where the observed data ( X , A , Y ) ∼ P (X,A,Y)\sim P is simulated as follows: the covariate X ∈ ℝ 3 X\in\mathbb{R}^{3} is sampled from a standard normal distribution, the treatment assignment assumes a logistic model on X X , and the conditional outcome Y | A , X Y\,|\,A,X follows a Gaussian distribution. 
 To probe the sensitivity of MTE and STE, we consider two distinct regimes governing the separation between the counterfactual outcome distributions P 0 P_{0} and P 1 P_{1} .
 Exp (i) (Mean shift): The counterfactual distributions differ only in their means: P 0 = 𝒩 ​ ( 0 , Σ ) P_{0}=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma) and P 1 = 𝒩 ​ ( θ ​ 𝟏 2 , Σ ) P_{1}=\mathcal{N}(\theta\mathbf{1}_{2},\Sigma) , where θ ≥ 0 \theta\geq 0 controls the magnitude of the shift. Exp (ii) (Covariance shift): The counterfactual distributions share the same mean but differ in covariance: P 0 = 𝒩 ​ ( 0 , Σ ) P_{0}=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma) and P 1 = 𝒩 ​ ( 𝟎 2 , Σ + θ ​ Δ ) P_{1}=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}_{2},\Sigma+\theta\Delta) , where Δ = u ​ v ⊤ + v ​ u ⊤ \Delta=uv^{\top}+vu^{\top} and u , v u,v are the eigenvectors of Σ \Sigma . This is a symmetric rank-2 covariance perturbation that induces a shearing-type geometric deformation. The parameter θ \theta controls the separation between counterfactual distributions, with θ = 0 \theta=0 corresponding to the null and θ > 0 \theta>0 alternatives. See exact simulation setup in Sec.   I.1.1 . Results are averaged over 10 3 10^{3} Monte Carlo replications and we report the results using the median heuristic to select ε \varepsilon . Additional sensitivity analysis over a finite grid of ε \varepsilon values, together with the performance of the corresponding max-aggregated tests, are reported in Sec.   I.1.1 . 
 Fig.   2 reports
(I) empirical type I error of the MTE and STE tests at the nominal 5 % 5\% level,
(II) empirical power across values of θ \theta in Exp (i), (III) empirical power across values of θ \theta in Exp (ii),
(IV) mean squared error of the plug-in and one-step STE estimators, and (V) empirical coverage of Wald-type 95 % 95\% confidence intervals.
As the sample size increases, both tests approach a nominal type I error and unit
power in this setting.
Panel (IV) shows that one-step estimation substantially reduces plug-in bias.
Though the confidence interval coverages in (V) are similar, the estimand covered by the STE interval better captures the geometry of the problem than does the one covered by the MTE interval. Indeed for Exp (ii), when θ = 0.2 \theta=0.2 , the (MTE, STE) values are ( 0.26 , 4.12 ) × 10 − 3 (0.26,4.12)\times 10^{-3} , when θ = 0.4 \theta=0.4 , the (MTE, STE) values are ( 1.04 , 16.42 ) × 10 − 3 (1.04,16.42)\times 10^{-3} , and at θ = 0.8 \theta=0.8 , the (MTE, STE) values are ( 4.60 , 68.43 ) × 10 − 3 (4.60,68.43)\times 10^{-3} . 
 6.2 Image Dataset 
 We next consider a setting with high-dimensional, structured outcomes. We use the PatchCamelyon (PCam) dataset (Veeling et al. , 2018 ) , which consists of 96 × 96 96\times 96 microscopy medical images, labeled by the presence or absence of metastatic tissue. We partition the images into two sets: 𝒟 1 \mathcal{D}_{1} (metastatic) and 𝒟 0 \mathcal{D}_{0} (non-metastatic), each containing over 100 ​ K 100K images. To reduce dimensionality, we use the first ten principal components of ResNet-18 (He et al. , 2016 ) embeddings. 
 We generate synthetic observational data ( X , A , Y ) (X,A,Y) .
Covariates X ∈ ℝ 3 X\in\mathbb{R}^{3} are drawn from a standard Gaussian distribution and determine
both latent disease status and treatment assignment via logistic models.
Outcomes are generated by sampling PCam images conditional on disease status and
treatment. Specifically, non-diseased units always generate outcomes from
 𝒟 0 \mathcal{D}_{0} . Diseased untreated units generate outcomes from 𝒟 1 \mathcal{D}_{1} ,
while diseased treated units generate outcomes from 𝒟 0 \mathcal{D}_{0} with
covariate-dependent probability
 q ​ ( x ) = expit ​ ( θ + 10 − 2 ​  1 3 ⊤ ​ x ) q(x)=\mathrm{expit}\!\left(\theta+10^{-2}\,\mathbf{1}_{3}^{\top}x\right) ,
and from 𝒟 1 \mathcal{D}_{1} otherwise. This construction yields heterogeneous,
covariate-dependent treatment effects on the distribution of image outcomes. Each simulation uses 10 4 10^{4} observations, split evenly to form P ^ \widehat{P} and
 P n P_{n} . Results are averaged over 10 3 10^{3} Monte Carlo replications for a range of values of θ \theta between [ − 20 , 20 ] [-20,20] . The case θ = − 20 \theta=-20 serves as an approximate
null, as treatment success is effectively unobservable at this sample size,
while θ = 20 \theta=20 corresponds to near-certain treatment success. Additional implementation
details are provided in Sec.   I.1.2 . 
 Empirical type I error and power are reported in Fig.   5 against the probability of success of treatment. For a moderate sample size of n = 5000 n=5000 and α = 0.05 \alpha=\textnormal{0.05} , STE reliably controls type I error at 0.04, while MTE has it inflated at roughly 0.11. Given that it fails to control type I error, it is not surprising that MTE has slightly higher power in this setting, though both have power that increases with the probability of success of treatment. Similarly to the previous simulation, STE better captures the geometry of the problem than does MTE. When θ = 20 \theta=20 , STE ≈ 2 \textnormal{STE}\approx 2 while MTE ≈ 5 × 10 − 3 \textnormal{MTE}\approx 5\times 10^{-3} . 
 7 Conclusion 
 We propose a novel optimal transport-based DTE that addresses limitations of existing approaches. Unlike kernel-based measures such as MMD, which suffer from flat geometry under limited overlap, and
 f f -divergence-based DTEs, which require mutual absolute continuity, our formulation faithfully lifts Euclidean geometry to probability spaces and naturally accommodates discrete outcomes.
Our contributions invite future work in several directions. One involves confronting the numerical instability that can arise from large eigenvalues of ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 (I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1} (Lavenant et al. , 2024 ) . Recent work proposes replacing the Sinkhorn kernel k μ k_{\mu} with ξ μ \xi_{\mu} to enable faster computations (Kokot and Luedtke, 2025 , Lem. 12) ; fully establishing the theoretical validity of this substitution for STE is an open problem. Another is to scale inference to larger datasets. For this, we recommend extending our approach to batched Sinkhorn divergences (Peyré et al. , 2019 ) .

 Appendix B Background 
 B.1 Pathwise differentiability and EIF 
 We refer the reader to Luedtke and Chung ( 2024 ) for a detailed exposition of pathwise differentiability. We briefly recall the key definitions needed for our analysis.
As introduced in Sec.   2 , let 𝒫 ⊂ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒵 ) \mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z}) denote our statistical model, where 𝒵 := 𝒳 × { 0 , 1 } × 𝒴 \mathcal{Z}:=\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}\times\mathcal{Y} is a Polish space equipped with its Borel σ \sigma -algebra ℬ 𝒵 \mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}} . We assume that all distributions in 𝒫 \mathcal{P} are dominated by a σ \sigma -finite measure λ \lambda . 
 A parametric submodel ( P t : t ∈ [ 0 , δ ) ) ⊂ 𝒫 (P_{t}:t\in[0,\delta))\subset\mathcal{P} with P 0 = P P_{0}=P is said to be quadratic mean differentiable (QMD) at P P if there exists a (Fisher) score function s ∈ L 0 2 ​ ( P ) s\in L_{0}^{2}(P) such that 
 ‖ p t − p − t ​ s ​ p ‖ L 2 ​ ( λ ) = o ​ ( t ) , \big\|\sqrt{p_{t}}-\sqrt{p}-ts\sqrt{p}\big\|_{L^{2}(\lambda)}=o(t), 
 where p t = d ​ P t / d ​ λ p_{t}=dP_{t}/d\lambda and p = d ​ P / d ​ λ p=dP/d\lambda . We denote by 𝔓 ​ ( P , 𝒫 , s ) \mathfrak{P}(P,\mathcal{P},s) the collection of all QMD submodels at P P with score function s s . The set { s ∈ L 0 2 ​ ( P ) : 𝔓 ​ ( P , 𝒫 , s ) ≠ ∅ } \{s\in L_{0}^{2}(P):\mathfrak{P}(P,\mathcal{P},s)\neq\emptyset\} is called the tangent set of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} at P P . Its closed linear span in L 0 2 ​ ( P ) L_{0}^{2}(P) is the tangent space , denoted by 𝒫 ˙ P \dot{\mathcal{P}}_{P} . 
 Let Φ : 𝒫 → ℋ \Phi:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{H} be a Hilbert-valued parameter. We say that Φ \Phi is pathwise differentiable at P P , relative to the model 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , if there exists a continuous linear operator D ​ Φ P : 𝒫 ˙ P → ℋ D\Phi_{P}:\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{P}\to\mathcal{H} such that, for all ( P t : t ∈ [ 0 , δ ) ) ∈ 𝔓 ( P , 𝒫 , s ) (P_{t}:t\in[0,\delta))\in\mathfrak{P}(P,\mathcal{P},s) , 
 ‖ Φ ​ ( P t ) − Φ ​ ( P ) − t ​ D ​ Φ P ​ ( s ) ‖ ℋ = o ​ ( t ) . \big\|\Phi(P_{t})-\Phi(P)-tD\Phi_{P}(s)\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}=o(t). 
 (16) 
 The operator D ​ Φ P D\Phi_{P} is called the local parameter of Φ \Phi at P P . Its image is a closed subspace of ℋ \mathcal{H} , denoted by ℋ ˙ P \dot{\mathcal{H}}_{P} , and referred to as the local parameter space.
The Hermitian adjoint of D ​ Φ P D\Phi_{P} , denoted by D ∗ ​ Φ P : ℋ → 𝒫 ˙ P D^{*}\Phi_{P}:\mathcal{H}\to\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{P} , is called the efficient influence operator . The operators D ​ Φ P D\Phi_{P} and D ∗ ​ Φ P D^{*}\Phi_{P} satisfy the adjoint relationship ⟨ h , D ​ Φ P ​ ( s ) ⟩ ℋ = ⟨ D ∗ ​ Φ P ​ ( h ) , s ⟩ L 2 ​ ( P ) \left\langle h,D\Phi_{P}(s)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\left\langle D^{*}\Phi_{P}(h),s\right\rangle_{L^{2}(P)} for all h ∈ ℋ , s ∈ 𝒫 ˙ P h\in\mathcal{H},\;s\in\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{P} .
We say that Φ \Phi admits an efficient influence function (EIF) at P P if, for P P -almost every z ∈ 𝒵 z\in\mathcal{Z} , the map h ↦ D ∗ ​ Φ P ​ ( h ) ​ ( z ) h\mapsto D^{*}\Phi_{P}(h)(z) defines a bounded linear functional on ℋ \mathcal{H} . In this case, by the Riesz representation theorem, there exists a function ϕ P : 𝒵 → ℋ \phi_{P}:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{H} such that 
 D ∗ ​ Φ P ​ ( h ) ​ ( z ) = ⟨ h , ϕ P ​ ( z ) ⟩ ℋ , for all  ​ h ∈ ℋ ​  and  ​ P ​ -a.e.  ​ z . D^{*}\Phi_{P}(h)(z)=\left\langle h,\phi_{P}(z)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},\quad\text{for all }h\in\mathcal{H}\text{ and }P\text{-a.e. }z. 
 (17) 
 Sufficient conditions for the existence of the EIF when ℋ \mathcal{H} and ℋ ˙ P \dot{\mathcal{H}}_{P} are RKHS are given in (Luedtke and Chung, 2024 , Theorem 1) . In the special case of real-valued parameters, ℋ = ℝ \mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R} , a function ϕ P : 𝒵 → ℝ \phi_{P}:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R} is an influence function of Φ \Phi at P P if the local parameter admits the representation 
 D ​ Φ P ​ ( s ) = ⟨ ϕ P , s ⟩ L 2 ​ ( P ) . D\Phi_{P}(s)=\left\langle\phi_{P},s\right\rangle_{L^{2}(P)}. 
 The efficient influence function is the unique influence function that lies in the tangent space 𝒫 ˙ P \dot{\mathcal{P}}_{P} , and can therefore be obtained as the orthogonal projection of any influence function onto 𝒫 ˙ P \dot{\mathcal{P}}_{P} . For fully nonparametric models, 𝒫 ˙ P = L 0 2 ​ ( P ) \dot{\mathcal{P}}_{P}=L_{0}^{2}(P) , so the EIF reduces to ϕ P − P ​ ϕ P \phi_{P}-P\phi_{P} (Bickel et al. , 1993 ) . 
 Second-order scores and influence functions 
 Again, we consider a smooth one-dimensional parametric submodel ( P t : t ∈ [ 0 , δ ) ) ⊂ 𝒫 (P_{t}:t\in[0,\delta))\subset\mathcal{P} through P 0 = P P_{0}=P , with corresponding densities p t p_{t} with respect to a common dominating measure. For a general treatment of higher-order scores and influence functions in r r -dimensional submodels ( r ≥ 1 r\geq 1 ), see Robins et al. ( 2008 , Sec. 2) . 
 Let s : 𝒵 → ℝ s:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R} denote the first-order score function s ​ ( z ) = ∂ t ∂ t ​ p t ​ ( z ) | t = 0 s(z)=\tfrac{\partial t}{\partial t}p_{t}(z)\big|_{t=0} . The second-order score function (Waterman and Lindsay, 1996 ; Small and McLeish, 2011 ; van der Vaart, 2014 ) r : 𝒵 × 𝒵 → ℝ r:\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R} is defined through the perturbation of the product measure P t ⊗ 2 = P t ⊗ P t P_{t}^{\otimes 2}=P_{t}\otimes P_{t} . Specifically, it is the second derivative of the joint density relative to the baseline product measure P 2 P^{2} .
A direct calculation is presented below 
 r ​ ( z 1 , z 2 ) \displaystyle r(z_{1},z_{2}) 
 = 1 2 ​ p ​ ( z 1 ) ​ p ​ ( z 2 ) ​ ( d 2 d ​ t 2 ​ ( p t ​ ( z 1 ) ​ p t ​ ( z 2 ) ) ) | t = 0 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2p(z_{1})p(z_{2})}\left(\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\left(p_{t}(z_{1})p_{t}(z_{2})\right)\right)\Big|_{t=0} 
 = 1 2 ​ p ​ ( z 1 ) ​ d 2 ​ p t ​ ( z 1 ) d ​ t 2 | t = 0 + 1 2 ​ p ​ ( z 2 ) ​ d 2 ​ p t ​ ( z 2 ) d ​ t 2 | t = 0 + s ​ ( z 1 ) ​ s ​ ( z 2 ) . \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2p(z_{1})}\frac{d^{2}p_{t}(z_{1})}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}+\frac{1}{2p(z_{2})}\frac{d^{2}p_{t}(z_{2})}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}+s(z_{1})s(z_{2}). 
 Thus, unlike the first-order score, the second-order score is not simply the second derivative of the univariate log-likelihood; rather, it arises from the second-order perturbation of the product measure. In particular, for a linear path P t = ( 1 + t ​ s ) ​ P P_{t}=(1+ts)P , this reduces to the product of scores: r ​ ( z 1 , z 2 ) = s ​ ( z 1 ) ​ s ​ ( z 2 ) r(z_{1},z_{2})=s(z_{1})s(z_{2}) . 
 The second-order influence function of Φ \Phi at P P is a symmetric measurable map Φ ¨ P ∈ L 0 2 ​ ( P ⊗ 2 ) \ddot{\Phi}_{P}\in L_{0}^{2}(P^{\otimes 2}) such that 𝔼 P ​ [ Φ ¨ P ] = 0 \mathbb{E}_{P}[\ddot{\Phi}_{P}]=0 and the following holds 
 d d ​ t ​ Φ ​ ( P t ) | t = 0 \displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Phi(P_{t})\Big|_{t=0} 
 = ∬ Φ ¨ P ​ ( z 1 , z 2 ) ​ ( s ​ ( z 1 ) + s ​ ( z 2 ) ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( z 1 ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( z 2 ) , and \displaystyle=\iint\ddot{\Phi}_{P}(z_{1},z_{2})(s(z_{1})+s(z_{2}))\,dP(z_{1})\,dP(z_{2}),\quad\textrm{and } 
 d 2 d ​ t 2 ​ Φ ​ ( P t ) | t = 0 \displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Phi(P_{t})\Big|_{t=0} 
 = ∬ Φ ¨ P ​ ( z 1 , z 2 ) ​ r ​ ( z 1 , z 2 ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( z 1 ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( z 2 ) . \displaystyle=\iint\ddot{\Phi}_{P}(z_{1},z_{2})\,r(z_{1},z_{2})\,dP(z_{1})\,dP(z_{2}). 
 (18) 
 Our presentation of second-order pathwise differentiability differs slightly from that of Robins et al. ( 2008 ) , in that we carry an extra factor of 1 / 2 1/2 in our expression for r r and 2 2 in our expression for the second-order part of Φ ¨ P \ddot{\Phi}_{P} . Concretely, in the notation of Robins et al. ( 2008 ) , the second-order influence function of Φ \Phi at P P would be Γ ​ ( Φ ¨ P ) \Gamma(\ddot{\Phi}_{P}) , where Γ : L 0 2 ​ ( P ⊗ 2 ) → L 0 2 ​ ( P ⊗ 2 ) \Gamma:L_{0}^{2}(P^{\otimes 2})\to L_{0}^{2}(P^{\otimes 2}) is the bijective map defined by 
 Γ ​ ( f ) : ( z 1 , z 2 ) ↦ ∫ [ f ​ ( z 1 , z ) + f ​ ( z , z 2 ) ] ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( z ) + 1 2 ​ [ f ​ ( z , z 2 ) − ∫ [ f ​ ( z 1 , z ) + f ​ ( z , z 2 ) ] ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( z ) ] . \displaystyle\Gamma(f):(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto\int[f(z_{1},z)+f(z,z_{2})]dP(z)+\frac{1}{2}\left[f(z,z_{2})-\int[f(z_{1},z)+f(z,z_{2})]dP(z)\right]. 
 In the case where the first-order EIF is 0 a.s., the first-order part of Φ ¨ P \ddot{\Phi}_{P} is also 0 , and so our second-order EIF is twice that from Robins et al. ( 2008 , Def. 2.1) , that is, Φ ¨ P = 2 ​ Γ ​ ( Φ ¨ P ) \ddot{\Phi}_{P}=2\Gamma(\ddot{\Phi}_{P}) . 
 B.2 Entropic Optimal Transport 
 We briefly review entropic optimal transport. We refer to Léonard ( 2013 ) and Cuturi ( 2013 ) for comprehensive treatments. Throughout, we work on the space of Borel probability measures 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) defined on the Polish space ( 𝒴 , d 𝒴 ) (\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}}) . 
 Given μ , ν ∈ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) , the Kantorovich formulation of the optimal transport problem (Kantorovich, 2006 ; Villani, 2009 ) with quadratic cost is 
 OT ​ ( μ , ν ) = inf π ∈ Π ​ ( μ , ν ) ∫ ‖ y 1 − y 2 ‖ 2 2 ​ 𝑑 π ​ ( y 1 , y 2 ) , \text{OT}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\int\frac{\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|^{2}}{2}d\pi(y_{1},y_{2}), 
 (19) 
 where Π ​ ( μ , ν ) \Pi(\mu,\nu) denotes the set of couplings of μ \mu and ν \nu , that is joint distributions on 𝒴 × 𝒴 \mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y} with marginals μ \mu and ν \nu .
Despite its appealing geometric properties, classical OT poses significant computational and statistical challenges. In particular, the empirical plug-in estimator converges at the slow rate n − 1 / d n^{-1/d} in dimension d d , reflecting the curse of dimensionality (Fournier and Guillin, 2015 ; Manole and Niles-Weed, 2024 ) . Entropic optimal transport addresses these difficulties by introducing a Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence-based regularization term. For ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 regularization, the EOT problem is defined as 
 OT ε ​ ( μ , ν ) = inf π ∈ Π ​ ( μ , ν ) ∫ ‖ y 1 − y 2 ‖ 2 2 ​ 𝑑 π ​ ( y 1 , y 2 ) + ε ​ KL ​ ( π ∣ μ ⊗ ν ) , \text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\int\frac{\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|^{2}}{2}d\pi(y_{1},y_{2})+\varepsilon\mathrm{KL}(\pi\mid\mu\otimes\nu), 
 (20) 
 where KL ( ⋅ ∣ ⋅ ) \mathrm{KL}(\cdot\mid\cdot) denotes the KL divergence. The regularized problem admits efficient solutions via the Sinkhorn algorithm, whose computational complexity scales quadratically in the sample size (Cuturi, 2013 ) .
The EOT problem enjoys strong duality, with dual formulation 
 OT ε ​ ( μ , ν ) = sup ( φ 1 , φ 0 ) ∈ L 1 ​ ( μ ) × L 1 ​ ( ν ) [ ∫ φ 1 ​ 𝑑 μ + ∫ φ 0 ​ 𝑑 ν − ε ​ ∫ exp ⁡ ( φ 1 ⊕ φ 0 − ∥ ⋅ − ⋅ ∥ 2 / 2 ε ) ​ d ​ ( μ ⊗ ν ) + ε ] , \text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)=\sup_{(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{0})\in L^{1}(\mu)\times L^{1}(\nu)}\left[\int\varphi_{1}\,d\mu+\int\varphi_{0}\,d\nu-\varepsilon\int\exp\left(\frac{\varphi_{1}\oplus\varphi_{0}-\|\cdot-\cdot\|^{2}/2}{\varepsilon}\right)d(\mu\otimes\nu)+\varepsilon\right], 
 (21) 
 where ( φ 1 ⊕ φ 0 ) ​ ( y , y ′ ) = φ 1 ​ ( y ) + φ 0 ​ ( y ′ ) (\varphi_{1}\oplus\varphi_{0})(y,y^{\prime})=\varphi_{1}(y)+\varphi_{0}(y^{\prime}) . The dual maximizers ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) , φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) (\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)},\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}) , known as entropic potentials, satisfy the Schrödinger system 
 φ 1 ( μ , ν ) \displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)} 
 = − ε log ( ∫ exp ( 1 ε ( φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ( y ′ ) − ∥ ⋅ − y ′ ∥ 2 / 2 ) d ν ( y ′ ) ) ) , \displaystyle=-\varepsilon\log\left(\int\exp\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}(y^{\prime})-\|\cdot-y^{\prime}\|^{2}/2\right)\,d\nu(y^{\prime})\right)\right), 
 φ 0 ( μ , ν ) \displaystyle\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)} 
 = − ε log ( ∫ exp ( 1 ε ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) ( y ) − ∥ y − ⋅ ∥ 2 / 2 ) d μ ( y ) ) ) . \displaystyle=-\varepsilon\log\left(\int\exp\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}(y)-\|y-\cdot\|^{2}/2\right)\,d\mu(y)\right)\right). 
 (22) 
 Note that ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) , φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) (\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)},\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}) are unique up to an additive constant, meaning that if ( φ ~ 1 ( μ , ν ) , φ ~ 2 ( μ , ν ) ) (\tilde{\varphi}_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)},\tilde{\varphi}_{2}^{(\mu,\nu)}) is another pair of entropic potentials satisfying ( B.2 ), there exists a constant c > 0 c>0 such that ( φ ~ 1 ( μ , ν ) , φ ~ 2 ( μ , ν ) ) ≡ ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) + c , φ 0 ( μ , ν ) − c ) (\tilde{\varphi}_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)},\tilde{\varphi}_{2}^{(\mu,\nu)})\equiv(\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}+c,\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}-c) . Following Luise et al. ( 2019 ) , we define unique potentials ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) , φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) (\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)},\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}) such that—at an arbitrarily chosen reference point y 0 ∈ 𝒴 y_{0}\in\mathcal{Y} — φ 1 ( μ , ν ) ​ ( y 0 ) = 0 \varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}(y_{0})=0 . 
 A limitation of EOT is that OT ε ​ ( μ , μ ) ≠ 0 \text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\mu\right)\neq 0 , which precludes its direct use as a discrepancy measure. To remove this bias, Ramdas et al. ( 2017 ) and Feydy et al. ( 2019 ) introduced the Sinkhorn divergence 
 S ε ​ ( μ , ν ) = OT ε ​ ( μ , ν ) − 1 2 ​ OT ε ​ ( μ , μ ) − 𝟙 2 ​ OT ε ​ ( ν , ν ) . {S}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)=\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)-\frac{1}{2}\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\mu\right)-\frac{\mathbbm{1}}{2}\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\nu,\,\nu\right). 
 Appendix C Hadamard Differentiability of EOT 
 C.1 Setup 
 In this section, we present the results on the Hadamard differentiability of EOT objects, specifically, the entropic potentials and the EOT cost, when viewed as mappings on the Banach space ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) . As discussed in Sec.   3 , the map J J allows us to identify each probability measure in 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) with a unique element of ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) . Working with this representation, we equip the space of measures with the product norm 
 ‖ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) ‖ ∞ := ‖ γ 1 ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) ∨ ‖ γ 2 ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) , ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) ∈ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) , \left\|(\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2})\right\|_{\infty}:=\left\|\gamma^{1}\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}\lor\left\|\gamma^{2}\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})},\quad(\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2})\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}), 
 Related differentiability results have been established in closely related normed spaces. In particular, on ℓ ∞ ​ ( B s ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( B s ) \ell^{\infty}(B^{s})\times\ell^{\infty}(B^{s}) , where B s = { f ∈ 𝒞 s ​ ( 𝒴 ) : ‖ f ‖ 𝒞 s ​ ( 𝒴 ) ≤ 1 } B^{s}=\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{Y}):\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{Y})}\leq 1\} denotes the unit ball of the s s -Hölder space 𝒞 s ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{Y}) , Goldfeld et al. ( 2024 ) proved first- and second-order Hadamard differentiability for the entropic potentials and the Sinkhorn divergence. More recently, Kokot and Luedtke ( 2025 ) derived an explicit expression for the first-order Hadamard derivative of the entropic potentials and for the second-order derivative of the Sinkhorn divergence under the null in the Gaussian RKHS topology. In this section, we extend these Hadamard differentiability results for the relevant EOT objects to the Sobolev RKHS topology. 
 Let μ ∈ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) be a reference measure, and define 𝒫 μ \mathcal{P}_{\mu} at μ \mu by 
 𝒫 μ = { ν ∈ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) : supp ​ ( ν ) ⊂ supp ​ ( μ ) } . \mathcal{P}_{\mu}=\left\{\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}):\text{supp}(\nu)\subset\text{supp}(\mu)\right\}. 
 We define the tangent cone to 𝒫 μ \mathcal{P}_{\mu} at μ \mu by 
 ℳ 0 , μ = { t ​ ( ν − μ ) : ν ∈ 𝒫 μ , t > 0 } ¯ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) . \mathcal{M}_{0,\mu}=\overline{\left\{t(\nu-\mu):\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{\mu},t>0\right\}}^{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}. 
 Tangent cone represents the collection of all admissible first-order perturbation directions at μ \mu induced by paths remaining in 𝒫 μ \mathcal{P}_{\mu} , with the closure taken in ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) topology. Hadamard differentiability at μ \mu is therefore understood with respect to perturbations along paths ( μ t : t ∈ ℝ ) ⊂ 𝒫 μ (\mu_{t}:t\in\mathbb{R})\subset\mathcal{P}_{\mu} , whose first-order increments lie in ℳ 0 , μ \mathcal{M}_{0,\mu} . 
 C.2 Sinkhorn Hadamard operator 
 Fix μ ∈ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) and recall the operators H μ H_{\mu} and T μ T_{\mu} defined in ( 9 ). Since T μ ​ 𝟙 𝒴 = 𝟙 𝒴 T_{\mu}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Y}} , the operator T μ T_{\mu} induces a well-defined operator on the quotient space 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R} , still denoted by T μ : 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ T_{\mu}:\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R} for convenience. Because ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y}) is canonically dual to 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R} , the Sinkhorn operator is defined by 
 K μ : ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ , K μ ​ γ = ε ​ ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 ​ [ H μ ​ γ ] . K_{\mu}:\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R},\quad K_{\mu}\gamma=\varepsilon(I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1}[H_{\mu}\gamma]. 
 This expression is well-defined because, by Lavenant et al. ( 2024 , Thm. 3.8) , the inverse ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 (I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1} exists as a bounded operator on 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R} . Consequently, for any γ 1 , γ 2 ∈ ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y}) , the evaluation of the Sinkhorn Hadamard operator B μ ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) := ⟨ γ 1 , K μ ​ γ 2 ⟩ B_{\mu}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}):=\left\langle\gamma_{1},K_{\mu}\gamma_{2}\right\rangle is well-defined by duality between ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y}) and 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R} . 
 Moreover, the quotient space 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R} is isometrically isomorphic to the closed subspace of 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) consisting of functions orthogonal to constants (equivalently, functions with zero constant component). For any γ ∈ ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y}) , the function H μ ​ γ H_{\mu}\gamma has zero constant component, hence belongs to this subspace and canonically represents its equivalence class [ H μ ​ γ ] ∈ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ [H_{\mu}\gamma]\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R} . Accordingly, when it will not cause confusion, we write ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 ​ H μ ​ γ (I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1}H_{\mu}\gamma instead of ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 ​ [ H μ ​ γ ] (I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1}[H_{\mu}\gamma] . 
 Following Lavenant et al. ( 2024 ) , we now refine this construction in the RKHS setting.
Let ℋ μ \mathcal{H}_{\mu} denote the RKHS associated with the self-transport kernel ξ μ \xi_{\mu} . The RKHS is well-defined because ξ μ \xi_{\mu} is continuous, symmetric, and positive definite.
By construction, both H μ H_{\mu} and T μ T_{\mu} map into ℋ μ \mathcal{H}_{\mu} . Since constant functions lie in ℋ μ \mathcal{H}_{\mu} and satisfy T μ ​ 𝟙 𝒴 = 𝟙 𝒴 T_{\mu}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Y}} , the operator T μ T_{\mu} preserves constants and therefore induces a well-defined operator on the quotient space T μ : ℋ μ / ℝ → ℋ μ / ℝ T_{\mu}:\mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R} .
Similarly, because H μ ​ γ : ℳ ​ ( 𝒴 ) → ℋ μ H_{\mu}\gamma:\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{H}_{\mu} is injective and has no constant component when γ ∈ ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y}) , the map [ H μ ​ ( ⋅ ) ] : ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) → ℋ μ / ℝ [H_{\mu}(\cdot)]:\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R} is also injective; for brevity, we will simply denote this map as H μ : ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) → ℋ μ / ℝ H_{\mu}:\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R} when clear from context.
The quotient space ℋ μ / ℝ \mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R} , equipped with the norm ‖ [ h ] ‖ ℋ μ / ℝ = inf λ ∈ ℝ ‖ h − λ ​ 𝟙 𝒴 ‖ ℋ μ \|[h]\|_{{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R}}}=\inf_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}\|h-\lambda\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}} , is isometrically isomorphic to the closed subspace 𝟏 𝒴 ⟂ := { h ∈ ℋ μ : ⟨ h , 𝟙 𝒴 ⟩ ℋ μ = 0 } {\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{Y}}}^{\perp}:=\{h\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu}:\left\langle h,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Y}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}}=0\} .
Hence ℋ μ / ℝ \mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R} is itself a Hilbert space.
The spectrum of T μ : ℋ μ / ℝ → ℋ μ / ℝ T_{\mu}:\mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R} is contained in [ 0 , q ] [0,q] for some q < 1 q<1 , and so the spectrum of ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 (I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1} is contained in [ ε , ε / ( 1 − q 2 ) ] [\varepsilon,\varepsilon/(1-q^{2})] (Lavenant et al. , 2024 , Thm. 4.3) . 
 We now derive a kernel representation of B μ ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) B_{\mu}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}) in terms of the inner product in the Hilbert space ℋ μ / ℝ \mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R} . 
 Lemma C.1 . 
 The kernel k μ : 𝒴 × 𝒴 → ℝ k_{\mu}:\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R} , defined so
