 is symmetric positive semi-definite and, for any γ 1 , γ 2 ∈ ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y}) , satisfies 
 B μ ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) = ∫ 𝒴 ∫ 𝒴 k μ ​ ( y , y ′ ) ​ 𝑑 γ 1 ​ ( y ) ​ 𝑑 γ 2 ​ ( d ​ y ′ ) . B_{\mu}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})=\int_{\mathcal{Y}}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}k_{\mu}(y,y^{\prime})\,d\gamma_{1}(y)\,d\gamma_{2}(dy^{\prime}). 
 A kernel representation satisfying the above display is not unique; in particular, B μ ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) = ∫ ∫ [ k μ ​ ( y , y ′ ) + a ​ ( y ) + b ​ ( y ′ ) ] ​ 𝑑 γ 1 ​ ( y ) ​ 𝑑 γ 2 ​ ( d ​ y ′ ) B_{\mu}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})=\int\int[k_{\mu}(y,y^{\prime})+a(y)+b(y^{\prime})]\,d\gamma_{1}(y)\,d\gamma_{2}(dy^{\prime}) for any measurable functions a , b : 𝒴 → ℝ a,b:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R} . 
 Proof. 
 With a slight abuse of notation, we will use K μ ​ γ 2 K_{\mu}\gamma_{2} to denote its representative element in 𝟏 𝒴 ⟂ ⊂ ℋ μ \mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\perp}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\mu} . Then, B μ ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) = ∫ K μ ​ γ 2 ​ 𝑑 γ 1 B_{\mu}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})=\int K_{\mu}\gamma_{2}\,d\gamma_{1} . Since H μ ​ γ 1 H_{\mu}\gamma_{1} is a kernel mean embedding, ∫ K μ ​ γ 2 ​ 𝑑 γ 1 = ⟨ H μ ​ γ 1 , K μ ​ γ 2 ⟩ ℋ μ \int K_{\mu}\gamma_{2}\,d\gamma_{1}=\left\langle H_{\mu}\gamma_{1},K_{\mu}\gamma_{2}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}} . Because H μ ​ γ 1 , K μ ​ γ 2 ∈ 𝟏 𝒴 ⟂ H_{\mu}\gamma_{1},K_{\mu}\gamma_{2}\in\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\perp} , we have that 
 B ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) = ⟨ H μ ​ γ 1 , K μ ​ γ 2 ⟩ ℋ μ = ⟨ [ H μ ​ γ 1 ] , [ K μ ​ γ 2 ] ⟩ ℋ μ / ℝ . B(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})=\left\langle H_{\mu}\gamma_{1},K_{\mu}\gamma_{2}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}}=\left\langle[H_{\mu}\gamma_{1}],[K_{\mu}\gamma_{2}]\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R}}. 
 Since ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 (I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1} is strictly positive, ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 / 2 (I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1/2} exists on ℋ μ / ℝ \mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R} . This and its self-adjointness (Lavenant et al. , 2024 , Prop. 4.2) give 
 B μ ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) \displaystyle B_{\mu}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}) 
 = ⟨ [ H μ ​ γ 1 ] , ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 ​ [ H μ ​ γ 2 ] ⟩ ℋ μ / ℝ = ⟨ ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 / 2 ​ [ H μ ​ γ 1 ] , ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 / 2 ​ [ H μ ​ γ 2 ] ⟩ ℋ μ / ℝ . \displaystyle=\left\langle[H_{\mu}\gamma_{1}],(I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1}[H_{\mu}\gamma_{2}]\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R}}=\left\langle(I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1/2}[H_{\mu}\gamma_{1}],(I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1/2}[H_{\mu}\gamma_{2}]\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R}}. 
 Let Q : ℋ μ → ℋ μ / ℝ Q:\mathcal{H}_{\mu}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R} be the standard quotient map such that Q : h ↦ [ h ] Q:h\mapsto[h] . Since Q Q is a bounded linear map, it commutes with Bochner integration. That is for any Bochner integrable ℋ μ \mathcal{H}_{\mu} -valued function h h , Q ​ ( ∫ h ​ 𝑑 γ ) = ∫ Q ​ ( h ) ​ 𝑑 γ Q(\int h\,d\gamma)=\int Q(h)\,d\gamma . The boundedness of ξ μ \xi_{\mu} on compact 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} implies Bochner integrability of h = ξ μ h=\xi_{\mu} , and then we have that [ H μ ​ γ i ] = [ ∫ ξ μ ​ ( ⋅ , y ) ​ 𝑑 γ i ​ ( y ) ] = ∫ [ ξ μ ​ ( ⋅ , y ) ] ​ 𝑑 γ i ​ ( y ) [H_{\mu}\gamma_{i}]=[\int\xi_{\mu}(\cdot,y)\,d\gamma_{i}(y)]=\int[\xi_{\mu}(\cdot,y)]\,d\gamma_{i}(y) for i ∈ { 1 , 2 } i\in\{1,2\} . Since ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 (I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1} is a bounded linear operator on ℋ μ / ℝ \mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R} (Lavenant et al. , 2024 , Thm. 4.3) , we can exchange the operator ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 / 2 (I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1/2} and this gives 
 ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 / 2 ​ [ H μ ​ γ ] = ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 / 2 ​ ∫ 𝒴 [ ξ μ ​ ( ⋅ , y ) ] ​ d ​ γ ​ ( y ) = ∫ 𝒴 ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 / 2 ​ [ ξ μ ​ ( ⋅ , y ) ] ​ d ​ γ ​ ( y ) . (I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1/2}[H_{\mu}\gamma]=(I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1/2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\limits_{\kern-5.0pt\mathcal{Y}}\kern-0.99998pt}{\textstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-4.00002pt\mathcal{Y}}}{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27779pt\mathcal{Y}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-2.94449pt\mathcal{Y}}}[\xi_{\mu}(\cdot,y)]\,d\gamma(y)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\limits_{\kern-5.0pt\mathcal{Y}}\kern-0.99998pt}{\textstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-4.00002pt\mathcal{Y}}}{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27779pt\mathcal{Y}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-2.94449pt\mathcal{Y}}}(I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1/2}[\xi_{\mu}(\cdot,y)]\,d\gamma(y). 
 Using the above, and the bilinearity and continuity of inner products, we have 
 B μ ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) \displaystyle B_{\mu}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}) 
 = ⟨ ∫ 𝒴 ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 / 2 ​ [ ξ μ ​ ( y 1 , ⋅ ) ] ​ d ​ γ 1 ​ ( y 1 ) , ∫ 𝒴 ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 / 2 ​ [ ξ μ ​ ( y 2 , ⋅ ) ] ​ d ​ γ 2 ​ ( y 2 ) ⟩ ℋ μ / ℝ \displaystyle=\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\limits_{\kern-5.0pt\mathcal{Y}}\kern-0.99998pt}{\textstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-4.00002pt\mathcal{Y}}}{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27779pt\mathcal{Y}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-2.94449pt\mathcal{Y}}}(I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1/2}[\xi_{\mu}(y_{1},\cdot)]d\gamma_{1}(y_{1}),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\limits_{\kern-5.0pt\mathcal{Y}}\kern-0.99998pt}{\textstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-4.00002pt\mathcal{Y}}}{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27779pt\mathcal{Y}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-2.94449pt\mathcal{Y}}}(I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1/2}[\xi_{\mu}(y_{2},\cdot)]d\gamma_{2}(y_{2})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R}} 
 = ∫ 𝒴 ∫ 𝒴 ⟨ ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 / 2 ​ [ ξ μ ​ ( y 1 , ⋅ ) ] , ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 / 2 ​ [ ξ μ ​ ( y 2 , ⋅ ) ] ⟩ ℋ μ / ℝ ​ d ​ γ 1 ​ ( y 1 ) ​ d ​ γ 2 ​ ( y 2 ) \displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\limits_{\kern-5.0pt\mathcal{Y}}\kern-0.99998pt}{\textstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-4.00002pt\mathcal{Y}}}{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27779pt\mathcal{Y}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-2.94449pt\mathcal{Y}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\limits_{\kern-5.0pt\mathcal{Y}}\kern-0.99998pt}{\textstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-4.00002pt\mathcal{Y}}}{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27779pt\mathcal{Y}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-2.94449pt\mathcal{Y}}}\left\langle(I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1/2}[\xi_{\mu}(y_{1},\cdot)],(I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1/2}[\xi_{\mu}(y_{2},\cdot)]\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R}}d\gamma_{1}(y_{1})\,d\gamma_{2}(y_{2}) 
 = : ∫ 𝒴 ∫ 𝒴 k μ ( y 1 , y 2 ) d γ 1 ( y 1 ) d γ 2 ( y 2 ) . \displaystyle=:\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\limits_{\kern-5.0pt\mathcal{Y}}\kern-0.99998pt}{\textstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-4.00002pt\mathcal{Y}}}{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27779pt\mathcal{Y}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-2.94449pt\mathcal{Y}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\limits_{\kern-5.0pt\mathcal{Y}}\kern-0.99998pt}{\textstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-4.00002pt\mathcal{Y}}}{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-3.27779pt\mathcal{Y}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits_{\kern-2.94449pt\mathcal{Y}}}k_{\mu}(y_{1},y_{2})\,d\gamma_{1}(y_{1})\,d\gamma_{2}(y_{2}). 
 Symmetry of k μ k_{\mu} follows by the symmetry of inner products. To see that the kernel k μ k_{\mu} is positive semi-definite, note that for every choice of points y 1 , … , y m ∈ 𝒴 y_{1},\dots,y_{m}\in\mathcal{Y} and vector ( c 1 , … , c m ) ∈ ℝ m (c_{1},\dots,c_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m} , 
 ∑ i , j c i ​ c j ​ k μ ​ ( y i , y j ) \displaystyle\sum_{i,j}c_{i}c_{j}k_{\mu}(y_{i},y_{j}) 
 = ∑ i , j c i ​ c j ​ ⟨ ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 / 2 ​ [ ξ μ ​ ( y i , ⋅ ) ] , ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 / 2 ​ [ ξ μ ​ ( y j , ⋅ ) ] ⟩ ℋ μ / ℝ \displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}c_{i}c_{j}\left\langle(I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1/2}[\xi_{\mu}(y_{i},\cdot)],(I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1/2}[\xi_{\mu}(y_{j},\cdot)]\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R}} 
 = ‖ ∑ i c i ​ ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 / 2 ​ [ ξ μ ​ ( y i , ⋅ ) ] ‖ ℋ μ / ℝ 2 ≥ 0 . \displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{i}c_{i}\,(I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1/2}[\xi_{\mu}(y_{i},\cdot)]\right\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}/\mathbb{R}}^{2}\geq 0. 
 ∎ 
 C.3 Hadamard differentiability of EOT potentials and divergence 
 Now we are ready to present the Hadamard differentiability results of entropic potentials and Sinkhorn divergence in the ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) topology. First, we characterize some properties of the entropic potentials without proof. 
 Lemma C.2 (Properties of entropic potentials) . 
 Recalling that the cost function c c is 𝒞 ∞ \mathcal{C}^{\infty} and 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} is compact subset of ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} , for every ( μ , ν ) ∈ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) (\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) , 
 1. 
 Genevay et al. ( 2019 , Thm. 2) : The entropic potentials ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) , φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) \big({\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)},\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}}\big) are uniformly bounded in ℋ \mathcal{H} , and their norms satisfy ‖ φ i ( μ , ν ) ‖ ℋ ≤ 𝒪 ​ ( 1 + ε − s + 1 ) \big\|{\varphi_{i}^{(\mu,\nu)}}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(1+\varepsilon^{-s+1}\right) for i ∈ { 1 , 0 } i\in\{1,0\} , and the constant depends on d d and | 𝒴 | \left|\mathcal{Y}\right| . 
 2. 
 Goldfeld et al. ( 2024 , Lem. 1) : The map ( μ , ν ) ↦ ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) , φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) (\mu,\nu)\mapsto\big({\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)},\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}}\big) is continuous relative to the topology of weak convergence, that is if ( μ n , ν n ) (\mu_{n},\nu_{n}) converges to ( μ , ν ) (\mu,\nu) weakly as n → ∞ n\to\infty , then ( φ 1 ( μ n , ν n ) , φ 0 ( μ n , ν n ) ) → ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) , φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) \big({\varphi_{1}^{(\mu_{n},\nu_{n})},\varphi_{0}^{(\mu_{n},\nu_{n})}}\big)\to\big({\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)},\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}}\big) in ℋ × ℋ \mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H} . 
 The second claim of Lem.   C.2 follows directly from a similar continuity of entropic potentials in the C k ​ ( 𝒴 ) C^{k}(\mathcal{Y}) norm for any arbitrary k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} (Goldfeld et al. , 2024 , Lem. 1) because the Sobolev norm can be upperbounded by the C s ​ ( 𝒴 ) C^{s}(\mathcal{Y}) norm for the compact domain. 
 Lemma C.3 (Sobolev RKHS properties) . 
 The following properties hold for Sobolev RKHS ℋ = H s ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{H}=H^{s}(\mathcal{Y}) for s > d / 2 s>d/2 : 
 1. 
 For every smooth function F : ℝ → ℝ F:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R} such that F ​ ( 0 ) = 0 F(0)=0 , there exists a non-decreasing function C F : ℝ + → ℝ + C_{F}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{+} such that 
 ‖ F ​ ( h ) ‖ ℋ ≤ C F ​ ( ‖ h ‖ L ∞ ​ ( 𝒴 ) ) ​ ‖ h ‖ ℋ . \|F(h)\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C_{F}\left(\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y})}\right)\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}. 
 2. 
 For all h 1 , h 2 ∈ ℋ h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathcal{H} , the pointwise product h 1 ​ h 2 h_{1}h_{2} belongs to ℋ \mathcal{H} (defined a.e. in 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} ). Further, there exists a constant K 2 ≡ K 2 ​ ( s , d , | 𝒴 | ) K_{2}\equiv K_{2}(s,d,|\mathcal{Y}|) such that 
 ‖ h 1 ​ h 2 ‖ ℋ ≤ K 2 ​ ‖ h 1 ‖ ℋ ​ ‖ h 2 ‖ ℋ . \|h_{1}h_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq K_{2}\|h_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\|h_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}. 
 3. 
 For h ∈ ℋ h\in\mathcal{H} and γ ∈ ℳ ​ ( 𝒴 ) \gamma\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{Y}) , define T γ ​ h : y ↦ ∫ 𝒴 e − ‖ y − y ′ ‖ 2 / 2 ​ ε ​ h ​ ( y ′ ) ​ 𝑑 γ ​ ( y ′ ) T_{\gamma}h:y\mapsto\int_{\mathcal{Y}}e^{-\|y-y^{\prime}\|^{2}/2\varepsilon}\,h(y^{\prime})\,d\gamma(y^{\prime}) . Then for all h , γ h,\gamma , it holds that T γ ​ h ∈ ℋ T_{\gamma}h\in\mathcal{H} and moreover, there exists a constant K 3 ≡ K 3 ​ ( s , d , | 𝒴 | ) K_{3}\equiv K_{3}(s,d,|\mathcal{Y}|) such that 
 ‖ T γ ​ h ‖ ℋ ≤ K 3 ​ ‖ h ‖ ℋ ​ ‖ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ ) . \left\|T_{\gamma}h\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq K_{3}\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}\|\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H})}. 
 Proof. 
 The first result follows from Sobolev inequalities (Gagliardo–Nirenberg–Moser estimates) (Gagliardo, 1959 ; Nirenberg, 1959 ; Moser, 1966 ) for composition. Since s > d / 2 s>d/2 , H s ​ ( 𝒴 ) ⊂ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) H^{s}(\mathcal{Y})\subset\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) (Taylor, 1996 , Chap. 4, Prop. 1.3) . Therefore, all h ∈ ℋ h\in\mathcal{H} are continuous and hence bounded on 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} , i.e. h ∈ L ∞ ​ ( 𝒴 ) h\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y}) . For every smooth function F : ℝ → ℝ F:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R} such that F ​ ( 0 ) = 0 F(0)=0 , there exists a non-decreasing function C F : ℝ + → ℝ + C_{F}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{+} such that ‖ F ​ ( h ) ‖ ℋ ≤ C F ​ ( ‖ h ‖ L ∞ ​ ( 𝒴 ) ) ​ ‖ h ‖ ℋ \|F(h)\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C_{F}(\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y})})\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}} (Taylor and others, 1996 , Chap. 13, Prop. 3.9) . 
 Because we chose s > d / 2 s>d/2 , ℋ \mathcal{H} is a Banach algebra (Adams and Fournier, 2003 , Thm. 4.39) , which implies that for any h 1 , h 2 ∈ ℋ h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathcal{H} , the pointwise product h 1 ​ h 2 h_{1}h_{2} (defined a.e. in 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} ) belongs to ℋ \mathcal{H} . Further, there exists a constant K 2 ≡ K 2 ​ ( s , d , 𝒴 ) K_{2}\equiv K_{2}(s,d,\mathcal{Y}) , such that ‖ h 1 ​ h 2 ‖ ℋ ≤ K 2 ​ ‖ h 1 ‖ ℋ ​ ‖ h 2 ‖ ℋ \|h_{1}h_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq K_{2}\|h_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\|h_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}} . 
 For the third result, the function T γ ​ h T_{\gamma}h is well-defined because ℋ ↪ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{H}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) , so h h has a continuous representative in 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) . Let k ​ ( y , y ′ ) = e − 1 2 ​ ε ​ ‖ y − y ′ ‖ 2 k(y,y^{\prime})=e^{-\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\|y-y^{\prime}\|^{2}} . We have that 
 | T γ ​ h ​ ( y ) | \displaystyle\left|T_{\gamma}h(y)\right| 
 = | ∫ k ​ ( y , y ′ ) ​ h ​ ( y ′ ) ​ 𝑑 γ ​ ( y ′ ) | ≤ ‖ h ‖ L ∞ ​ ( 𝒴 ) ​ ‖ γ ‖ TV < ∞ . \displaystyle=\left|\int k(y,y^{\prime})\,h(y^{\prime})\,d\gamma(y^{\prime})\right|\leq\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y})}\|\gamma\|_{\text{TV}}<\infty. 
 Now we show that T γ ​ h ∈ ℋ T_{\gamma}h\in\mathcal{H} . Let k ​ ( y , y ′ ) = e − 1 2 ​ ε ​ ‖ y − y ′ ‖ 2 k(y,y^{\prime})=e^{-\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\|y-y^{\prime}\|^{2}} . For every y ′ ∈ 𝒴 y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y} , the function y ′ ↦ k ​ ( y , y ′ ) y^{\prime}\mapsto k(y,y^{\prime}) is C ∞ C^{\infty} . For each y ∈ 𝒴 y\in\mathcal{Y} , T γ ​ h ​ ( y ) = γ ​ ( k ​ ( y , ⋅ ) ​ h ) T_{\gamma}h(y)=\gamma\left(k(y,\cdot)\,h\right) . Since y ′ ↦ k ​ ( y , y ′ ) ∈ C ∞ ​ ( 𝒴 ) y^{\prime}\mapsto k(y,y^{\prime})\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y}) and ℋ \mathcal{H} is an algebra k ​ ( y , ⋅ ) ​ h k(y,\cdot)h belongs to ℋ \mathcal{H} . As a consequence, 
 | T γ ​ h ​ ( y ) | \displaystyle\left|T_{\gamma}h(y)\right| 
 ≤ ‖ k ​ ( y , ⋅ ) ​ h ‖ ℋ ​ ‖ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \displaystyle\leq\|k(y,\cdot)h\|_{\mathcal{H}}\|\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})} 
 ≤ K 2 ​ ‖ k ​ ( y , ⋅ ) ‖ ℋ ​ ‖ h ‖ ℋ ​ ‖ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \displaystyle\leq K_{2}\|k(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{H}}\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\|\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})} 
 ⟹ ‖ T γ ​ h ‖ L ∞ ​ ( 𝒴 ) \displaystyle\implies\|T_{\gamma}h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y})} 
 ≤ K 2 ​ M 0 ​ ‖ h ‖ ℋ ​ ‖ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) , \displaystyle\leq K_{2}M_{0}\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\|\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}, 
 where M 0 = sup y ‖ k ​ ( y , ⋅ ) ‖ ℋ < ∞ M_{0}=\sup_{y}\|k(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\infty because 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} is compact. Similarly, for any multi-index α \alpha , define M α = sup y ‖ ∂ y α k ​ ( y , ⋅ ) ‖ ℋ < ∞ M_{\alpha}=\sup_{y}\|\partial^{\alpha}_{y}k(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\infty . Using the same argument and knowing that ∂ α T γ ​ h ​ ( y ) = γ ​ ( ∂ α k ​ ( y , ⋅ ) ​ h ) {\partial^{\alpha}T_{\gamma}h(y)}={\gamma(\partial^{\alpha}k(y,\cdot)h)} , we have that ‖ ∂ α T γ ​ h ‖ L ∞ ​ ( 𝒴 ) < ∞ \|\partial^{\alpha}T_{\gamma}h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y})}<\infty . Then it holds that 
 ‖ T γ ​ h ‖ ℋ \displaystyle\|T_{\gamma}h\|_{\mathcal{H}} 
 = ( ∑ | α | ≤ s ‖ ∂ α T γ ​ h ‖ L 2 ​ ( 𝒴 ) 2 ) 1 / 2 \displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq s}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}T_{\gamma}h\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{Y})}^{2}\right)^{1/2} 
 ≤ | 𝒴 | 1 / 2 ​ ∑ | α | ≤ s ‖ ∂ α T γ ​ h ‖ L ∞ ​ ( 𝒴 ) \displaystyle\leq\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|^{1/2}\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq s}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}T_{\gamma}h\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y})} 
 ≤ K 2 | 𝒴 | 1 / 2 ∥ h ∥ ℋ ∥ γ ∥ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) ∑ | α | ≤ s M α = : K 3 ∥ h ∥ ℋ ∥ γ ∥ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) . \displaystyle\leq K_{2}\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|^{1/2}\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}\|\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq s}M_{\alpha}=:K_{3}\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}\|\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}. 
 The first inequality follows from elementary inequalities between L 2 L^{2} and L ∞ L^{\infty} norms. The last inequality uses that ℋ \mathcal{H} is an algebra from previous result.
∎ 
 Next, we prove the Hadamard differentiability of Sinkhorn divergence in the dual Sobolev RKHS topology. The Hadamard derivative is of the same form as presented in Goldfeld et al. ( 2024 , Lem. 3) , but in an ambient RKHS space ( ℋ \mathcal{H} ) rather than a dual Hölder space. We present a proof here for completeness. 
 Lemma C.4 (Hadamard derivative of S ε S_{\varepsilon} ) . 
 For a fixed ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 , the functional S ε : ( μ , ν ) ↦ S ε ​ ( μ , ν ) , 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ⊂ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) → ℝ S_{\varepsilon}:(\mu,\nu)\mapsto{S}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right),\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\subset\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\to\mathbb{R} is Hadamard differentiable at ( μ , ν ) (\mu,\nu) , tangentially to ℳ 0 , μ × ℳ 0 , ν \mathcal{M}_{0,\mu}\times\mathcal{M}_{0,\nu} , with Hadamard derivative S ε ′ ​ [ μ , ν ] : ℳ 0 , μ × ℳ 0 , ν → ℝ S_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}[\mu,\nu]:\mathcal{M}_{0,\mu}\times\mathcal{M}_{0,\nu}\to\mathbb{R} given by 
 S ε ′ ​ [ μ , ν ] ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) = ∫ υ 1 ( μ , ν ) ​ 𝑑 γ 1 + ∫ υ 0 ( μ , ν ) ​ 𝑑 γ 2 . S_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}[\mu,\nu](\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2})=\int\upsilon_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}\,d\gamma^{1}+\int\upsilon_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}\,d\gamma^{2}. 
 Proof. 
 By linearity of derivatives, it suffices to compute the derivative of ( α , β ) ↦ OT ε ​ ( α , β ) (\alpha,\beta)\mapsto\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\alpha,\,\beta\right) . Consider paths ( μ t ) t > 0 (\mu_{t})_{t>0} and ( ν t ) t > 0 (\nu_{t})_{t>0} in 𝒫 μ \mathcal{P}_{\mu} and 𝒫 ν \mathcal{P}_{\nu} respectively, such that μ t = μ + t ​ γ t 1 \mu_{t}=\mu+t\gamma^{1}_{t} and ν t = ν + t ​ γ t 2 \nu_{t}=\nu+t\gamma^{2}_{t} , with γ t i → γ i \gamma_{t}^{i}\to\gamma^{i} as t ↓ 0 t\downarrow 0 in ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) , for i ∈ { 1 , 2 } i\in\{1,2\} and some ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) ∈ ℳ 0 , μ × ℳ 0 , ν (\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{0,\mu}\times\mathcal{M}_{0,\nu} . Then, 
 1 t ​ ( OT ε ​ ( μ t , ν t ) − OT ε ​ ( μ , ν ) ) = 1 t ​ [ ( μ t ​ φ 1 ( μ t , ν t ) + ν t ​ φ 0 ( μ t , ν t ) ) − ( μ ​ φ 1 ( μ , ν ) + ν ​ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) ] . \frac{1}{t}\left(\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu_{t},\,\nu_{t}\right)-\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)\right)=\frac{1}{t}\left[\left(\mu_{t}\varphi_{1}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}+\nu_{t}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}\right)-\left(\mu\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}+\nu\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}\right)\right]. 
 For any ( μ , ν ) ∈ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) (\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) , recall the density of the EOT plan 
 ξ ( μ , ν ) ​ ( y 1 , y 2 ) = exp ⁡ ( 1 ε ​ ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) ​ ( y 1 ) + φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ​ ( y 2 ) − ‖ y 1 − y 2 ‖ 2 2 ) ) . \xi^{(\mu,\nu)}(y_{1},y_{2})=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}(y_{1})+\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}(y_{2})-\frac{\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|^{2}}{2}\right)\right). 
 From the optimality of ( φ 1 ( μ t , ν t ) , φ 0 ( μ t , ν t ) ) \left(\varphi_{1}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})},\varphi_{0}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}\right) , we have that 
 1 t ​ ( OT ε ​ ( μ t , ν t ) − OT ε ​ ( μ , ν ) ) \displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\left(\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu_{t},\,\nu_{t}\right)-\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)\right) 
 ≥ 1 t ​ [ ( μ t ​ φ 1 ( μ , ν ) + ν t ​ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) − ( μ ​ φ 1 ( μ , ν ) + ν ​ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) ] \displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{t}\left[{\left(\mu_{t}\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}+\nu_{t}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}\right)-\left(\mu\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}+\nu\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}\right)}\right] 
 − ε t ​ [ ∫ ∫ ξ ( μ , ν ) ​ ( y 1 , y 2 ) ​ 𝑑 μ t ​ ( y 1 ) ​ 𝑑 ν t ​ ( y 2 ) − 1 ] \displaystyle\quad-\frac{\varepsilon}{t}\left[\int\int\xi^{(\mu,\nu)}(y_{1},y_{2})\,d\mu_{t}(y_{1})\,d\nu_{t}(y_{2})-1\right] 
 = γ t 1 ​ φ 1 ( μ , ν ) + γ t 2 ​ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) − t ​ ε ​ ∫ ∫ ξ ( μ , ν ) ​ ( y 1 , y 2 ) ​ 𝑑 γ t 1 ​ ( y 1 ) ​ 𝑑 γ t 2 ​ ( y 2 ) , \displaystyle=\gamma_{t}^{1}\,\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}+\gamma_{t}^{2}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}-t\varepsilon\int\int\xi^{(\mu,\nu)}(y_{1},y_{2})\,d\gamma_{t}^{1}(y_{1})\,d\gamma_{t}^{2}(y_{2}), 
 where the last equality follows by expanding μ t \mu_{t} (and ν t \nu_{t} ) as μ + t ​ γ t 1 \mu+t\gamma_{t}^{1} (and ν + t ​ γ t 2 \nu+t\gamma_{t}^{2} ), and noting that μ ​ ( ξ ( μ , ν ) ​ ( ⋅ , y 2 ) ) = 1 \mu\left(\xi^{(\mu,\nu)}(\cdot,y_{2})\right)=1 and ν ​ ( ξ ( μ , ν ) ​ ( y 1 , ⋅ ) ) = 1 \nu\left(\xi^{(\mu,\nu)}(y_{1},\cdot)\right)=1 for all y 1 , y 2 ∈ 𝒴 y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y} . Consequently, 
 lim inf t ↓ 0 1 t ​ ( OT ε ​ ( μ t , ν t ) − OT ε ​ ( μ , ν ) ) ≥ lim t ↓ 0 ( γ t 1 ​ φ 1 ( μ , ν ) + γ t 2 ​ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) − t ​ ε ​ ∫ ∫ ξ ( μ , ν ) ​ ( y 1 , y 2 ) ​ 𝑑 γ t 1 ​ ( y 1 ) ​ 𝑑 γ t 2 ​ ( y 2 ) ) . \liminf_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{t}\left(\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu_{t},\,\nu_{t}\right)-\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)\right)\geq\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\left(\gamma_{t}^{1}\,\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}+\gamma_{t}^{2}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}-t\varepsilon\int\int\xi^{(\mu,\nu)}(y_{1},y_{2})\,d\gamma_{t}^{1}(y_{1})\,d\gamma_{t}^{2}(y_{2})\right). 
 We will show that the limit on the RHS is equal to γ 1 ​ φ 1 ( μ , ν ) + γ 2 ​ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) \gamma^{1}\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}+\gamma^{2}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)} . We already have that 
 | ( γ t 1 − γ 1 ) ​ φ 1 ( μ , ν ) + ( γ t 2 − γ 2 ) ​ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) | \displaystyle\left|(\gamma_{t}^{1}-\gamma^{1})\,\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}+(\gamma_{t}^{2}-\gamma^{2})\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}\right| 
 ≤ ‖ γ t 1 − γ 1 ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) ​ ‖ φ 1 ( μ , ν ) ‖ ℋ + ‖ γ t 2 − γ 2 ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) ​ ‖ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ‖ ℋ → t ↓ 0 0 \displaystyle\leq\left\|\gamma_{t}^{1}-\gamma^{1}\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}\left\|\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}+\left\|\gamma_{t}^{2}-\gamma^{2}\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}\left\|\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\xrightarrow[]{t\downarrow 0}0 
 because γ t i → γ i \gamma_{t}^{i}\to\gamma^{i} as t → 0 t\to 0 in ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) for i ∈ { 1 , 2 } i\in\{1,2\} and entropic potentials are uniformly bounded by the first part of Lem.   C.2 . Therefore, it suffices to show that 
 ∫ ∫ ξ ( μ , ν ) ​ ( y 1 , y 2 ) ​ 𝑑 γ t 1 ​ ( y 1 ) ​ 𝑑 γ t 2 ​ ( y 2 ) = 𝒪 ​ ( 1 ) as  ​ t ↓ 0 . \int\int\xi^{(\mu,\nu)}(y_{1},y_{2})\,d\gamma_{t}^{1}(y_{1})\,d\gamma_{t}^{2}(y_{2})=\mathcal{O}(1)\quad\text{as }t\downarrow 0. 
 (23) 
 We show this by using three properties for the Sobolev RKHS ℋ \mathcal{H} detailed in Lem.   C.3 . Choosing F ​ ( h ) = e h − 1 F(h)=e^{h}-1 in the first property gives us that there exists a non-decreasing function C F : ℝ + → ℝ + C_{F}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{+} such that for all h ∈ ℋ h\in\mathcal{H} , 
 ‖ e h − 1 ‖ ℋ ≤ C F ​ ( ‖ h ‖ L ∞ ​ ( 𝒴 ) ) ​ ‖ h ‖ ℋ . \|e^{h}-1\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C_{F}(\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y})})\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}. 
 Because the constant function 𝟏 ∈ ℋ \mathbf{1}\in\mathcal{H} , we have that 
 ‖ e h ‖ ℋ ≤ ‖ e h − 1 ‖ ℋ + ‖ 𝟏 ‖ ℋ ≤ C F ​ ( ‖ h ‖ L ∞ ​ ( 𝒴 ) ) ​ ( ‖ h ‖ ℋ + 1 ) . \|e^{h}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\|e^{h}-1\|_{\mathcal{H}}+\|\mathbf{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C_{F}(\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y})})(\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}+1). 
 (24) 
 The Sobolev embedding theorem (Adams and Fournier, 2003 , Thm. 4.12) gives that ℋ \mathcal{H} embeds continuously in L ∞ ​ ( 𝒴 ) L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y}) . Further, Lem.   C.2 gives that the functions of the form h = φ i ( μ , ν ) h=\varphi_{i}^{(\mu,\nu)} are bounded in ℋ \mathcal{H} , uniformly over i ∈ { 1 , 0 } i\in\{1,0\} and ( μ , ν ) ∈ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) (\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) ; denote the bound by M 1 ≡ M 1 ​ ( ε , s , d , | 𝒴 | ) M_{1}\equiv M_{1}(\varepsilon,s,d,\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|) . As a consequence, h = φ i ( μ , ν ) h=\varphi_{i}^{(\mu,\nu)} are uniformly bounded in L ∞ ​ ( 𝒴 ) L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y}) norm, denote the bound by M 2 ≡ M 2 ​ ( ε , s , d , | 𝒴 | ) M_{2}\equiv M_{2}(\varepsilon,s,d,\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|) . Therefore, using ( 24 ), for all i ∈ { 1 , 0 } i\in\{1,0\} and ( μ , ν ) ∈ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) (\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) , 
 ∥ e 1 ε ​ φ i ( μ , ν ) ∥ ℋ ≤ C F ( M 2 ε ) ( M 1 ε + 1 ) = : K 1 ( ε , s , d , | 𝒴 | ) ≡ K 1 . \left\|e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varphi_{i}^{(\mu,\nu)}}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C_{F}\left(\frac{M_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)\left(\frac{M_{1}}{\varepsilon}+1\right)=:K_{1}(\varepsilon,s,d,\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|)\equiv K_{1}. 
 (25) 
 The second property from Lem.   C.3 shows that ℋ \mathcal{H} is a Banach algebra. Therefore, for every h 1 , h 2 h_{1},h_{2} , the pointwise product h 1 ​ h 2 ∈ ℋ h_{1}h_{2}\in\mathcal{H} (defined a.e. 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} ), belongs to ℋ \mathcal{H} and there exists a constant K 2 ≡ K 2 ​ ( s , d , | 𝒴 | ) K_{2}\equiv K_{2}(s,d,\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|) such that 
 ‖ h 1 ​ h 2 ‖ ℋ ≤ K 2 ​ ‖ h 1 ‖ ℋ ​ ‖ h 2 ‖ ℋ . \|h_{1}h_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq K_{2}\,\|h_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\|h_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}. 
 (26) 
 Let k ε ​ ( y , y ′ ) = e − 1 2 ​ ε ​ ‖ y − y ′ ‖ 2 k_{\varepsilon}(y,y^{\prime})=e^{-\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\|y-y^{\prime}\|^{2}} , and define G γ ​ h : y ↦ ∫ 𝒴 k ε ​ ( y , y ′ ) ​ h ​ ( y ′ ) ​ 𝑑 γ ​ ( y ′ ) G_{\gamma}h:y\mapsto\int_{\mathcal{Y}}k_{\varepsilon}(y,y^{\prime})\,h(y^{\prime})\,d\gamma(y^{\prime}) . The third property from Lem.   C.3 shows that for all h ∈ ℋ h\in\mathcal{H} and γ ∈ ℳ ​ ( 𝒴 ) \gamma\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{Y}) , G γ ​ h ∈ ℋ G_{\gamma}h\in\mathcal{H} , and there exists a constant K 3 ≡ K 3 ​ ( s , d , | 𝒴 | ) K_{3}\equiv K_{3}(s,d,\left|\mathcal{Y}\right|) such that 
 ‖ G γ ​ h ‖ ℋ ≤ K 3 ​ ‖ h ‖ ℋ ​ ‖ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) . \|G_{\gamma}h\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq K_{3}\,\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\|\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}. 
 (27) 
 The three bounds ( 24 ), ( 26 ), and ( 27 ) allow us to establish the following string of inequalities 
 | ∫ ∫ ξ ( μ , ν ) ​ ( y 1 , y 2 ) ​ 𝑑 γ t 1 ​ ( y 1 ) ​ 𝑑 γ t 2 ​ ( y 2 ) | \displaystyle\left|\int\int\xi^{(\mu,\nu)}(y_{1},y_{2})\,d\gamma_{t}^{1}(y_{1})\,d\gamma_{t}^{2}(y_{2})\right| 
 = | ⟨ γ t 2 , e 1 ε ​ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ​ T γ t 1 ​ e 1 ε ​ φ 1 ( μ , ν ) ⟩ | \displaystyle=\left|\left\langle\gamma_{t}^{2},\,e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}}\,T_{\gamma_{t}^{1}}e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}}\right\rangle\right| 
 ≤ ‖ γ t 2 ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) ​ ‖ e 1 ε ​ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ​ T γ t 1 ​ e 1 ε ​ φ 1 ( μ , ν ) ‖ ℋ \displaystyle\leq\left\|\gamma_{t}^{2}\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}\left\|e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}}\,T_{\gamma_{t}^{1}}e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}} 
 ≤ K 2 ​ ‖ γ t 2 ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) ​ ‖ e 1 ε ​ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ‖ ℋ ​ ‖ T γ t 1 ​ e φ 1 ( μ , ν ) / ε ‖ ℋ \displaystyle\leq K_{2}\left\|\gamma_{t}^{2}\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}\left\|e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\left\|\,T_{\gamma_{t}^{1}}e^{\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}/\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}} 
 ≤ K 2 ​ K 3 ​ ‖ e 1 ε ​ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ‖ ℋ ​ ‖ e 1 ε ​ φ 1 ( μ , ν ) ‖ ℋ ​ ‖ γ t 1 ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) ​ ‖ γ t 2 ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \displaystyle\leq K_{2}K_{3}\left\|e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\left\|e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\left\|\gamma_{t}^{1}\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}\left\|\gamma_{t}^{2}\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})} 
 ≤ K 1 2 ​ K 2 ​ K 3 ​ ‖ γ t 1 ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) ​ ‖ γ t 2 ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) . \displaystyle\leq K_{1}^{2}K_{2}K_{3}\,\left\|\gamma_{t}^{1}\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}\,\left\|\gamma_{t}^{2}\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}. 
 (28) 
 The first equality follows by the fact that e 1 ε ​ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) , T γ t 1 ​ e 1 ε ​ φ 1 ( μ , ν ) ∈ ℋ e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}},T_{\gamma_{t}^{1}}e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}}\in\mathcal{H} , and ℋ \mathcal{H} is a Banach algebra and therefore, their product also belongs to ℋ \mathcal{H} . The second, third, and last inequalities follow from ( 26 ), ( 27 ), and ( 25 ) respectively. Because γ t i → γ i \gamma_{t}^{i}\to\gamma^{i} in ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) , there exists a t 0 > 0 t_{0}>0 such that sup t ≤ t 0 ‖ γ t i ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) < ∞ \sup_{t\leq t_{0}}\|\gamma_{t}^{i}\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}<\infty for i ∈ { 1 , 2 } i\in\{1,2\} . And therefore, Equation   23 holds. Following this, we have that 
 γ 1 ​ φ 1 ( μ , ν ) + γ 2 ​ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ≤ lim inf t ↓ 0 1 t ​ ( OT ε ​ ( μ t , ν t ) − OT ε ​ ( μ , ν ) ) . \gamma^{1}\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}+\gamma^{2}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}\leq\liminf_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{t}\left(\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu_{t},\,\nu_{t}\right)-\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)\right). 
 (29) 
 Now by optimality of ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) , φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) (\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)},\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}) , we have that 
 1 t ​ ( OT ε ​ ( μ t , ν t ) − OT ε ​ ( μ , ν ) ) \displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\left(\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu_{t},\,\nu_{t}\right)-\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)\right) 
 ≤ 1 t ​ [ ( μ t ​ φ 1 ( μ t , ν t ) + ν t ​ φ 0 ( μ t , ν t ) ) − ( μ ​ φ 1 ( μ t , ν t ) + ν ​ φ 0 ( μ t , ν t ) ) ] \displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{t}\left[{\left(\mu_{t}\varphi_{1}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}+\nu_{t}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}\right)-\left(\mu\varphi_{1}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}+\nu\varphi_{0}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}\right)}\right] 
 + ε t ​ [ ∫ ∫ ξ ( μ t , ν t ) ​ ( y 1 , y 2 ) ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( y 1 ) ​ 𝑑 ν ​ ( y 2 ) − 1 ] \displaystyle\quad+\frac{\varepsilon}{t}\left[\int\int\xi^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}(y_{1},y_{2})\,d\mu(y_{1})\,d\nu(y_{2})-1\right] 
 = γ t 1 ​ φ 1 ( μ t , ν t ) + γ t 2 ​ φ 0 ( μ t , ν t ) + t ​ ε ​ ∫ ∫ ξ ( μ t , ν t ) ​ ( y 1 , y 2 ) ​ 𝑑 γ t 1 ​ ( y 1 ) ​ 𝑑 γ t 2 ​ ( y 2 ) , \displaystyle=\gamma_{t}^{1}\,\varphi_{1}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}+\gamma_{t}^{2}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}+t\varepsilon\int\int\xi^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}(y_{1},y_{2})\,d\gamma_{t}^{1}(y_{1})\,d\gamma_{t}^{2}(y_{2}), 
 (30) 
 where the equality follows because μ t ​ ( ξ ( μ t , ν t ) ​ ( ⋅ , y 2 ) ) = 1 \mu_{t}\left(\xi^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}(\cdot,y_{2})\right)=1 and ν t ​ ( ξ ( μ t , ν t ) ​ ( y 1 , ⋅ ) ) = 1 \nu_{t}\left(\xi^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}(y_{1},\cdot)\right)=1 for all y 1 , y 2 ∈ 𝒴 y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y} . Consequently, 
 lim sup t ↓ 0 1 t ​ ( OT ε ​ ( μ t , ν t ) − OT ε ​ ( μ , ν ) ) \displaystyle\limsup_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{t}\left(\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu_{t},\,\nu_{t}\right)-\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)\right) 
 ≤ lim t ↓ 0 ( γ t 1 ​ φ 1 ( μ t , ν t ) + γ t 2 ​ φ 0 ( μ t , ν t ) + t ​ ε ​ ∫ ∫ ξ ( μ t , ν t ) ​ ( y 1 , y 2 ) ​ 𝑑 γ t 1 ​ ( y 1 ) ​ 𝑑 γ t 2 ​ ( y 2 ) ) \displaystyle\leq\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\left(\gamma_{t}^{1}\,\varphi_{1}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}+\gamma_{t}^{2}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}+t\varepsilon\int\int\xi^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}(y_{1},y_{2})\,d\gamma_{t}^{1}(y_{1})\,d\gamma_{t}^{2}(y_{2})\right) 
 = lim t ↓ 0 ( γ t 1 ​ φ 1 ( μ t , ν t ) + γ t 2 ​ φ 0 ( μ t , ν t ) ) . \displaystyle=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\left(\gamma_{t}^{1}\,\varphi_{1}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}+\gamma_{t}^{2}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}\right). 
 The equality follows again from Sec.   C.3 because the upper bound established there is uniform over all ( μ , ν ) (\mu,\nu) . For any sequence ( f t ) t > 0 ⊂ ℋ (f_{t})_{t>0}\subset\mathcal{H} and ( η t , t > 0 ) ⊂ ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) (\eta_{t},t>0)\subset\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y}) , such that f t → f f_{t}\to f in ℋ \mathcal{H} and η t → η \eta_{t}\to\eta in ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) , we have that η t ​ f t → η ​ f \eta_{t}f_{t}\to\eta f as t ↓ 0 t\downarrow 0 . This hold because 
 | η t ​ f t − η ​ f | \displaystyle\left|\eta_{t}f_{t}-\eta f\right| 
 ≤ | ( η t − η ) ​ f t | + | η ​ ( f t − f ) | ≤ ‖ η t − η ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) ​ ‖ f t ‖ ℋ + ‖ η ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) ​ ‖ f t − f ‖ ℋ . \displaystyle\leq\left|(\eta_{t}-\eta)f_{t}\right|+\left|\eta(f_{t}-f)\right|\leq\|\eta_{t}-\eta\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}\|f_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}}+\|\eta\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}\|f_{t}-f\|_{\mathcal{H}}. 
 Since f t → f f_{t}\to f in ℋ \mathcal{H} , for every ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 , there exists a t 0 > 0 t_{0}>0 such that ‖ f t ‖ ℋ < ϵ \|f_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\epsilon for all t < t 0 t<t_{0} . Further, lim t ↓ 0 ‖ f t − f ‖ ℋ = 0 \lim_{t\downarrow 0}\|f_{t}-f\|_{\mathcal{H}}=0 and lim t ↓ 0 ‖ η t − η ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) = 0 \lim_{t\downarrow 0}\|\eta_{t}-\eta\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}=0 , which proves the claim. Therefore, 
 lim sup t ↓ 0 1 t ​ ( OT ε ​ ( μ t , ν t ) − OT ε ​ ( μ , ν ) ) ≤ γ 1 ​ φ 1 ( μ , ν ) + γ 2 ​ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) . \limsup_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{t}\left(\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu_{t},\,\nu_{t}\right)-\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)\right)\leq\gamma^{1}\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}+\gamma^{2}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}. 
 (31) 
 Combining ( 29 ) and ( 31 ), we prove that 
 lim t ↓ 0 1 t ​ ( OT ε ​ ( μ t , ν t ) − OT ε ​ ( μ , ν ) ) = γ 1 ​ φ 1 ( μ , ν ) + γ 2 ​ φ 0 ( μ , ν ) . \lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{t}\left(\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu_{t},\,\nu_{t}\right)-\text{OT}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mu,\,\nu\right)\right)=\gamma^{1}\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}+\gamma^{2}\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}. 
 ∎ 
 Now we focus on the first-order Hadamard differentiability of the function ( μ , ν ) ↦ ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) , φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) , 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ⊂ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) → ℋ × ℋ (\mu,\nu)\mapsto(\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)},\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}),\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\subset\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\to\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H} . 
 Lemma C.5 (Hadamard differentiability of entropic potentials in 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) ) . 
 The 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) map ( μ , ν ) ↦ ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) , φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) (\mu,\nu)\mapsto(\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)},\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}) is Hadamard differentiable at ( μ , ν ) (\mu,\nu) , tangentially to ℳ 0 , μ × ℳ 0 , ν \mathcal{M}_{0,\mu}\times\mathcal{M}_{0,\nu} . 
 Proof. 
 The Hadamard differentiable of the map Φ : ( μ , ν ) ↦ ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) , φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) \Phi:(\mu,\nu)\mapsto(\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)},\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}) in 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) has been established in the dual Hölder space topology by Goldfeld et al. ( 2024 , Thm. 3) , and in the Gaussian RKHS topology by Kokot and Luedtke ( 2025 , Cor. 43) , with the latter deriving explicit derivatives. Extending the differentiability in the Sobolev RKHS topology for measures follows by continuously embedding the Gaussian RKHS in the Sobolev RKHS.
Let 𝒢 \mathcal{G} be the Gaussian RKHS with kernel ( y 1 , y 2 ) ↦ exp ⁡ ( − ‖ y 1 − y 2 ‖ 2 / 2 ​ ε ) (y_{1},y_{2})\mapsto\exp(-\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|^{2}/2\varepsilon) , with norm ∥ ⋅ ∥ 𝒢 \|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}} and unit ball denoted by 𝒢 1 \mathcal{G}_{1} . The Hadamard differentiability of Φ \Phi as a map 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ⊂ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\subset\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) , is established in Kokot and Luedtke ( 2025 , Cor. 43) . We show that the same map is Hadamard differentiable when the domain is instead equipped with the stronger topology induced by ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) . 
 By the continuous embedding of the Gaussian RKHS into the Sobolev RKHS, there exists a constant C s > 0 C_{s}>0 such that for any g ∈ 𝒢 g\in\mathcal{G} , ‖ g ‖ ℋ ≤ C s ​ ‖ g ‖ 𝒢 \|g\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C_{s}\|g\|_{\mathcal{G}} . Now we dualize this inclusion. If g ∈ 𝒢 1 g\in\mathcal{G}_{1} , then C s − 1 ​ g ∈ ℋ 1 C_{s}^{-1}g\in\mathcal{H}_{1} . Therefore, for every signed measure γ \gamma , 
 ‖ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) = sup g ∈ 𝒢 1 | ∫ g ​ 𝑑 γ | ≤ C s ​ sup h ∈ ℋ 1 | ∫ h ​ 𝑑 γ | = C s ​ ‖ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) . \left\|\gamma\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})}=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{1}}\left|\int g\,d\gamma\right|\leq C_{s}\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}_{1}}\left|\int h\,d\gamma\right|=C_{s}\left\|\gamma\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}. 
 Therefore, the identity map from ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) → ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\to\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1}) is continuous.
Since Φ \Phi is Hadamard differentiable at ( μ , ν ) (\mu,\nu) as a map defined on 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ⊂ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\subset\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1}) ,
the defining limit remains valid for any perturbation sequences converging in the stronger topology ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) . So the Hadamard expansion proved in Kokot and Luedtke ( 2025 , Cor. 43) applies without change. 
 Therefore, Φ \Phi as a map 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ⊂ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\subset\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) , is Hadamard differentiable at ( μ , ν ) (\mu,\nu) tangentially to ℳ 0 , μ × ℳ 0 , ν \mathcal{M}_{0,\mu}\times\mathcal{M}_{0,\nu} . Moreover, the Hadamard derivative is exactly the same as the derivative obtained when the domain is viewed as a subset of ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1}) .
∎ 
 Now, we lift the Hadamard differentiability of entropic potentials from 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) to ℋ × ℋ \mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H} . 
 Proposition C.6 (Hadamard differentiability of entropic potentials in ℋ \mathcal{H} ) . 
 The 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) → ℋ × ℋ \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H} map ( μ , ν ) ↦ ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) , φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) (\mu,\nu)\mapsto(\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)},\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}) is Hadamard differentiable at ( μ , ν ) (\mu,\nu) , tangentially to ℳ 0 , μ × ℳ 0 , ν \mathcal{M}_{0,\mu}\times\mathcal{M}_{0,\nu} . 
 Proof. 
 The argument follows the same strategy as Goldfeld et al. ( 2024 , Thm. 3) , so we only sketch the main steps and refer to that result for full details. For each i ∈ { 0 , 1 } i\in\{0,1\} , Goldfeld et al. ( 2024 , Lem. 4) establish that the maps ( μ , ν ) ↦ φ i ( μ , ν ) (\mu,\nu)\mapsto\varphi_{i}^{(\mu,\nu)} , i ∈ { 1 , 0 } i\in\{1,0\} , are Hadamard differentiable in 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) . Since 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} is compact, the canonical embedding 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ↪ L 2 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\mathcal{Y}) is continuous. It follows that, for every multi-index α = ( α 1 , … , α d ) ∈ ℕ 0 d \alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d} ( ℕ 0 = ℕ ∪ { 0 } \mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\} ) with 0 < | α | ≤ s 0<\left|\alpha\right|\leq s , the functional ( μ , ν ) ↦ D α ​ φ i ( μ , ν ) , 𝒫 μ × 𝒫 ν ⊂ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) → L 2 ​ ( 𝒴 ) (\mu,\nu)\mapsto D^{\alpha}\varphi_{i}^{(\mu,\nu)},\mathcal{P}_{\mu}\times\mathcal{P}_{\nu}\subset\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{Y}) is Hadamard differentiable. We denote the derivative by [ D α ​ φ i ] ′ ​ [ μ , ν ] : ℳ 0 , μ × ℳ 0 , ν → L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ) [D^{\alpha}\varphi_{i}]^{\prime}[\mu,\nu]:\mathcal{M}_{0,\mu}\times\mathcal{M}_{0,\nu}\to L^{2}(P^{*}) . 
 Now consider paths ( μ t ) t ≥ 0 = ( μ + t ​ γ t 1 ) t ≥ 0 ⊂ 𝒫 μ (\mu_{t})_{t\geq 0}=(\mu+t\gamma_{t}^{1})_{t\geq 0}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\mu} and ( ν t ) t ≥ 0 = ( ν + t ​ γ t 2 ) t ≥ 0 ⊂ 𝒫 ν (\nu_{t})_{t\geq 0}=(\nu+t\gamma_{t}^{2})_{t\geq 0}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\nu} such that γ t i → γ i \gamma_{t}^{i}\to\gamma^{i} as t ↓ 0 t\downarrow 0 in ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) . Pick any sequence t n ↓ 0 t_{n}\downarrow 0 as n → ∞ n\to\infty , then q t n := t n − 1 ​ ( φ i ( μ t n , ν t n ) − φ i ( μ , ν ) ) q_{t_{n}}:=t_{n}^{-1}\left(\varphi_{i}^{(\mu_{t_{n}},\nu_{t_{n}})}-\varphi_{i}^{(\mu,\nu)}\right) is Cauchy in ℋ \mathcal{H} . This is because for any n , m ∈ ℕ n,m\in\mathbb{N} , 
 ‖ q t n − q t m ‖ ℋ 2 = ∑ | α | ≤ s ‖ D α ​ φ i ( μ t n , ν t n ) − D α ​ φ i ( μ , ν ) t n − D α ​ φ i ( μ t m , ν t m ) − D α ​ φ i ( μ , ν ) t m ‖ L 2 ​ ( 𝒴 ) 2 , \left\|q_{t_{n}}-q_{t_{m}}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq s}\left\|\frac{D^{\alpha}\varphi_{i}^{(\mu_{t_{n}},\nu_{t_{n}})}-D^{\alpha}\varphi_{i}^{(\mu,\nu)}}{t_{n}}-\frac{D^{\alpha}\varphi_{i}^{(\mu_{t_{m}},\nu_{t_{m}})}-D^{\alpha}\varphi_{i}^{(\mu,\nu)}}{t_{m}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{Y})}^{2}, 
 which converges to 0 because each component term in the finite sum of the RKHS above is Cauchy in L 2 L^{2} . Since there are finitely many terms for | α | ≤ s \left|\alpha\right|\leq s , the RHS converges to 0 as n , m → ∞ n,m\to\infty . By completeness of ℋ \mathcal{H} , the limit exists in ℋ \mathcal{H} . So let t n − 1 ​ ( φ i ( μ t n , ν t n ) − φ i ( μ , ν ) ) → φ i ¯ t_{n}^{-1}\left(\varphi_{i}^{(\mu_{t_{n}},\nu_{t_{n}})}-\varphi_{i}^{(\mu,\nu)}\right)\to\overline{\varphi_{i}} in ℋ \mathcal{H} . Then, by definition of the limit in the Sobolev space ℋ \mathcal{H} , t n − 1 ​ ( D α ​ φ i ( μ t n , ν t n ) − D α ​ φ i ( μ , ν ) ) → D α ​ φ ¯ i t_{n}^{-1}\left(D^{\alpha}\varphi_{i}^{(\mu_{t_{n}},\nu_{t_{n}})}-D^{\alpha}\varphi_{i}^{(\mu,\nu)}\right)\to D^{\alpha}\overline{\varphi}_{i} in L 2 ​ ( 𝒴 ) L^{2}(\mathcal{Y}) . On the other hand, by the Hadamard differentiability of ( μ , ν ) ↦ D α ​ φ i ( μ , ν ) (\mu,\nu)\mapsto D^{\alpha}\varphi_{i}^{(\mu,\nu)} , the same limit must equal [ D α ​ φ i ] ′ ​ [ μ , ν ] ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) [D^{\alpha}\varphi_{i}]^{\prime}[\mu,\nu](\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2}) .
Therefore, 
 D α ​ φ ¯ i = [ D α ​ φ i ] ′ ​ [ μ , ν ] ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) for all  ​ α ∈ ℕ 0 d ​  with  ​ | α | ≤ s . D^{\alpha}\bar{\varphi}_{i}=[D^{\alpha}\varphi_{i}]^{\prime}[\mu,\nu](\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2})\qquad\text{for all }\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\text{ with }|\alpha|\leq s. 
 This shows that φ ¯ i \overline{\varphi}_{i} is exactly the Sobolev function in ℋ \mathcal{H} whose weak derivatives coincide with the coordinatewise directional derivatives, that is D α φ ¯ i = [ D α φ i ] ′ [ μ , ν ] ) ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) D^{\alpha}\overline{\varphi}_{i}=[D^{\alpha}\varphi_{i}]^{\prime}[\mu,\nu])(\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2}) . Therefore, if the Hadamard derivative φ i ′ ​ [ μ , ν ] \varphi_{i}^{\prime}[\mu,\nu] exists, then φ i ′ ​ [ μ , ν ] ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) = φ ¯ i \varphi_{i}^{\prime}[\mu,\nu](\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2})=\overline{\varphi}_{i} . Using this pointwise characterization, indeed the map ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) ↦ φ i ′ ​ [ μ , ν ] ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) (\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2})\mapsto\varphi_{i}^{\prime}[\mu,\nu](\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2}) is linear and continuous from ℳ 0 , μ × ℳ 0 , ν \mathcal{M}_{0,\mu}\times\mathcal{M}_{0,\nu} into ℋ \mathcal{H} because D α ​ φ i ′ ​ [ μ , ν ] = [ D α ​ φ i ] ′ ​ [ μ , ν ] D^{\alpha}\varphi_{i}^{\prime}[\mu,\nu]=[D^{\alpha}\varphi_{i}]^{\prime}[\mu,\nu] is a linear and continuous function on ℳ 0 , μ × ℳ 0 , ν \mathcal{M}_{0,\mu}\times\mathcal{M}_{0,\nu} and summing over finitely such α \alpha gives the linearity and continuity of φ i ′ ​ [ μ , ν ] \varphi_{i}^{\prime}[\mu,\nu] on ℳ 0 , μ × ℳ 0 , ν \mathcal{M}_{0,\mu}\times\mathcal{M}_{0,\nu} . The explicit form of φ i ′ ​ [ μ , ν ] \varphi_{i}^{\prime}[\mu,\nu] is presented in Kokot and Luedtke ( 2025 , Cor. 43) .
∎ 
 The following corollary provides the explicit form of the Hadamard derivatives of the centered entropic potentials maps υ 1 : ( μ , ν ) ↦ υ 1 ( μ , ν ) \upsilon_{1}:(\mu,\nu)\mapsto\upsilon_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)} and υ 0 : ( μ , ν ) ↦ υ 0 ( μ , ν ) \upsilon_{0}:(\mu,\nu)\mapsto\upsilon_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)} under the null. 
 Corollary C.7 . 
 The Hadamard derivatives of υ i : ( μ , ν ) ↦ υ i ( μ , ν ) , 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ⊂ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) → ℋ \upsilon_{i}:(\mu,\nu)\mapsto\upsilon_{i}^{(\mu,\nu)},\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\subset\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\to\mathcal{H} for i ∈ { 1 , 0 } i\in\{1,0\} are given by 
 υ 1 ′ [ μ , μ ] ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) = K μ ( γ 1 − γ 2 ) + ε ρ 𝟏 and  υ 0 ′ [ μ , μ ) ] ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) = − K μ ( γ 1 − γ 2 ) − ε ρ 𝟏 \upsilon_{1}^{\prime}[\mu,\mu](\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2})=K_{\mu}(\gamma^{1}-\gamma^{2})+\varepsilon\rho\mathbf{1}\quad\textrm{and }\quad\upsilon_{0}^{\prime}[\mu,\mu)](\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2})=-K_{\mu}(\gamma^{1}-\gamma^{2})-\varepsilon\rho\mathbf{1} 
 for a constant ρ ∈ ℝ \rho\in\mathbb{R} . This constant is fully expressed in Kokot and Luedtke ( 2025 , Thm. 40) , and omitted here because it is inconsequential in our case and ends up getting canceled out. 
 The corollary is stated without proof and follows directly from Kokot and Luedtke ( 2025 , Cor. 43) by plugging υ i ′ ​ [ μ , μ ] ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) = φ i ′ ​ [ μ , μ ] ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) − φ i ′ ​ [ μ , μ ] ​ ( γ 2 − i , γ 2 − i ) \upsilon_{i}^{\prime}[\mu,\mu](\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2})=\varphi_{i}^{\prime}[\mu,\mu](\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2})-\varphi_{i}^{\prime}[\mu,\mu](\gamma^{2-i},\gamma^{2-i}) for i ∈ { 1 , 0 } i\in\{1,0\} . 
 Lemma C.8 (Thm. 48 in Kokot and Luedtke, 2025 ) . 
 The map S ε : ( μ , ν ) ↦ S ε ​ ( μ , ν ) , 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ⊂ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) → ℝ S_{\varepsilon}:(\mu,\nu)\mapsto S_{\varepsilon}(\mu,\nu),\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\subset\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\to\mathbb{R} is Hadamard differentiable at ( μ , μ ) (\mu,\mu) with Hadamard derivative S ε ′ ​ [ μ , μ ] : ℳ 0 , μ × ℳ 0 , μ → ℝ S_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}[\mu,\mu]:\mathcal{M}_{0,\mu}\times\mathcal{M}_{0,\mu}\to\mathbb{R} given by 
 S ε ′′ ​ [ μ , μ ] ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) = ( γ 1 − γ 2 ) ​ K μ ​ ( γ 1 − γ 2 ) . S_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}[\mu,\mu](\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2})=(\gamma^{1}-\gamma^{2})K_{\mu}(\gamma^{1}-\gamma^{2}). 
 We do not reproduce the full proof here, since the argument follows from Kokot and Luedtke ( 2025 , Thm. 48) after replacing the required topologies. Kokot and Luedtke ( 2025 , Thm. 48) derive the second-order Hadamard derivative (under the null) of a localized quadratic approximation of the Sinkhorn divergence from Kokot and Luedtke ( 2025 , Lem. 45) . In that argument, second-order Hadamard differentiability of the localized Sinkhorn divergence reduces to first-order Hadamard differentiability of the entropic potentials. This required first-order differentiability in the ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) topology is established in Prop.   C.6 , together with an explicit characterization of the corresponding derivative. Substituting this derivative formula into the proof of Kokot and Luedtke ( 2025 , Thm. 48) yields the stated Hadamard derivative S ε ′′ ​ [ μ , μ ] ​ ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) = ( γ 1 − γ 2 ) ​ K μ ​ ( γ 1 − γ 2 ) S_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}[\mu,\mu](\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2})=(\gamma^{1}-\gamma^{2})\,K_{\mu}\,(\gamma^{1}-\gamma^{2}) . 
 Appendix D Pathwise Differentiability of STE 
 In this appendix, we prove the main results stated in Sec.   3 . We begin by introducing notation that will be used throughout the proofs. For any score function s ∈ 𝒫 ˙ P s\in\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{P} , define 
 s X ​ ( x ) := 𝔼 P ​ [ s ​ ( X , A , Y ) | X = x ] and  s Y | A , X ​ ( y | a , x ) := s ​ ( x , a , y ) − 𝔼 P ​ [ s ​ ( X , A , Y ) | A = a , X = x ] . s_{X}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[s(X,A,Y)\,|\,X=x\right]\quad\textrm{and }\quad s_{Y\,|\,A,X}(y\,|\,a,x):=s(x,a,y)-\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[s(X,A,Y)\,|\,A=a,X=x\right]. 
 (32)

We also define the composed operators

 Similarly, because ( P Y | A , X ​ f ) ​ ( z ) (P_{Y\,|\,A,X}f)(z) is invariant in y y and

of ( A , X ) (A,X) and X X , respectively. Specifically, for

 D.1 First-order pathwise differentiability of STE 
 The first-order pathwise differentiability of 𝒮 = S ε ∘ J ∘ Ψ \mathscr{S}=S_{\varepsilon}\circ J\circ\Psi is established in three steps: (1) Hadamard differentiability of S ε S_{\varepsilon} ( Lem.   C.4 ), (2) Hadamard differentiability of J J ( Lem.   D.1 ), and (3) pathwise differentiability of Ψ \Psi ( Lem.   D.2 ). 
 Lemma D.1 (Hadamard derivative of J J ) . 
 The functional J : ℋ × ℋ → ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) J:\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}\to\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) is Hadamard differentiable at every ( f , g ) ∈ ℋ × ℋ (f,g)\in\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H} and the Hadamard derivative J ′ ​ [ f , g ] : ℋ × ℋ → ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) × ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) J^{\prime}[f,g]:\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}\to\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) is given by 
 J ′ ​ [ f , g ] ​ ( h 1 , h 2 ) = J ​ ( h 1 , h 2 ) . J^{\prime}[f,g](h^{1},h^{2})=J(h^{1},h^{2}). 
 Proof. 
 Consider paths ( f t ) t > 0 (f_{t})_{t>0} and ( g t ) t > 0 (g_{t})_{t>0} in ℋ \mathcal{H} such that f t = f + t ​ h t 1 f_{t}=f+th_{t}^{1} and g t = g + t ​ h t 2 g_{t}=g+th_{t}^{2} such that h t 1 → h 1 h_{t}^{1}\to h^{1} and h t 2 → h 2 h_{t}^{2}\to h^{2} as t → 0 t\to 0 in ℋ \mathcal{H} for some ( h 1 , h 2 ) ∈ ℋ × ℋ (h^{1},h^{2})\in\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H} . Then, 
 1 t ​ [ J ​ ( f t , g t ) − J ​ ( f , g ) ] \displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\left[J(f_{t},g_{t})-J(f,g)\right] 
 = J ​ ( h t 1 , h t 2 ) \displaystyle=J(h_{t}^{1},h_{t}^{2}) 
 ⟹ lim t → 0 1 t ​ [ J ​ ( f t , g t ) − J ​ ( f , g ) ] \displaystyle\implies\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{1}{t}\left[J(f_{t},g_{t})-J(f,g)\right] 
 = lim t → 0 J ​ ( h t 1 , h t 2 ) . \displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0}J(h_{t}^{1},h_{t}^{2}). 
 We have that J ​ ( h t 1 , h t 2 ) J(h_{t}^{1},h_{t}^{2}) converges to J ​ ( h 1 , h 2 ) J(h^{1},h^{2}) in the supnorm as 
 ‖ J ​ ( h t 1 , h t 2 ) − J ​ ( h 1 , h 2 ) ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \displaystyle\left\|J(h_{t}^{1},h_{t}^{2})-J(h^{1},h^{2})\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})} 
 = sup h ∈ ℋ 1 | ⟨ h , h t 1 − h 1 ⟩ ℋ | + sup h ∈ ℋ 1 | ⟨ h , h t 2 − h 2 ⟩ ℋ | \displaystyle=\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}_{1}}\left|\left\langle h,h_{t}^{1}-h^{1}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right|+\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}_{1}}\left|\left\langle h,h_{t}^{2}-h^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right| 
 ≤ ‖ h t 1 − h 1 ‖ ℋ + ‖ h t 2 − h 2 ‖ ℋ → 0 . \displaystyle\leq\left\|h_{t}^{1}-h^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}+\left\|h_{t}^{2}-h^{2}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\to 0. 
 This completes the proof.
∎ 
 Lemma D.2 (Pathwise derivative of Ψ \Psi Luedtke and Chung, 2024 , Ex. 3) . 
 Recall that (1) the Sobolev kernel k : 𝒴 × 𝒴 → ℝ k:\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R} is bounded, symmetric, and positive definite and (2) the model 𝒫 \mathcal{P} satisfies strong positivity. Then, the parameter Ψ : 𝒫 → ℋ × ℋ \Psi:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H} is pathwise differentiable at all P ∈ 𝒫 P\in\mathcal{P} with local parameter D ​ Ψ P : 𝒫 ˙ P → ℋ × ℋ D\Psi_{P}:\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{P}\to\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H} given by 
 D ​ Ψ P ​ ( s ) = [ D ​ ψ P 1 ​ ( s ) D ​ ψ P 0 ​ ( s ) ] = [ ∫ 𝒳 ∫ 𝒴 k ​ ( y , ⋅ ) ​ [ s Y | A , X ​ ( y |  1 , x ) + s X ​ ( x ) ] ​ P Y | A , X ​ ( d ​ y |  1 , x ) ​ P X ​ ( d ​ x ) ∫ 𝒳 ∫ 𝒴 k ​ ( y , ⋅ ) ​ [ s Y | A , X ​ ( y |  0 , x ) + s X ​ ( x ) ] ​ P Y | A , X ​ ( d ​ y |  0 , x ) ​ P X ​ ( d ​ x ) ] . D\Psi_{P}(s)=\begin{bmatrix}D{\psi}^{1}_{P}(s)\\
D{\psi}^{0}_{P}(s)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}k(y,\cdot)\left[s_{Y\,|\,A,X}(y\,|\,1,x)+s_{X}(x)\right]\,P_{Y\,|\,A,X}(dy\,|\,1,x)\,P_{X}(dx)\\
\int_{\mathcal{X}}\int_{\mathcal{Y}}k(y,\cdot)\left[s_{Y\,|\,A,X}(y\,|\,0,x)+s_{X}(x)\right]\,P_{Y\,|\,A,X}(dy\,|\,0,x)\,P_{X}(dx)\end{bmatrix}. 
 (35) 
 Finally, we combine three ingredients through a chain rule: the Hadamard differentiability of the Sinkhorn divergence S ε S_{\varepsilon} , the Hadamard differentiability of the canonical embedding J J , and the pathwise differentiability of KME
 Ψ \Psi . Together, these results yield the local parameter of the STE.

 D.2 Second-order pathwise differentiability of STE under the null 
 Proof of Thm.   3.2 .. 
 Let P ∈ ℋ 0 P\in\mathscr{H}_{0} , so that P 1 = P 0 P_{1}=P_{0} . Consider the submodel ( P t , t ∈ [ 0 , δ ) ) ⊂ 𝔓 ​ ( P , 𝒫 , s ) (P_{t},t\in[0,\delta))\subset\mathfrak{P}(P,\mathcal{P},s) starting at P 0 = P P_{0}=P . By Lem.   C.8 , the Sinkhorn divergence S ε S_{\varepsilon} is second-order Hadamard differentiable under the null. Since J J is linear, it is also second-order Hadamard differentiable (with derivative zero). For notational convenience, define T ε = S ε ∘ J T_{\varepsilon}=S_{\varepsilon}\circ J . Our goal is to derive the second-order local parameter of 𝒮 \mathscr{S} , which amounts to computing the second derivative of t ↦ 𝒮 ​ ( P t ) t\mapsto\mathscr{S}(P_{t}) at t = 0 t=0 . By definition,

 where Q P , z j = ( ℰ z j − ( ℰ a j , x j − ( ℰ x j − P X ) ​ P A | X ) ​ P Y | A , X ) Q_{P,z_{j}}=(\mathscr{E}_{z_{j}}-(\mathscr{E}_{a_{j},x_{j}}-(\mathscr{E}_{x_{j}}-P_{X})P_{A\,|\,X})P_{Y\,|\,A,X}) .
By definition, the (locally nonparametric) second-order EIF 𝒮 ¨ P : 𝒵 × 𝒵 → ℝ \ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P}:\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R} is the unique function that satisfies 
 D 2 ​ 𝒮 P ​ ( s ) = ∫ ∫ 𝒮 ¨ P ​ ( z 1 , z 2 ) ​ s ​ ( z 1 ) ​ s ​ ( z 2 ) ​ P ​ ( d ​ z 1 ) ​ P ​ ( d ​ z 2 ) , D^{2}\mathscr{S}_{P}(s)=\int\int\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P}(z_{1},z_{2})\,s(z_{1})\,s(z_{2})\,P(dz_{1})\,P(dz_{2}), 
 which yields the claimed form of 𝒮 ¨ P \ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P} and completes the proof.
∎ 
 Appendix E One-Step Estimators of STE 
 Let P ^ \widehat{P} be an initial estimator of P ∗ P^{*} constructed from n n independent samples drawn from P ∗ P^{*} , and let P n P_{n} denote the empirical measure based on another set of n n independent samples from P ∗ P^{*} , taken independently of P ^ \widehat{P} . In this section, we study the asymptotic distributions of the one-step estimator for STE under the alternative hypothesis ( P 1 ∗ ≠ P 0 ∗ ) (P_{1}^{*}\neq P_{0}^{*}) , and of the second-order one-step estimator for STE under the null hypothesis ( P 1 ∗ = P 0 ∗ ) (P_{1}^{*}=P_{0}^{*}) .
Throughout this section, for notational convenience, we write υ ^ a \widehat{\upsilon}_{a} for υ a ( P ^ 1 , P ^ 0 ) \upsilon_{a}^{(\widehat{P}_{1},\widehat{P}_{0})} , υ a ∗ \upsilon_{a}^{*} for υ a ( P 1 ∗ , P 0 ∗ ) \upsilon_{a}^{(P_{1}^{*},P_{0}^{*})} , e ^ ​ ( a ∣ x ) \hat{e}(a\mid x) for e P ^ ​ ( a ∣ x ) e_{\widehat{P}}(a\mid x) , and e ∗ ​ ( a ∣ x ) e^{*}(a\mid x) for e P ∗ ​ ( a ∣ x ) e_{P^{*}}(a\mid x) , for a ∈ { 1 , 0 } a\in\{1,0\} . 
 E.1 Limit theorem for first-order one-step estimator 
 As discussed in Sec.   4 , the one-step estimator 𝒮 ^ = 𝒮 ​ ( P ^ ) + P n ​ 𝒮 ˙ P ^ \widehat{\mathscr{S}}=\mathscr{S}(\widehat{P})+P_{n}\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}} can be expanded as 
 𝒮 ^ − 𝒮 ∗ = ( P n − P ∗ ) ​ ( 𝒮 ˙ P ^ − 𝒮 ˙ P ∗ ) ⏟ 𝒟 n + { 𝒮 ​ ( P ^ ) + P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ˙ P ^ − 𝒮 ​ ( P ∗ ) } ⏟ ℛ n + P n ​ 𝒮 ˙ P ∗ . \widehat{\mathscr{S}}-\mathscr{S}^{*}=\underbrace{(P_{n}-P^{*})(\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}-\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}})}_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}+\underbrace{\left\{\mathscr{S}(\widehat{P})+P^{*}\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}-\mathscr{S}(P^{*})\right\}}_{\mathcal{R}_{n}}+P_{n}\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}. 
 Here, the goal is to show that the drift and remainder terms, 𝒟 n \mathcal{D}_{n} and ℛ n \mathcal{R}_{n} respectively, are both o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) o_{p}(n^{-1/2}) . This is proven in Lem.   E.2 and Lem.   E.3 respectively. 
 We begin with a helper lemma. 
 Lemma E.1 (Empirical process drift bound) . 
 Let P ∈ 𝒫 P\in\mathcal{P} and f n ∈ L 2 ​ ( P ) f_{n}\in L^{2}(P) be a sequence of random measurable functions such that ‖ f n ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ) = o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) \|f_{n}\|_{L^{2}(P)}=o_{p}(n^{-1/2}) . Let P n P_{n} be an empirical distribution based on n n iid samples from P P , independent of f n f_{n} , then ( P n − P ) ​ f n = o P ​ ( n − 1 ) (P_{n}-P)f_{n}=o_{P}(n^{-1}) . 
 Proof. 
 We will show that for every δ > 0 \delta>0 , 
 lim n → ∞ ℙ ​ ( n ​ ( P n − P ) ​ f n > δ ) = 0 , \lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(n(P_{n}-P)f_{n}>\delta\right)=0, 
 where ℙ \mathbb{P} is the probability taken over the measure ( P ∗ ) n (P^{*})^{n} . Let { Z 1 , … , Z n } \{Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}\} be the iid random variables used to construct f n f_{n} and let E n E_{n} denote the event that n ​ ‖ f n ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ) 2 ≤ δ 2 n\left\|f_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}(P^{*})}^{2}\leq\delta^{2} ; E n c E_{n}^{c} be its complement. Then, 
 ℙ ​ ( n ​ ( P n − P ) ​ f n > δ ) \displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(n(P_{n}-P)f_{n}>\delta\right) 
 ≤ ℙ ​ ( { n ​ ( P n − P ) ​ f n > δ } ∩ E n ) + ℙ ​ ( E n c ) \displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{n(P_{n}-P)f_{n}>\delta\right\}\cap E_{n}\right)+\mathbb{P}(E_{n}^{c}) 
 = 𝔼 P ​ [ ℙ ​ ( { n ​ ( P n − P ) ​ f n > δ } ∩ E n | Z 1 , … , Z n ) ] + ℙ ​ ( E n c ) \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{n(P_{n}-P)f_{n}>\delta\right\}\cap E_{n}\,|\,Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}\right)\right]+\mathbb{P}(E_{n}^{c}) 
 (law of total probability) 
 = 𝔼 P ​ [ 𝟙 E n ​ ℙ ​ ( { n ​ ( P n − P ) ​ f n > δ } | Z 1 , … , Z n ) ] + ℙ ​ ( E n c ) . \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{E_{n}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{n(P_{n}-P)f_{n}>\delta\right\}\,|\,Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}\right)\right]+\mathbb{P}(E_{n}^{c}). 
 Since we are given that n ​ ‖ f n ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ) → 𝑝 0 \sqrt{n}\left\|f_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}(P^{*})}\xrightarrow[]{p}0 , we have that lim n → ∞ ℙ ​ ( E n c ) = 0 \lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(E_{n}^{c})=0 . Therefore, we just need to show that the first term on the RHS above converges to 0 as n → ∞ n\to\infty . Consider the integrand in the first term. Using Chebyshev’s inequality, 
 ℙ ​ ( { n ​ ( P n − P ) ​ f n > δ } | Z 1 , … , Z n ) \displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{n(P_{n}-P)f_{n}>\delta\right\}\,|\,Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}\right) 
 ≤ n 2 δ 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ { ( P n − P ) ​ f n } 2 | Z 1 , … , Z n ] \displaystyle\leq\frac{n^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\{(P_{n}-P)f_{n}\right\}^{2}\,|\,Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}\right] 
 = n 2 ​ Var ​ ( ( P n − P ) ​ f n | Z 1 , … , Z n ) δ 2 \displaystyle=\frac{n^{2}\text{Var}\left((P_{n}-P)f_{n}\,|\,Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}\right)}{\delta^{2}} 
 = n ​ Var ​ ( f n | Z 1 , … , Z n ) δ 2 \displaystyle=\frac{n\text{Var}\left(f_{n}\,|\,Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}\right)}{\delta^{2}} 
 ≤ n ​ ‖ f n ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ) 2 δ 2 . \displaystyle\leq\frac{n\left\|f_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}(P)}^{2}}{\delta^{2}}. 
 Because under E n E_{n} , n ​ ‖ f n ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ) 2 ≤ δ n\left\|f_{n}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P^{*})}\leq\delta , 
 U n := 𝟙 E n ​ ℙ ​ ( { n ​ ( P n − P ) ​ f n > δ } | Z 1 , … , Z n ) ≤ min ⁡ { 1 , n ​ ‖ f n ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ) 2 δ 2 } . U_{n}:=\mathbbm{1}_{E_{n}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{n(P_{n}-P)f_{n}>\delta\right\}\,|\,Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}\right)\leq\min\left\{1,\frac{n\left\|f_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}(P^{*})}^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\right\}. 
 Since n ​ ‖ f n ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ) 2 → 𝑝 0 n\left\|f_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}(P^{*})}^{2}\xrightarrow[]{p}0 , we have that U n → 𝑝 0 U_{n}\xrightarrow[]{p}0 and U n U_{n} is uniformly integrable. Using the dominated convergence theorem, we have that 𝔼 ​ [ U n ] → 𝑝 0 \mathbb{E}[U_{n}]\xrightarrow[]{p}0 . This completes the proof.
∎ 
 Recall the definition of expectations operators ( 3 ) and ( D ). We denote p ^ = d ​ P ^ d ​ λ \widehat{p}=\tfrac{d\widehat{P}}{d\lambda} and p ∗ = d ​ P ∗ d ​ λ p^{*}=\tfrac{dP^{*}}{d\lambda} . Further, let p ^ Y | A , X \widehat{p}_{Y\,|\,A,X} and p Y | A , X ∗ p^{*}_{Y\,|\,A,X} be the associated conditional densities. 
 Lemma E.2 (Rate for first-order drift term) . 
 Let P ∗ ∉ ℋ 0 P^{*}\notin\mathscr{H}_{0} and assume that the nuisance estimators satisfy 
 1. 
 ∥ e ^ ( 1 ∣ ⋅ ) − e ∗ ( 1 ∣ ⋅ ) ∥ L 2 ​ ( P X ∗ ) = o p ( 1 ) \left\|\hat{e}(1\mid\cdot\,)-e^{*}(1\mid\cdot\,)\right\|_{L^{2}(P^{*}_{X})}=o_{p}(1) ;

 ∫ ( p ^ Y ∣ A , X ​ ( y ∣ a , x ) − p Y ∣ A , X ∗ ​ ( y ∣ a , x ) ) 2 ​ d ​ λ ​ ( y ) ​ d ​ P X ∗ ​ ( x ) = o p ​ ( 1 ) \mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\limits\kern-0.99998pt}{\textstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits\kern-0.99998pt}{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits\kern-0.81943pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits\kern-0.7361pt}\left(\hat{p}_{Y\mid A,X}(y\mid a,x)-p^{*}_{Y\mid A,X}(y\mid a,x)\right)^{2}\,d\lambda(y)\,dP^{*}_{X}(x)=o_{p}(1) for a ∈ { 1 , 0 } a\in\{1,0\} ;

 ‖ p ^ Y ∣ A , X p Y ∣ A , X ∗ ‖ L r ​ ( P ∗ ) = 𝒪 p ​ ( 1 ) \left\|\frac{\widehat{p}_{Y\mid A,X}}{{p}^{*}_{Y\mid A,X}}\right\|_{L^{r}(P^{*})}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(1) for some r > 1 r>1 . 
 Then, 
 | 𝒟 n | = | ( P n − P ∗ ) ​ ( 𝒮 ˙ P ^ − 𝒮 ˙ P ∗ ) | = o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) . \left|\mathcal{D}_{n}\right|=\left|(P_{n}-P^{*})(\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}-\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}})\right|=o_{p}(n^{-1/2}). 
 The first three assumptions of this lemma impose standard consistency requirements on the three nuisance parameters—the propensity score, the entropic potentials, and the outcome regression—each in their respective norms (Luedtke and Chung, 2024 , App. B.2.5) . Consistency of the entropic potentials (the second assumption) in the L 2 L^{2} norm follows directly from consistency in the Hölder norm established by del Barrio et al. ( 2023 ) . The fourth assumption concerns the empirical likelihood ratio; this condition is mild given that ‖ p ^ Y ∣ A , X / p Y ∣ A , X ∗ ‖ L 1 ​ ( P ∗ ) = 1 \|\widehat{p}_{Y\mid A,X}/{p}^{*}_{Y\mid A,X}\|_{L^{1}(P^{*})}=1 . 
 Proof. 
 By Lem.   E.1 , a sufficient condition for showing that | 𝒟 n | = o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) \left|\mathcal{D}_{n}\right|=o_{p}(n^{-1/2}) is that ‖ 𝒮 ˙ P ^ − 𝒮 ˙ P ∗ ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ) = o p ​ ( 1 ) \|\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}-\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}\|_{L^{2}(P^{*})}=o_{p}(1) , which we establish in what follows. Recall from ( 8 ), the EIF 𝒮 ˙ P \dot{\mathscr{S}}_{P} can be written as 
 𝒮 ˙ P : ( x , a , y ) \displaystyle\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{P}:(x,a,y) 
 ↦ f P − P Y ∣ A , X ​ f P + P A , Y ∣ X ​ f P − P ​ f P , f P ​ ( x , a , y ) = υ a ( P 1 , P 0 ) ​ ( y ) e P ​ ( a ∣ x ) . \displaystyle\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,1}f_{P}}-{\color[rgb]{.75,0,.25}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{.75,0,.25}P_{Y\mid A,X}f_{P}}+{\color[rgb]{0,.5,.5}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,.5,.5}P_{A,Y\mid X}f_{P}}-{\color[rgb]{.75,.5,.25}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{.75,.5,.25}Pf_{P}},\quad f_{P}(x,a,y)=\frac{\upsilon_{a}^{(P_{1},P_{0})}(y)}{e_{P}(a\mid x)}. 
 Let E > 0 E>0 be the strong positivity constant, that is inf P ∈ 𝒫 ess ​ inf ( a , x ) ⁡ e P ​ ( a ∣ x ) > E \inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\operatorname*{ess\,inf}_{(a,x)}e_{P}(a\mid x)>E .

 by assumptions 1 and 2 of the lemma, and uniform boundedness of ‖ υ a ( μ , ν ) ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \|\upsilon_{a}^{(\mu,\nu)}\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})} .  Lemma E.3 (Rate for first-order remainder term) . 
 Let P ∗ ∉ ℋ 0 P^{*}\notin\mathscr{H}_{0} and ‖ n r ​ ( P ^ a − P a ∗ ) ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) = 𝒪 p ​ ( 1 ) \|n^{r}(\widehat{P}_{a}-P^{*}_{a})\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(1) for a ∈ { 1 , 0 } a\in\{1,0\} and some r > 1 / 4 r>1/4 .  From assumption, the second and third additive terms are o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) o_{p}(n^{-1/2}) . The assumption is valid because the term is a product of estimation error of two nuisance parameters - propensity score e P ∗ e_{P^{*}} and outcome regression P Y | A , X ∗ P^{*}_{Y\,|\,A,X} . The requirement of o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) o_{p}(n^{-1/2}) convergence rate for this double product is standard. Therefore, now ignoring the established o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) o_{p}(n^{-1/2}) term, it remains to show that 
 S ε ​ ( P ^ 1 , P ^ 0 ) − S ε ​ ( P 1 ∗ , P 0 ∗ ) − [ ( P 1 ∗ − P ^ 1 ) ​ υ 1 + ( P 0 ∗ − P ^ 0 ) ​ υ 0 ] = o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) . S_{\varepsilon}(\widehat{P}_{1},\widehat{P}_{0})-S_{\varepsilon}(P_{1}^{*},P_{0}^{*})-\left[(P_{1}^{*}-\widehat{P}_{1})\upsilon_{1}+(P_{0}^{*}-\widehat{P}_{0})\upsilon_{0}\right]=o_{p}(n^{-1/2}). 
 (36) 
 The above is true via the Hadamard differentiability of ( α , β ) ↦ S ε ​ ( α , β ) (\alpha,\beta)\mapsto S_{\varepsilon}(\alpha,\beta) .
For any ( μ , ν ) ∈ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) (\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) and ( γ 1 , γ 2 ) ∈ ℳ 0 , μ × ℳ 0 , μ (\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{0,\mu}\times\mathcal{M}_{0,\mu} , consider paths of the form μ t = μ + t ​ γ t 1 \mu_{t}=\mu+t\gamma_{t}^{1} and ν t = ν + t ​ γ t 2 \nu_{t}=\nu+t\gamma_{t}^{2} such that γ t 1 → γ 1 \gamma_{t}^{1}\to\gamma_{1} and γ t 2 → γ 2 \gamma_{t}^{2}\to\gamma_{2} as t ↓ 0 t\downarrow 0 in ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) . Because ( α , β ) ↦ S ε ​ ( α , β ) (\alpha,\beta)\mapsto S_{\varepsilon}(\alpha,\beta) is second-order Hadamard differentiable with a bounded second-order Hadamard derivative, the following first-order expansion is immediate from the definition 
 S ε ​ ( μ t , ν t ) − S ε ​ ( μ , ν ) − S ε ′ ​ [ μ , ν ] ​ ( μ t − μ , ν t − ν ) = O ​ ( t 2 ) . S_{\varepsilon}(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})-S_{\varepsilon}(\mu,\nu)-S_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}[\mu,\nu](\mu_{t}-\mu,\nu_{t}-\nu)=O(t^{2}). 
 Plugging in the form of S ε ′ ​ [ μ , ν ] S_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}[\mu,\nu] from Lem.   C.4 , we have 
 S ε ​ ( μ t , ν t ) − S ε ​ ( μ , ν ) − [ ( μ t − μ ) ​ υ 1 ( μ , ν ) + ( ν t − ν ) ​ υ 0 ( μ , ν ) ] = O ​ ( t 2 ) . S_{\varepsilon}(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})-S_{\varepsilon}(\mu,\nu)-\left[(\mu_{t}-\mu)\upsilon_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}+(\nu_{t}-\nu)\upsilon_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}\right]=O(t^{2}).

 S ε ​ ( μ t , ν t ) − S ε ​ ( μ , ν ) − t ​ γ t 1 ​ υ 1 ( μ t , ν t ) − t ​ γ t 2 ​ υ 0 ( μ t , ν t ) = t ​ γ t 1 ​ ( υ 1 ( μ , ν ) − υ 1 ( μ t , ν t ) ) + t ​ γ t 2 ​ ( υ 0 ( μ , ν ) − υ 0 ( μ t , ν t ) ) + O ​ ( t 2 ) . S_{\varepsilon}(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})-S_{\varepsilon}(\mu,\nu)-t\gamma_{t}^{1}\upsilon_{1}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}-t\gamma_{t}^{2}\upsilon_{0}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})}=t\gamma_{t}^{1}(\upsilon_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}-\upsilon_{1}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})})+t\gamma_{t}^{2}(\upsilon_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}-\upsilon_{0}^{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})})+O(t^{2}). 
 From the Hadamard differentiability of the map ( α , β ) ↦ ( υ 1 ( α , β ) , υ 0 ( α , β ) ) (\alpha,\beta)\mapsto(\upsilon_{1}^{(\alpha,\beta)},\upsilon_{0}^{(\alpha,\beta)}) at ( μ , ν ) (\mu,\nu) and boundedness of the derivative, we have that the RHS in the above display is O ​ ( t 2 ) O(t^{2}) . If ‖ n r ​ ( P ^ a − P a ∗ ) ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) = 𝒪 p ​ ( 1 ) \|n^{r}(\widehat{P}_{a}-P^{*}_{a})\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(1) for a ∈ { 1 , 0 } a\in\{1,0\} , then by functional delta method, we get that ℛ n \mathcal{R}_{n} is 𝒪 p ​ ( n − 2 ​ r ) \mathcal{O}_{p}(n^{-2r}) . This completes the proof for any r > 1 / 4 r>1/4 .

 E.2 Limit theorem for second-order one-step estimator 
 This section proves Thm.   4.1 by proving that all three terms 𝒟 n \mathscr{D}_{n} , 𝒰 n \mathscr{U}_{n} , and ℛ n \mathscr{R}_{n} are o p ​ ( n − 1 ) o_{p}(n^{-1}) .
Throughout this section, for the sake of notational simplicity, we suppress superscripts and write υ ^ 1 \widehat{\upsilon}_{1} for υ 1 ( P ^ 1 , P ^ 0 ) \upsilon_{1}^{(\widehat{P}_{1},\widehat{P}_{0})} and υ ^ 0 \widehat{\upsilon}_{0} for υ 0 ( P ^ 1 , P ^ 0 ) \upsilon_{0}^{(\widehat{P}_{1},\widehat{P}_{0})} . Similarly, we suppress the subscripts and write e ^ \hat{e} for e P ^ e_{\widehat{P}} and e ∗ {e}^{*} for e P ∗ e_{P^{*}} . Recall the expectation operators defined in ( 3 ). Define the operators Q P ^ Q_{\widehat{P}} and Q P ∗ , P ^ Q_{P^{*},\widehat{P}} as

 First, we study the stability of the Sinkhorn operator. 
 Lemma E.4 (Stability of Sinkhorn Operator) .
