 Step 1: Control (i). 
 We can uniformly upperbound the operator norm ‖ ( I − T μ ^ 2 ) − 1 ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ \|{(I-T_{\hat{\mu}}^{2})^{-1}}\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R}} using uniform bounds on the spectrum of T μ ^ : 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ T_{\hat{\mu}}:\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R} in Lavenant et al. ( 2024 , Prop. 3.7) and the Neumann series expansion of ( I − T μ ^ 2 ) − 1 (I-T_{\hat{\mu}}^{2})^{-1} . This shows that there exists q ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) q\in(0,1) such that ‖ ( I − T ν 2 ) − 1 ‖ ≤ 1 / ( 1 − q 2 ) \|(I-T_{\nu}^{2})^{-1}\|\leq 1/(1-q^{2}) on 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R} for all ν \nu .
Now it remains to control the term ‖ H μ ^ − H μ ‖ ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \|H_{\hat{\mu}}-H_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y})} . First, trivially, ‖ H μ ^ − H μ ‖ ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ≤ ‖ H μ ^ − H μ ‖ ℳ ​ ( 𝒴 ) → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \|H_{\hat{\mu}}-H_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})}\leq\|H_{\hat{\mu}}-H_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})} . Then, for any γ ∈ ℳ ​ ( 𝒴 ) \gamma\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{Y}) , 
 ‖ H μ ^ ​ γ − H μ ​ γ ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \displaystyle\|H_{\hat{\mu}}\gamma-H_{\mu}\gamma\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})} 
 = ‖ b μ ^ ​ 𝔤 ​ ( M b μ ^ ​ γ ) − b μ ​ 𝔤 ​ ( M b μ ​ γ ) ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \displaystyle=\left\|b_{\hat{\mu}}\mathfrak{g}(M_{b_{\hat{\mu}}}\gamma)-b_{{\mu}}\mathfrak{g}(M_{b_{{\mu}}}\gamma)\right\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})} 
 ≤ ‖ b μ ^ − b μ ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ​ ‖ 𝔤 ​ ( M b μ ^ ​ γ ) ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) + ‖ b μ ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ​ ‖ 𝔤 ​ ( M b μ ^ ​ γ ) − 𝔤 ​ ( M b μ ​ γ ) ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \displaystyle\leq\left\|b_{\hat{\mu}}-b_{{\mu}}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})}\left\|\mathfrak{g}(M_{b_{\hat{\mu}}}\gamma)\right\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})}+\left\|b_{{\mu}}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})}\left\|\mathfrak{g}(M_{b_{\hat{\mu}}}\gamma)-\mathfrak{g}(M_{b_{{\mu}}}\gamma)\right\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})} 
 ≤ ‖ b μ ^ − b μ ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ​ ‖ 𝔤 ​ ( M b μ ^ ​ γ ) ‖ 𝒢 + ‖ b μ ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ​ ‖ 𝔤 ​ ( M b μ ^ ​ γ ) − 𝔤 ​ ( M b μ ​ γ ) ‖ 𝒢 . \displaystyle\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,1}\left\|b_{\hat{\mu}}-b_{{\mu}}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})}}{\color[rgb]{0,.5,.5}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,.5,.5}\left\|\mathfrak{g}(M_{b_{\hat{\mu}}}\gamma)\right\|_{\mathcal{G}}}+\left\|b_{{\mu}}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})}{\color[rgb]{.75,0,.25}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{.75,0,.25}\left\|\mathfrak{g}(M_{b_{\hat{\mu}}}\gamma)-\mathfrak{g}(M_{b_{{\mu}}}\gamma)\right\|_{\mathcal{G}}}. 
 The last inequality follows from the fact that, for any f ∈ 𝒢 f\in\mathcal{G} , ‖ f ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) = sup y | f ​ ( y ) | ≤ ‖ f ‖ 𝒢 ​ sup y g ε ​ ( y , y ) = ‖ f ‖ 𝒢 \|f\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})}=\sup_{y}|f(y)|\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{G}}\sup_{y}\sqrt{g_{\varepsilon}(y,y)}=\|f\|_{\mathcal{G}} . We tackle the terms above separately. 
 • 
 Controlling ‖ 𝔤 ​ ( M b μ ^ ​ γ ) ‖ 𝒢 {\color[rgb]{0,.5,.5}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,.5,.5}\left\|\mathfrak{g}(M_{b_{\hat{\mu}}}\gamma)\right\|_{\mathcal{G}}} . We can bound the ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1}) norm of measures { M b ν ​ γ , ν ∈ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) } \{M_{b_{\nu}}\gamma,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\} by a uniform constant times ‖ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) \|\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})} . Consider the set of functions in 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) , U := { b ν : ν ∈ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) } U:=\{{b_{\nu}}:\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\} . We know that { b ν : ν ∈ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) } \{b_{\nu}:\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\} is a uniformly bounded subset of 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) in supnorm (Goldfeld et al. , 2024 , Lem. 1) . Let C = sup ν ‖ b ν ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) C=\sup_{\nu}\|b_{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})} .
We now show that U U is a compact subset of 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) . Indeed, 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) is tight, and hence Prokhorov’s theorem (Billingsley, 2013 , Thm. 5.1) gives relative compactness. Since weak limits of probability measures on 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} are again probability measures, 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) is closed, and therefore compact. The map ν ↦ b ν \nu\mapsto{b_{\nu}} is continuous from 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) (equipped with the topology of weak convergence) into 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) (Goldfeld et al. , 2024 , Lem. 1) .
Therefore, it follows that U U is the continuous image of a compact set. Hence U U is compact in 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) . At this point, we can use Kokot and Luedtke ( 2025 , Lem. 22) to show that there exists a constant C 1 > 0 C_{1}>0 such that for all γ ∈ ℳ ​ ( 𝒴 ) ⊂ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) \gamma\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{Y})\subset\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1}) and ν ∈ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) , 
 ‖ M b ν ​ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) ≤ C 1 ​ ‖ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) . \|M_{b_{\nu}}\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})}\leq C_{1}\|\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})}. 
 Therefore, ‖ 𝔤 ​ ( M b μ ^ ​ γ ) ‖ 𝒢 ≤ C 1 ​ ‖ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) {\color[rgb]{0,.5,.5}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,.5,.5}\left\|\mathfrak{g}(M_{b_{\hat{\mu}}}\gamma)\right\|_{\mathcal{G}}}\leq C_{1}\|\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})} . 
 • 
 Controlling ‖ b μ ^ − b μ ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,1}\left\|b_{\hat{\mu}}-b_{{\mu}}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})}} . From Goldfeld et al. ( 2024 , Thm. 3) , we know that the map Φ : 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) × 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \Phi:\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) given by ( μ , ν ) ↦ ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) , φ 0 ( μ , ν ) ) (\mu,\nu)\mapsto(\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)},\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}) is Hadamard differentiable. Further the function b : 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) b:\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) given by u ↦ exp ⁡ ( u / ε ) u\mapsto\exp(u/\varepsilon) is Fréchet differentiable on 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) with derivative given by the linear operator b ′ ​ [ u ] ​ ( h ) = 1 ε ​ exp ⁡ ( u / ε ) ​ h b^{\prime}[u](h)=\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\exp(u/\varepsilon)h . Hence b b is Hadamard differentiable and by chain rule, the mapping ( μ , ν ) ↦ ( exp ⁡ ( φ 1 ( μ , ν ) / ε ) , exp ⁡ ( φ 0 ( μ , ν ) / ε ) ) (\mu,\nu)\mapsto(\exp(\varphi_{1}^{(\mu,\nu)}/\varepsilon),\exp(\varphi_{0}^{(\mu,\nu)}/\varepsilon)) is Hadamard differentiable. 
 This implies that the function μ ↦ b μ \mu\mapsto b_{\mu} is Hadamard differentiable and there exists a continuous linear operator b ′ ​ [ μ ] : 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) b^{\prime}[\mu]:\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) such that along the path μ t = μ + t ​ γ t \mu_{t}=\mu+t\gamma_{t} with γ t → γ \gamma_{t}\to\gamma in ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) for some γ ∈ ℳ 0 , μ \gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{0,\mu} , 
 lim t → 0 ‖ b μ t − b μ − t ​ b ′ ​ [ μ ] ​ ( γ t ) ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) t = 0 . \lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\left\|b_{\mu_{t}}-b_{\mu}-tb^{\prime}[\mu](\gamma_{t})\right\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})}}{t}=0. 
 By functional delta theorem, in supremum norm, 
 n r ​ ( b μ ^ − b μ ) = b ′ ​ [ μ ] ​ ( n r ​ ( μ ^ − μ ) ) + o p ​ ( 1 ) . n^{r}(b_{\hat{\mu}}-b_{\mu})=b^{\prime}[\mu](n^{r}(\hat{\mu}-\mu))+o_{p}(1). 
 Because b ′ ​ [ μ ] b^{\prime}[\mu] is a tight continuous operator, b ′ ​ [ μ ] ​ ( n r ​ ( μ ^ − μ ) ) b^{\prime}[\mu](n^{r}(\hat{\mu}-\mu)) is tight, and therefore ‖ ( b μ ^ − b μ ) ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) = 𝒪 p ​ ( n − r ) {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,1}\|(b_{\hat{\mu}}-b_{\mu})\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})}}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(n^{-r}) . 
 • 
 Controlling ‖ 𝔤 ​ ( M b μ ^ ​ γ ) − 𝔤 ​ ( M b μ ​ γ ) ‖ 𝒢 {\color[rgb]{.75,0,.25}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{.75,0,.25}\left\|\mathfrak{g}(M_{b_{\hat{\mu}}}\gamma)-\mathfrak{g}(M_{b_{{\mu}}}\gamma)\right\|_{\mathcal{G}}} . By Kokot and Luedtke ( 2025 , Cor. 23) , ‖ b μ ^ − b μ ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) = 𝒪 p ​ ( n − r ) \|b_{\hat{\mu}}-b_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(n^{-r}) immediately implies that ‖ M b μ ^ − M b μ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) → ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) = 𝒪 p ​ ( n − r ) \left\|M_{b_{\hat{\mu}}}-M_{b_{{\mu}}}\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})\to\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(n^{-r}) .
As a consequence, ‖ 𝔤 ​ ( M b μ ^ ​ γ ) − 𝔤 ​ ( M b μ ​ γ ) ‖ 𝒢 = 𝒪 p ​ ( n − r ) ​ ‖ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) {\color[rgb]{.75,0,.25}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{.75,0,.25}\left\|\mathfrak{g}(M_{b_{\hat{\mu}}}\gamma)-\mathfrak{g}(M_{b_{{\mu}}}\gamma)\right\|_{\mathcal{G}}}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(n^{-r})\|\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})} . 
 Altogether, term (i) is 𝒪 p ​ ( n − r ) ​ ‖ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) \mathcal{O}_{p}(n^{-r})\|\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})} . 
 Step 2: Control (ii). 
 We bound ‖ H μ ‖ ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \|H_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})} as following. For any γ ∈ ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ⊂ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) \gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y})\subset\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1}) , 
 ⟨ γ , H μ ​ γ ⟩ = ‖ M b μ ​ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) 2 ≤ C 1 2 ​ ‖ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) 2 . \left\langle\gamma,H_{\mu}\gamma\right\rangle=\|M_{b_{\mu}}\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})}^{2}\leq C_{1}^{2}\|\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})}^{2}. 
 Therefore, the operator norm must be uniformly bounded.
For the difference term, we consider the following identity 
 ‖ ( I − T μ ^ 2 ) − 1 − ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 ‖ \displaystyle\left\|(I-T_{\hat{\mu}}^{2})^{-1}-(I-T_{{\mu}}^{2})^{-1}\right\| 
 = ‖ ( I − T μ ^ 2 ) − 1 ​ [ T μ 2 − T μ ^ 2 ] ​ ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 ‖ \displaystyle=\left\|(I-T_{\hat{\mu}}^{2})^{-1}\left[T_{\mu}^{2}-T_{\hat{\mu}}^{2}\right](I-T_{{\mu}}^{2})^{-1}\right\| 
 ≤ ‖ ( I − T μ ^ 2 ) − 1 ‖ ​ ‖ ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 ‖ ​ ‖ T μ ^ 2 − T μ 2 ‖ \displaystyle\leq\left\|(I-T_{\hat{\mu}}^{2})^{-1}\right\|\left\|(I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1}\right\|\left\|T_{\hat{\mu}}^{2}-T_{\mu}^{2}\right\| 
 ≤ ‖ ( I − T μ ^ 2 ) − 1 ‖ ​ ‖ ( I − T μ 2 ) − 1 ‖ ​ ( ‖ T μ ^ ‖ + ‖ T μ ‖ ) ​ ‖ T μ ^ − T μ ‖ , \displaystyle\leq\left\|(I-T_{\hat{\mu}}^{2})^{-1}\right\|\,\left\|(I-T_{\mu}^{2})^{-1}\right\|\,\left(\left\|T_{\hat{\mu}}\right\|+\left\|T_{\mu}\right\|\right)\,\left\|T_{\hat{\mu}}-T_{\mu}\right\|, 
 where all operator norms are on the common function space 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R} . As in Step 1, the operator norm of ( I − T μ ^ 2 ) − 1 (I-T_{\hat{\mu}}^{2})^{-1} is uniformly bounded on 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R} . Lavenant et al. ( 2024 , Prop. 3.7) show that the operator norm of T ν T_{\nu} is uniformly bounded by 1 1 for all ν ∈ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}) . Since centering is a contraction, ‖ T μ ^ − T μ ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ ≤ ‖ T μ ^ − T μ ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \|{T_{\hat{\mu}}-T_{\mu}}\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R}}\leq\|T_{\hat{\mu}}-T_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})} . For any f f inside a unit ball in 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) , 
 ‖ ( T μ ^ − T μ ) ​ f ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \displaystyle\left\|(T_{\hat{\mu}}-T_{\mu})f\right\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})} 
 ≤ ‖ H μ ^ ​ M f ​ μ ^ − H μ ​ M f ​ μ ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \displaystyle\leq\|H_{\hat{\mu}}M_{f}\hat{\mu}-H_{{\mu}}M_{f}{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})} 
 ≤ ‖ ( H μ ^ − H μ ) ​ M f ​ μ ^ + H μ ​ M f ​ ( μ ^ − μ ) ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \displaystyle\leq\|(H_{\hat{\mu}}-H_{\mu})M_{f}\hat{\mu}+H_{\mu}M_{f}(\hat{\mu}-\mu)\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})} 
 ≤ ‖ H μ ^ − H μ ‖ ℳ ​ ( 𝒴 ) → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ​ ‖ M f ​ μ ^ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) + ‖ H μ ‖ ℳ ​ ( 𝒴 ) → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ​ ‖ M f ​ ( μ ^ − μ ) ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) . \displaystyle\leq\|H_{\hat{\mu}}-H_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})}\|M_{f}\hat{\mu}\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})}+\|H_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})}\|M_{f}(\hat{\mu}-\mu)\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})}. 
 Since f f belongs to a compact subset in 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) , again by Kokot and Luedtke ( 2025 , Lem. 22) , ‖ M f ​ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) ≤ C 1 ​ ‖ γ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) \|M_{f}\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})}\leq C_{1}\|\gamma\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})} . Using this and the fact that ‖ H μ ‖ ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ≤ C 1 2 \|H_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})}\leq C_{1}^{2} , 
 ‖ ( T μ ^ − T μ ) ​ f ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \displaystyle\left\|(T_{\hat{\mu}}-T_{\mu})f\right\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})} 
 ≲ ‖ H μ ^ − H μ ‖ ℳ ​ ( 𝒴 ) → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) + ‖ μ ^ − μ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( 𝒢 1 ) = 𝒪 p ​ ( n − r ) . \displaystyle\lesssim\|H_{\hat{\mu}}-H_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})}+\|\hat{\mu}-\mu\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{G}_{1})}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(n^{-r}). 
 Together, terms (i) and (ii) are 𝒪 p ​ ( n − r ) \mathcal{O}_{p}(n^{-r}) and this completes the proof.
∎ 
 Lemma E.5 (Rate for second-order drift term) . 
 Let P ∗ ∈ ℋ 0 P^{*}\in\mathscr{H}_{0} and assume that ‖ n r ​ ( μ ^ − μ ) ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) = 𝒪 p ​ ( 1 ) \|n^{r}(\hat{\mu}-\mu)\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(1) for some r > 1 / 4 r>1/4 . Further, assume that the estimated nuisance parameters satisfy 
 P X ∗ ​ [ x ↦ ( e P ^ ​ ( a | x ) e P ∗ ​ ( a | x ) − 1 ) ​ ( P ^ Y | a , x − P Y | a , x ∗ ) ​ k P ^ 1 ​ ( ⋅ , y ) ] = o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) , \displaystyle P^{*}_{X}\left[x\mapsto\left(\frac{e_{\widehat{P}}(a\,|\,x)}{e_{P^{*}}(a\,|\,x)}-1\right)\big({\widehat{P}_{Y\,|\,a,x}-P^{*}_{Y\,|\,a,x}}\big)k_{\widehat{P}_{1}}(\cdot,y)\right]=o_{p}(n^{-1/2}), 
 uniformly in y ∈ 𝒴 y\in\mathcal{Y} and a ∈ { 1 , 0 } a\in\{1,0\} . Then the drift term satisfies

 Proof. 
 Since P ^ \widehat{P} and P n P_{n} are constructed from independent samples, the random function 𝒮 ˙ P ^ ​ ( ⋅ ) + ∫ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ ​ ( ⋅ , z ) ​ d ​ P ∗ ​ ( z ) \dot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}(\cdot)+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\limits\kern-0.99998pt}{\textstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits\kern-0.99998pt}{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits\kern-0.81943pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits\kern-0.7361pt}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}(\cdot,z)\,dP^{*}(z) is independent of P n P_{n} . By Lem.   E.1 , it suffices to show that ‖ 𝒮 ˙ P ^ ​ ( ⋅ ) + ∫ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ ​ ( ⋅ , z ) ​ d ​ P ∗ ​ ( z ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ) = o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) \big\|{\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}(\cdot)+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\limits\kern-0.99998pt}{\textstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits\kern-0.99998pt}{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits\kern-0.81943pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits\kern-0.7361pt}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}(\cdot,z)\,dP^{*}(z)}\big\|_{L^{2}(P^{*})}=o_{p}(n^{-1/2}) to show that 𝒟 n = o p ​ ( n − 1 ) \mathscr{D}_{n}=o_{p}(n^{-1}) .
Recall from ( 8 ) and ( 10 ) the formulations of 𝒮 ˙ P ^ \dot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}} and 𝒮 ¨ P ^ \ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}} . Using Q Q operator notation in ( 37 ), at evaluation points z j = ( x j , a j , y j ) z_{j}=(x_{j},a_{j},y_{j}) for j = 1 , 2 j=1,2 in 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} , we have that 
 𝒮 ˙ P ^ \displaystyle\dot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}} 
 = Q P ^ ​ f P ^ , f P ^ ​ ( z 1 ) = a 1 e ^ ​ ( 1 ∣ x 1 ) ​ υ ^ 1 ​ ( y 1 ) + 1 − a 1 e ^ ​ ( 0 ∣ x 1 ) ​ υ ^ 0 ​ ( y 1 ) , \displaystyle=Q_{\widehat{P}}f_{\widehat{P}},\quad f_{\widehat{P}}(z_{1})=\frac{a_{1}}{\hat{e}(1\mid x_{1})}\widehat{\upsilon}_{1}(y_{1})+\frac{1-a_{1}}{\hat{e}(0\mid x_{1})}\widehat{\upsilon}_{0}(y_{1}),

Note that, with k P ^ 1 ​ ( y , ⋅ ) ⊗ ω P ^ : ( y ′ , a ′ , x ′ ) ↦ k P ^ 1 ​ ( y , y ′ ) ⊗ ω P ^ ​ ( a ′ , x ′ ) k_{\widehat{P}_{1}}(y,\cdot)\otimes\omega_{\widehat{P}}:(y^{\prime},a^{\prime},x^{\prime})\mapsto k_{\widehat{P}_{1}}(y,y^{\prime})\otimes\omega_{\widehat{P}}(a^{\prime},x^{\prime}) ,

 The last two terms in both ζ 1 \zeta_{1} and ζ 2 \zeta_{2} involve products of estimation errors for propensity score e P ∗ e_{P^{*}} and the outcome regression P Y | A , X ∗ P^{*}_{Y\,|\,A,X} . By assumption, these terms are o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) o_{p}(n^{-1/2}) uniformly over y ∈ 𝒴 y\in\mathcal{Y} , and hence negligible. It therefore remains to control the leading components: υ ^ 1 − K P ^ 1 ​ ( P ^ 1 − P ^ 0 ) \widehat{\upsilon}_{1}-K_{\widehat{P}_{1}}(\widehat{P}_{1}-\widehat{P}_{0}) and υ ^ 0 + K P ^ 1 ​ ( P ^ 1 − P ^ 0 ) \widehat{\upsilon}_{0}+K_{\widehat{P}_{1}}(\widehat{P}_{1}-\widehat{P}_{0}) . Consider the breakdown 
 υ ^ 1 − K P ^ 1 ​ ( P ^ 1 − P ^ 0 ) = υ ^ 1 − K P 1 ∗ ​ ( P ^ 1 − P ^ 0 ) + ( K P 1 ∗ − K P ^ 1 ) ​ ( P ^ 1 − P 1 ∗ ) − ( K P 1 ∗ − K P ^ 1 ) ​ ( P ^ 0 − P 0 ∗ ) , \widehat{\upsilon}_{1}-K_{\widehat{P}_{1}}(\widehat{P}_{1}-\widehat{P}_{0})=\widehat{\upsilon}_{1}-K_{P_{1}^{*}}(\widehat{P}_{1}-\widehat{P}_{0})+(K_{P_{1}^{*}}-K_{\widehat{P}_{1}})(\widehat{P}_{1}-P_{1}^{*})-(K_{P_{1}^{*}}-K_{\widehat{P}_{1}})(\widehat{P}_{0}-P_{0}^{*}), 
 and 
 υ ^ 0 + K P ^ 1 ​ ( P ^ 1 − P ^ 0 ) = υ ^ 0 + K P 1 ∗ ​ ( P ^ 1 − P ^ 0 ) − ( K P 1 ∗ − K P ^ 1 ) ​ ( P ^ 1 − P 1 ∗ ) + ( K P 1 ∗ − K P ^ 1 ) ​ ( P ^ 0 − P 0 ∗ ) . \widehat{\upsilon}_{0}+K_{\widehat{P}_{1}}(\widehat{P}_{1}-\widehat{P}_{0})=\widehat{\upsilon}_{0}+K_{P_{1}^{*}}(\widehat{P}_{1}-\widehat{P}_{0})-(K_{P_{1}^{*}}-K_{\widehat{P}_{1}})(\widehat{P}_{1}-P_{1}^{*})+(K_{P_{1}^{*}}-K_{\widehat{P}_{1}})(\widehat{P}_{0}-P_{0}^{*}). 
 From Lem.   E.4 , we have that for each i , j ∈ { 1 , 0 } i,j\in\{1,0\} 
 ‖ ( K P ^ i − K P i ∗ ) ​ ( P ^ j − P j ∗ ) ‖ 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) ≤ ‖ K P ^ i − K P i ∗ ‖ ℳ 0 ​ ( 𝒴 ) → 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) / ℝ ​ ‖ P ^ j − P j ∗ ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ ) = 𝒪 p ​ ( n − 2 ​ r ) . \left\|(K_{\widehat{P}_{i}}-K_{P_{i}^{*}})(\widehat{P}_{j}-P^{*}_{j})\right\|_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})}\leq\left\|K_{\widehat{P}_{i}}-K_{P_{i}^{*}}\right\|_{\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y})/\mathbb{R}}\left\|\widehat{P}_{j}-P^{*}_{j}\right\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H})}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(n^{-2r}). 
 Since r > 1 / 4 r>1/4 , we have that ( K P ^ 1 − K P 1 ∗ ) ​ ( P ^ a − P a ∗ ) (K_{\widehat{P}_{1}}-K_{P_{1}^{*}})(\widehat{P}_{a}-P_{a}^{*}) is o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) o_{p}(n^{-1/2}) in the L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ) L^{2}(P^{*}) sense.
Finally, by Cor.   C.7 , the quantities υ ^ 1 − K P 1 ∗ ​ ( P ^ 1 − P ^ 0 ) \widehat{\upsilon}_{1}-K_{P_{1}^{*}}(\widehat{P}_{1}-\widehat{P}_{0}) and υ ^ 0 + K P 1 ∗ ​ ( P ^ 1 − P ^ 0 ) \widehat{\upsilon}_{0}+K_{P_{1}^{*}}(\widehat{P}_{1}-\widehat{P}_{0}) constitute the second-order remainder terms in the Hadamard expansion of the centered entropic potentials. From assumption, ‖ n r ​ ( P ^ 1 − P 1 ∗ ) ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \|n^{r}(\widehat{P}_{1}-P_{1}^{*})\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})} and ‖ n r ​ ( P ^ 0 − P 0 ∗ ) ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \|n^{r}(\widehat{P}_{0}-P_{0}^{*})\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})} are 𝒪 p ​ ( 1 ) \mathcal{O}_{p}(1) for some r > 1 / 4 r>1/4 . Then by functional delta theorem and second-order Hadamard differentiability of entropic potentials (Goldfeld et al. , 2024 , Theorem 4) , these remainders are 𝒪 p ​ ( n − 2 ​ r ) \mathcal{O}_{p}(n^{-2r}) in 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒴 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Y}) topology, and hence o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) o_{p}(n^{-1/2}) in L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ) L^{2}(P^{*}) norm.
∎ 
 Lemma E.6 (Rate for second-order U-process term) . 
 If ‖ C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ − C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ⊗ P ∗ ) → 𝑝 0 \|C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}-C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}\|_{L^{2}(P^{*}\otimes P^{*})}\xrightarrow[]{p}0 , then the U-process term 
 𝒰 n = 1 2 ​ 𝕌 n ​ ( C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ − C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ ) = o p ​ ( n − 1 ) . \mathscr{U}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{U}_{n}\left(C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}-C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}\right)=o_{p}(n^{-1}). 
 Proof. 
 Denote f n := C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ − C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ f_{n}:=C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}-C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}} . We will show that for every δ > 0 \delta>0 , 
 ℙ ​ ( | n ​ 𝕌 n ​ f n | > δ ) ≤ C δ 2 ​ ‖ f n ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ⊗ P ∗ ) 2 . \mathbb{P}\left(\left|n\mathbb{U}_{n}f_{n}\right|>\delta\right)\leq\frac{C}{\delta^{2}}{\|f_{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P^{*}\otimes P^{*})}}. 
 Because C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}} is P ∗ P^{*} -degenerate, 𝔼 P ∗ ​ [ U n ​ f n | P ^ ] = 0 \mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\left[U_{n}f_{n}\,|\,\widehat{P}\right]=0 . Let Z 1 , … , Z n Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n} be the iid samples from P ∗ P^{*} used to construct P ^ \widehat{P} . By Chebyshev’s inequality, 
 ℙ ​ ( | n ​ 𝕌 n ​ f n | > δ | P ^ ) \displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|n\mathbb{U}_{n}f_{n}\right|>\delta\,|\,\widehat{P}\right) 
 ≤ 𝔼 ​ [ ( n ​ 𝕌 n ​ f n ) 2 | P ^ ] δ 2 \displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(n\mathbb{U}_{n}f_{n})^{2}\,|\,\widehat{P}\right]}{\delta^{2}} 
 = n 2 δ 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ ( ( n 2 ) − 1 ​ ∑ i < j f n ​ ( Z i , Z j ) ) 2 | P ^ ] \displaystyle=\frac{n^{2}}{\delta^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left({n\choose 2}^{-1}\sum_{i<j}f_{n}(Z_{i},Z_{j})\right)^{2}\Big|\,\widehat{P}\right] 
 = 2 ​ n δ 2 ​ ( n − 1 ) ​ 𝔼 ​ [ f n ​ ( Z 1 , Z 2 ) 2 | P ^ ] ≤ C δ 2 ​ ‖ f n ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ⊗ P ∗ ) 2 a.s. for some  ​ C > 0 . \displaystyle=\frac{2n}{\delta^{2}(n-1)}\mathbb{E}\left[f_{n}(Z_{1},Z_{2})^{2}\,|\,\widehat{P}\right]\leq\frac{C}{\delta^{2}}\left\|f_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}(P^{*}\otimes P^{*})}^{2}\quad\text{a.s. for some }C>0. 
 Taking expectation on both sides, 
 ℙ ​ ( | n ​ 𝕌 n ​ f n | > δ ) = 𝔼 ​ [ ℙ ​ ( | n ​ 𝕌 n ​ f n | > δ | P ^ ) ] \displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|n\mathbb{U}_{n}f_{n}\right|>\delta\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(\left|n\mathbb{U}_{n}f_{n}\right|>\delta\,|\,\widehat{P}\right)\right] 
 ≤ 𝔼 ​ [ min ⁡ ( 1 , C δ 2 ​ ‖ f n ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ⊗ P ∗ ) 2 ) ] . \displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\min\left(1,\frac{C}{\delta^{2}}\|f_{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P^{*}\otimes P^{*})}\right)\right]. 
 Since ‖ f n ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ⊗ P ∗ ) 2 → 𝑝 0 \|f_{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P^{*}\otimes P^{*})}\xrightarrow[]{p}0 , the integrand on the right converges to 0 in probability. Since it is also dominated by the constant function 1 1 , by the dominated convergence theorem, the right-hand side converges to 0 as n → ∞ n\to\infty .
∎ 
 Lemma E.7 (Second-order remainder for the Sinkhorn divergence) . 
 Consider paths μ t = μ + t ​ γ t 1 \mu_{t}=\mu+t\gamma_{t}^{1} and ν t = μ + t ​ γ t 2 \nu_{t}=\mu+t\gamma_{t}^{2} in 𝒫 μ \mathcal{P}_{\mu} such that γ t i → γ i \gamma_{t}^{i}\to\gamma^{i} in ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}) , for
 i = 1 , 2 i=1,2 , with γ 1 , γ 2 ∈ ℳ 0 , μ \gamma^{1},\gamma^{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{0,\mu} . Suppose that S ε : ( μ , ν ) ↦ S ε ​ ( μ , ν ) S_{\varepsilon}:(\mu,\nu)\mapsto S_{\varepsilon}(\mu,\nu) 
is twice differentiable along the path t ↦ ( μ + t ​ γ t 1 , μ + t ​ γ t 2 ) t\mapsto(\mu+t\gamma_{t}^{1},\mu+t\gamma_{t}^{2}) for t t small enough, and its second derivative satisfies the local

 Proof.

 Lemma E.8 (Rate for second-order remainder term) . 
 Let P ∗ ∈ ℋ 0 P^{*}\in\mathscr{H}_{0} and its initial estimator P ^ \widehat{P} satisfy ‖ n r ​ ( P ^ a − P a ∗ ) ‖ ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℋ 1 ) = 𝒪 p ​ ( 1 ) \|n^{r}(\widehat{P}_{a}-P_{a}^{*})\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(1) , a ∈ { 1 , 0 } a\in\{1,0\} , for some r > 1 / 3 r>1/3 .  ∎ 
 Lemma E.9 (Consistency under fixed alternative) . 
 Let P ∗ ∉ ℋ 0 P^{*}\notin\mathscr{H}_{0} and let ( λ j , j > 0 ) (\lambda_{j},j>0) are eigenvalues of the integral operator f ↦ ∫ 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ ​ ( ⋅ , z ) ​ f ​ ( z ) ​ 𝑑 P ∗ ​ ( y ) f\mapsto\int\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}(\cdot,z)f(z)dP^{*}(y) repeated according to their multiplicity. Suppose conditions of Sec.   E.1 hold, 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ ∈ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ⊗ P ∗ ) \ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}\in L^{2}(P^{*}\otimes P^{*}) , and further ‖ C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ − C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ⊗ P ∗ ) → 𝑝 0 \|C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}-C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}\|_{L^{2}(P^{*}\otimes P^{*})}\xrightarrow[]{p}0 .
Then, 
 P ∗ ​ ( n ​ 𝒮 ¯ > q 1 − α ) → 1 , as  ​ n → ∞ , P^{*}(n\overline{\mathscr{S}}>q_{1-\alpha})\to 1,\quad\text{as }n\to\infty, 
 where q 1 − α q_{1-\alpha} is the ( 1 − α ) (1-\alpha) th quantile of ∑ j = 1 n λ j ​ ( N j 2 − 1 ) \sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}(N_{j}^{2}-1) . 
 Proof. 
 For 𝒮 ¯ = 𝒮 ^ + 1 2 ​ 𝕌 n ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ \overline{\mathscr{S}}=\widehat{\mathscr{S}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{U}_{n}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}} , consider the breakdown 
 n ​ 𝒮 ¯ = n ​ 𝒮 ∗ + n ​ ( 𝒮 ^ − 𝒮 ∗ ) + n 2 ​ 𝕌 n ​ ( C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ − C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ ) + n 2 ​ 𝕌 n ​ ( 𝒮 ¨ P ^ − C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ ) + n 2 ​ 𝕌 n ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ . \sqrt{n}\,\overline{\mathscr{S}}=\sqrt{n}\,\mathscr{S}^{*}+\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\mathscr{S}}-\mathscr{S}^{*})+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2}\mathbb{U}_{n}(C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}-C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}})+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2}\mathbb{U}_{n}(\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}-C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}})+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2}\mathbb{U}_{n}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}. 
 Under the alternative ( P ∗ ∉ ℋ 0 P^{*}\notin\mathscr{H}_{0} ), we have that 𝒮 ∗ > 0 \mathscr{S}^{*}>0 and therefore, n ​ 𝒮 ∗ \sqrt{n}\,\mathscr{S}^{*} diverges to infinity. Under the sufficient conditions presented in Lem.   E.2 and Lem.   E.3 , the second additive term converges weakly to a mean-zero normal distribution ( 11 ). Since ‖ C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ − C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ ‖ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ⊗ P ∗ ) = o p ​ ( 1 ) \|C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}-C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}\|_{L^{2}(P^{*}\otimes P^{*})}=o_{p}(1) by assumption, the third term is o p ​ ( n − 1 / 2 ) o_{p}(n^{-1/2}) by Chebyshev’s inequality for U-statistics (see Lem.   E.6 ). The degeneracy of 𝒮 ¨ P ^ − C P ∗ ​ 𝒮 ¨ P ^ \ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}}-C_{P^{*}}\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{\widehat{P}} and 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ \ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}} , together with Chebyshev’s inequality for U-statistics, implies that the fourth and fifth terms are o p ​ ( 1 ) o_{p}(1) . Putting the four terms together, we have that n 1 / 2 ​ 𝒮 ¯ n^{1/2}\,\overline{\mathscr{S}} diverges to infinity in probability as n → ∞ n\to\infty , and so the test statistic n ​ 𝒮 ¯ n\overline{\mathscr{S}} does as well. 
 Because 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ ∈ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ⊗ P ∗ ) \ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}\in L^{2}(P^{*}\otimes P^{*}) , the Hilbert-Schmidt norm ∑ j = 1 ∞ λ j 2 < ∞ \sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}^{2}<\infty . Consequently, all quantiles of ∑ j = 1 ∞ λ j ​ ( N j 2 − 1 ) \sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}(N_{j}^{2}-1) are finite. Hence, P ​ ( n ​ 𝒮 ¯ > q 1 − α ) → 1 P(n\overline{\mathscr{S}}>q_{1-\alpha})\to 1 as n → ∞ n\to\infty for any α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) .
∎ 
 Appendix F One-Step Estimator of MMD Distribution Treatment Effect 
 As a benchmark, we also compute first- and second-order one-step estimators for the maximum mean discrepancy between counterfactual outcome distributions; we call this the MMD treatment effect (MTE). The corresponding EIFs are derived using the same framework as for the Sinkhorn divergence; however, smoothness with respect to the underlying kernel mean embeddings is immediate for MMD due to its quadratic form. 
 The (squared) MMD functional is defined as 
 ℳ : P ∈ 𝒫 ↦ 1 2 MMD 2 ( ψ 1 ( P ) ) , ψ 0 ( P ) ) = 1 2 ∥ ψ 1 ( P ) ) − ψ 0 ( P ) ) ∥ 2 𝒢 , \mathscr{M}:P\in\mathcal{P}\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\text{MMD}^{2}\left(\psi^{1}(P)),\,\psi^{0}(P)\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left\|\psi^{1}(P))-\psi^{0}(P))\right\|^{2}_{\mathcal{G}}, 
 (38) 
 where recall that 𝒢 \mathcal{G} is the Gaussian RKHS with kernel g ε ​ ( y 1 , y 2 ) = exp ⁡ ( − ‖ y 1 − y 2 ‖ 2 / 2 ​ ε ) g_{\varepsilon}(y_{1},y_{2})=\exp(-\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|^{2}/2\varepsilon) and KME operator 𝔤 ​ ( μ ) = ∫ g ε ​ ( ⋅ , y ) ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( y ) \mathfrak{g}(\mu)=\int g_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,y)d\mu(y) . 
 Lemma F.1 (MMD first-order local parameter, Ex. 3 in Luedtke and Chung ( 2024 ) ) . 
 The parameter ℳ : 𝒫 → ℝ \mathscr{M}:\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{R} is pathwise differentiable at P ∈ 𝒫 P\in\mathcal{P} with first-order EIF given by 
 ℳ ˙ P = ( I − ( I − ( I − P X ) ​ P A | X ) ​ P Y | A , X ) ​ f , \dot{\mathscr{M}}_{P}=(I-(I-(I-P_{X})P_{A\,|\,X})P_{Y\,|\,A,X})f, 
 where f ​ ( x , a , y ) = 2 ​ a − 1 e P ​ ( a | x ) ​ ( 𝔤 ​ ( P 1 ) ​ ( y ) − 𝔤 ​ ( P 0 ) ​ ( y ) ) f(x,a,y)=\frac{2a-1}{e_{P}(a\,|\,x)}\left(\mathfrak{g}(P_{1})(y)-\mathfrak{g}(P_{0})(y)\right) . 
 Proof. 
 This lemma follows by applying the automatic differentiation algorithm in Thm. 1 of Luedtke ( 2026 ) with the composition of two primitives from that work: the kernel mean embedding in Lem. S11 in Appx. C.2.5 and the squared (RKHS) norm in Appx. C.4.3.
∎ 
 Lemma F.2 (MMD second-order local parameter) . 
 The parameter ℳ : 𝒫 → ℝ \mathscr{M}:\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{R} is second-
order pathwise differentiable at all P ∈ ℋ 0 P\in\mathscr{H}_{0} with second-
order EIF, denoted by ℳ ¨ P ∈ L 2 ​ ( P ⊗ 2 ) \ddot{\mathscr{M}}_{P}\in L^{2}(P^{\otimes 2}) , and equal to 
 ℳ ¨ P = ( I − ( I − ( I − P X ) ​ P A | X ) ​ P Y | A , X ) ⊗ 2 ​ g , \ddot{\mathscr{M}}_{P}=(I-(I-(I-P_{X})P_{A\,|\,X})P_{Y\,|\,A,X})^{\otimes 2}g, 
 where g = ( ω P ⊗ ω P ) ⊙ g ε g=(\omega_{P}\otimes\omega_{P})\odot g_{\varepsilon} , and 
 ω P ​ ( a , x ) = ( a e P ​ ( 1 | x ) − 1 − a e P ​ ( 0 | x ) ) . \omega_{P}(a,x)=\left(\frac{a}{e_{P}(1\,|\,x)}-\frac{1-a}{e_{P}(0\,|\,x)}\right). 
 Proof. 
 Consider a submodel ( P t : t ∈ [ 0 , δ ) ] ) ∈ 𝔓 ( P , 𝒫 , s ) (P_{t}:t\in[0,\delta)])\in\mathfrak{P}(P,\mathcal{P},s) starting at P 0 = P P_{0}=P . Then, the second-order local parameter of ℳ \mathscr{M} can be derived as

 = ‖ D ​ ψ P 1 ​ ( s ) − D ​ ψ P 0 ​ ( s ) ‖ ℋ 2 + 0 . \displaystyle=\left\|D\psi^{1}_{P}(s)-D\psi^{0}_{P}(s)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+0.

Using the form of D ​ ψ P 1 D\psi^{1}_{P} and D ​ ψ P 0 D\psi^{0}_{P} from Lem.   D.2 , we have that d 2 d ​ t 2 ​ ℳ ​ ( P t ) | t = 0 \frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\mathscr{M}(P_{t})\Big|_{t=0} is equal to 
 ∫ ∫ \displaystyle\int\int

Since the above expression is bilinear in s s , it is equal to D 2 ​ ℳ P ​ ( s ) D^{2}\mathscr{M}_{P}(s) . The proof concludes by noting that (i) D 2 ​ ℳ P ​ ( s ) = ∫ ⟨ ℳ ¨ P ​ ( z , ⋅ ) , s ⟩ L 2 ​ ( P ) ​ s ​ ( z ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( z ) D^{2}\mathscr{M}_{P}(s)=\int\langle\ddot{\mathscr{M}}_{P}(z,\cdot),s\rangle_{L^{2}(P)}\,s(z)\,dP(z) and (ii) ℳ ¨ P \ddot{\mathscr{M}}_{P} is P ⊗ 2 P^{\otimes 2} -square integrable since the kernel is bounded and strong positivity holds.
∎ 
 Lem.   F.1 and Lem.   F.2 yield the first and second order EIF used to construct the one-step estimator of ℳ ​ ( P ∗ ) \mathscr{M}(P^{*}) and the test statistic for testing the null hypothesis.
The one-step estimator of ℳ ​ ( P ∗ ) \mathscr{M}(P^{*}) is 
 ℳ ^ = ℳ ​ ( P ^ ) + P n ​ ℳ ˙ P ^ . \widehat{\mathscr{M}}=\mathscr{M}(\widehat{P})+P_{n}\dot{\mathscr{M}}_{\widehat{P}}. 
 The corresponding test statistic is 
 ℳ ¯ = ℳ ​ ( P ^ ) + P n ​ ℳ ˙ P ^ + 𝕌 n 2 ​ ℳ ¨ P ^ , \overline{\mathscr{M}}=\mathscr{M}(\widehat{P})+P_{n}\dot{\mathscr{M}}_{\widehat{P}}+\frac{\mathbb{U}_{n}}{2}\ddot{\mathscr{M}}_{\widehat{P}}, 
 where 𝕌 n \mathbb{U}_{n} denotes the U-statistic operator constructed using the same sample split as P n P_{n} . 
 Finite sample implementation. 
 From a computational perspective, the first-order EIF of MMD is obtained by applying Alg.   1 with 𝐔 1 = 𝐆 ​ ( 𝐏 1 − 𝐏 0 ) \mathbf{U}^{1}=\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{P}^{1}-\mathbf{P}^{0}) and 𝐔 2 = − 𝐆 ​ ( 𝐏 1 − 𝐏 0 ) \mathbf{U}^{2}=-\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{P}^{1}-\mathbf{P}^{0}) . The second-order EIF is computed via Alg.   2 by omitting the intermediate operator application (Step 3) and replacing 𝐊 \mathbf{K} with 𝐆 \mathbf{G} in Step 4. 
 Appendix G Algorithms for Finite-Sample Implementation 
 Here we present the algorithms for debiasing the STE estimator using first-order EIF computations in Alg.   1 and second-order EIF computations in Alg.   2 . Refer to the precomputations in Sec.   5 . 
 Algorithm 1 First-order EIF evaluations

 Appendix H Max-Aggregated Test 
 Recall that 𝒯 n , ε = n ​ 𝒮 ¯ \mathcal{T}_{n,\varepsilon}=n\overline{\mathscr{S}} is the test statistic for a fixed ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 with explicit dependence on n n and ε \varepsilon . Under the null, from Thm.   4.1 , we have that 𝒯 n , ε = n ​ 𝕌 n ​ h ε / 2 + o p ​ ( 1 ) \mathcal{T}_{n,\varepsilon}=n{\mathbb{U}_{n}}h_{\varepsilon}/2+o_{p}(1) , where h ε h_{\varepsilon} is the symmetric, one-degenerate kernel h ε = 𝒮 ¨ P ∗ ∈ L 2 ​ ( P ∗ ⊗ P ∗ ) h_{\varepsilon}=\ddot{\mathscr{S}}_{P^{*}}\in L^{2}(P^{*}\otimes P^{*}) .  Proof.

 For a fixed 0 < β < 1 0<\beta<1 and for each r r , let F r F_{r} denote the distribution function of W ​ ( ε r ) W(\varepsilon_{r}) , and let q ε r := F r − 1 ​ ( 1 − β ) q_{\varepsilon_{r}}:=F_{r}^{-1}(1-\beta) .

 Proof Thm.   4.2 .

 Appendix I Experimental Details 
 For our experiments, we use a sample splitting approach in which an effective sample of size 2 ​ n 2n is divided evenly. The first n n observations are used to construct an initial estimator P ^ \widehat{P} of P ∗ P^{*} , and the remaining n n observations are used to form the empirical distribution P n P_{n} . 
 I.1 Datasets 
 I.1.1 Simulations. 
 The covariates X ∼ 𝒩 ​ ( 𝟎 3 , I 3 ) X\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}_{3},I_{3}) , and the treatment assignment model is logistic such that, 
 A | X = x ∼ Bernoulli ( expit ( 𝟏 3 ⊤ x ) ) ) . A\,|\,X=x\sim\text{Bernoulli}(\text{expit}(\mathbf{1}_{3}^{\top}x))). 
 Throughout Exp (i) and (ii), we maintain a fixed covariance matrix Σ \Sigma . For Exp (i) (mean difference), the outcome Y Y given ( A , X ) (A,X) is sampled as 
 Y | ( A = a , X = x ) ∼ { 𝒩 ​ ( 𝟎 2 , Σ ) if  ​ a = 0 , 𝒩 ​ ( θ ​ 𝟏 2 , Σ ) if  ​ a = 1 . , Y\,|\,(A=a,X=x)\sim\begin{cases}\mathcal{N}({\mathbf{0}_{2},\,\Sigma})&\quad\text{if }a=0,\\
\mathcal{N}({\theta\mathbf{1}_{2},\,\Sigma})&\quad\text{if }a=1.\end{cases}, 
 For Exp (ii) (covariance difference), the outcome Y Y given ( A , X ) (A,X) is sampled as 
 Y | ( A = a , X = x ) ∼ { 𝒩 ​ ( 𝟎 2 , Σ ) if  ​ a = 0 , 𝒩 ​ ( 𝟎 2 , Σ + θ ​ ( u ​ v ⊤ + v ​ u ⊤ ) ) if  ​ a = 1 , Y\,|\,(A=a,X=x)\sim\begin{cases}\mathcal{N}({\mathbf{0}_{2},\,\Sigma})&\quad\text{if }a=0,\\
\mathcal{N}({\mathbf{0}_{2},\,\Sigma+\theta(uv^{\top}+vu^{\top})})&\quad\text{if }a=1,\end{cases} 
 where u u and v v are the eigenvectors of Σ \Sigma .
Figure  3 illustrates the resulting 95 % 95\% ellipsoids for varying θ \theta . 
 Figure 3 : Mean and covariance ellipsoids ( 95 % 95\% ) of counterfactual outcome distributions under varying gap between P 0 P_{0} and P 1 P_{1} , parametrized by θ \theta . Exp (i): Mean difference experiment P 0 = ℕ ​ ( 𝟎 2 , Σ ) P_{0}=\mathbb{N}(\mathbf{0}_{2},\Sigma) and P 1 = ℕ ​ ( θ ​ 𝟏 2 , Σ ) P_{1}=\mathbb{N}(\theta\mathbf{1}_{2},\Sigma) . Exp (ii): Covariance difference experiment P 0 = ℕ ​ ( 𝟎 2 , Σ ) P_{0}=\mathbb{N}(\mathbf{0}_{2},\Sigma) and P 1 = ℕ ​ ( 𝟎 2 , Σ + θ ​ Δ ) P_{1}=\mathbb{N}(\mathbf{0}_{2},\Sigma+\theta\Delta) .
 Simulations for aggregated test. 
 We replicate the simulation setup of Exp (ii), but now evaluate the tests over a grid of ε \varepsilon values of the form ε = η ​ m \varepsilon=\eta m , where m m is the median heuristic and η ∈ { 0.25 , 0.5 , 1 , 2 , 4 } \eta\in\{0.25,0.5,1,2,4\} . For each fixed ε \varepsilon , we compute the corresponding MTE- and STE-based test statistics and p-values, and we additionally evaluate the aggregated procedures MTEAgg and STEAgg. The results are shown in Fig.   4 under both the null (Exp (ii) with θ = 0.0 \theta=0.0 ) and an alternative (Exp (ii) with θ = 0.8 \theta=0.8 ). Under the null, both MTE- and STE-based tests exhibit lower type-I error as ε \varepsilon increases. Under the alternative, for STE, power initially improves with ε \varepsilon , but eventually deteriorates when ε \varepsilon becomes too large, reflecting oversmoothing and a loss of sensitivity to distributional differences; in this regime, Sinkhorn divergence begins to behave more like MMD (Feydy et al. , 2019 ) . Interestingly, for STE power is also lower when ε \varepsilon is too small, which is consistent with the numerical instability of Sinkhorn iterations at low regularization. Overall, this experiment recovers the expected bias-variance tradeoff in the choice of ε \varepsilon for STE. Among the values considered, the strongest performance is attained near the median heuristic, which we therefore recommend as a practical default. 
 Figure 4 : Type-I error and power in Exp (ii) for the aggregated procedures MTEAgg and STEAgg, together with the corresponding MTE- and STE-based tests evaluated on a finite grid of kernel bandwidth parameters ε = η ​ m \varepsilon=\eta m ; m = m= median heuristic 
 I.1.2 PCam Dataset 
 We provide here the exact data-generating mechanism used for the image-outcome experiments. For each unit, covariates are generated as X ∼ 𝒩 ​ ( 0 , I 5 ) X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{5}) . Conditional on X = x X=x , a latent disease status S ∈ { 0 , 1 } S\in\{0,1\} is sampled from a Bernoulli distribution with success probability expit ​ ( π 0 + π 1 ⊤ ​ x ) \mathrm{expit}(\pi_{0}+\pi_{1}^{\top}x) , where S = 1 S=1 denotes metastatic tissue. Treatment assignment A ∈ { 0 , 1 } A\in\{0,1\} is generated independently given X = x X=x from a Bernoulli distribution with success probability expit ​ ( γ 0 + γ 1 ⊤ ​ x ) \mathrm{expit}(\gamma_{0}+\gamma_{1}^{\top}x) , inducing confounding through shared dependence on X X . 
 Outcome images Y Y are sampled from the empirical PCam image distributions conditional on ( S , A , X ) (S,A,X) . Specifically, if S = 0 S=0 , outcomes are drawn from the empirical distribution supported on 𝒟 0 \mathcal{D}_{0} . If S = 1 S=1 and A = 0 A=0 , outcomes are drawn from 𝒟 1 \mathcal{D}_{1} . If S = 1 S=1 and A = 1 A=1 , outcomes are drawn from 𝒟 0 \mathcal{D}_{0} with probability q ​ ( x ) = expit ​ ( β 0 + β 1 ⊤ ​ x ) q(x)=\mathrm{expit}(\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}^{\top}x) and from 𝒟 1 \mathcal{D}_{1} otherwise. This construction induces heterogeneous, covariate-dependent treatment effects on the counterfactual distributions while preserving overlap and positivity. 
 Figure 5 : 
Type 1 error (far left point) and power (all other points) of STE and MTE as a function of the treatment success probability for the PCam dataset.
 I.2 Compute details 
 The code was written in Python 3 and we use PyTorch for automatic differentiation. All our experiments were conducted on a CUDA-enabled machine with 12GB GPU memory, 64GB RAM, and 24 vCPUs. Although the experiments were run on a GPU, we observe similar complexity trends for CPU implementations. Fig.   6 plots the average wall-clock time (averaged over 20 Monte Carlo simulations) and memory usage across increasing sample sizes n n for both GPU and CPU implementations. 
 Figure 6 : Wall-clock runtime (in seconds) and memory usage (in MB) for the simulation setup across increasing sample sizes n n , averaged over 20 20 Monte Carlo simulations for both GPU and CPU implementations. 
 I.3 Acceleration recommendations 
 We now make the computational bottlenecks of the second-order one-step STE estimator explicit and summarize practical acceleration strategies. From Sec.   5 , the total computational complexity is O ​ ( n 2 ​ ( n + t ) ) O(n^{2}(n+t)) , where n n is the sample size and t t is the number of Sinkhorn iterations. This cost is driven primarily by the Sinkhorn algorithm, which contributes O ​ ( n 2 ​ t ) O(n^{2}t) , and Algorithm 2, which contributes O ​ ( n 3 ) O(n^{3}) . In addition, once the Gram matrix of the second-order EIF has been formed, the second-order bias-correction term requires a U-statistic computation, which incurs an additional quadratic computational load. We now discuss standard acceleration techniques that can be incorporated directly into our framework. While we expect these strategies to yield empirical gains similar to those reported in the existing literature, a full asymptotic analysis of the resulting accelerated procedures is beyond the scope of the present paper. 
 Sinkhorn algorithm. 
 To accelerate the Sinkhorn step, several complementary strategies are available.
Greenkhorn (Altschuler et al. , 2017 ) replaces full alternating row/column normalizations by greedy coordinate updates, which often improves practical performance while retaining the same dense-kernel representation. Batch Greenkhorn methods (Kostic et al. , 2022 ) extend this idea by updating batches of coordinates and provide refined convergence guarantees. These approaches aim to reduce the amount of scaling work required for convergence, but they do not by themselves eliminate the O ​ ( n 2 ) O(n^{2}) cost of storing and multiplying by a dense Gibbs kernel. To address this bottleneck, Nyström-Sinkhorn (Altschuler et al. , 2019 ) approximates the Gibbs kernel K i ​ j = e − C i ​ j / ε K_{ij}=e^{-C_{ij}/\varepsilon} using a low-rank Nyström factorization, reducing per-iteration kernel-vector multiplication costs and memory usage from O ​ ( n 2 ) O(n^{2}) to O ​ ( n ​ r ) O(nr) , where r r is the approximation rank. These approaches can be incorporated into our one-step STE estimator to reduce the per-iteration computational cost. 
 Algorithm 2 acceleration. 
 A natural way to reduce the O ​ ( n 3 ) O(n^{3}) cost of Alg.   2 is to replace the dense discretized self-transport kernel 𝐗 \mathbf{X} , defined in line 3 by 
 𝐗 i ​ j = exp ⁡ { 𝐅 i + 𝐅 j − 𝐆 i ​ j ε } , \mathbf{X}_{ij}=\exp\!\left\{\frac{\mathbf{F}_{i}+\mathbf{F}_{j}-\mathbf{G}_{ij}}{\varepsilon}\right\}, 
 with a rank- r r Nyström approximation 𝐗 ~ = C ​ W † ​ C ⊤ = U ​ V ⊤ \tilde{\mathbf{X}}=CW^{\dagger}C^{\top}=UV^{\top} with r ≪ n r\ll n . This is well aligned with prior work on scalable entropic OT, where low-rank Nyström approximations are used to compress the Gibbs kernel while preserving the Sinkhorn scaling structure (Altschuler et al. , 2019 ) . In our setting, the cubic bottleneck arises because line 5 requires solving a dense n × n n\times n linear system, 
 𝐌 = ( I − ( 𝐏 1 ⊙ 𝐗 ) 2 ) − 1 ​ ( 𝐗 − 𝟏𝟏 ⊤ / n ) , \mathbf{M}=\bigl(I-(\mathbf{P}^{1}\odot\mathbf{X})^{2}\bigr)^{-1}\left(\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}/n\right), 
 and because the subsequent contractions 𝐏𝐌 \mathbf{P}\mathbf{M} in lines 8-10 amount to repeated dense matrix multiplications, each of overall order n 3 n^{3} . Replacing 𝐗 \mathbf{X} by 𝐗 ~ \tilde{\mathbf{X}} lowers both costs: first, if A := 𝐏 1 ⊙ 𝐗 ~ A:=\mathbf{P}^{1}\odot\tilde{\mathbf{X}} is represented in low-rank form as A = U ~ ​ V ⊤ A=\tilde{U}V^{\top} , then 
 A 2 = ( U ~ ​ V ⊤ ) ​ ( U ~ ​ V ⊤ ) = U ~ ​ B ​ V ⊤ , B := V ⊤ ​ U ~ ∈ ℝ r × r , A^{2}=(\tilde{U}V^{\top})(\tilde{U}V^{\top})=\tilde{U}BV^{\top},\qquad B:=V^{\top}\tilde{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}, 
 so ( I − A 2 ) − 1 \bigl(I-A^{2}\bigr)^{-1} can be applied via the Woodbury identity using only an r × r r\times r inner solve, reducing line 5 from O ​ ( n 3 ) O(n^{3}) to O ​ ( n ​ r 2 + r 3 ) O(nr^{2}+r^{3}) after the low-rank factors are formed. Second, every multiplication by an n × n n\times n matrix generated from 𝐗 \mathbf{X} can be evaluated through the factorization U ​ V ⊤ UV^{\top} , costing O ​ ( n ​ r ) O(nr) per vector and O ​ ( n 2 ​ r ) O(n^{2}r) per matrix block, so the contractions 𝐏𝐌 \mathbf{P}\mathbf{M} drop from O ​ ( n 3 ) O(n^{3}) to O ​ ( n 2 ​ r ) O(n^{2}r) . The remaining operations in the algorithm: forming 𝐗 \mathbf{X} , applying 𝐃 A , X \mathbf{\mathbf{D}}_{A,X} , and elementwise products are at most quadratic, so under a Nyström representation the total complexity of the second-order routine is reduced from O ​ ( n 3 ) O(n^{3}) to roughly O ​ ( n 2 ​ r + n ​ r 2 + r 3 ) O(n^{2}r+nr^{2}+r^{3}) , with memory reduced from O ​ ( n 2 ) O(n^{2}) to O ​ ( n ​ r ) O(nr) . Therefore, a Nyström approximation to the discretized Sinkhorn self-transport kernel is a direct and computationally efficient way to scale the second-order correction while retaining the kernel structure induced by the entropic potentials. 
 U U -statistic computation. 
 The O ​ ( n 2 ) O(n^{2}) complexity of the U-statistic, standard in kernel two-sample testing, can be mitigated using linear-time approximations (Gretton et al. , 2012 ) or incomplete U-statistics (Schrab et al. , 2022 ) . Both can be directly substituted into the second-order bias-correction term of the STE one-step estimator.
